1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Product Introduction
Nirogacestat (OGSIVEO®) is an oral, small molecule γ‐secretase inhibitor. At the time of the New
Drug Application (NDA) submission on December 23, 2022, nirogacestat was not approved for
any indication in the United States or elsewhere. The Applicant’s proposed indication at the
time of the NDA submission was:
OGSIVEO is a gamma secretase inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adults with
desmoid tumors.
The proposed dosing regimen is 150 mg (3 × 50 mg tablets) administered orally twice daily
(BID).

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The recommendation for traditional approval of nirogacestat in patients with desmoid tumors
(DT) is based on the results of the NIR‐DT‐301 study, an international, multicenter, randomized,
double‐blind, placebo‐controlled study in 142 adult patients with progressing DTs which
demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvement in progression‐
free survival (PFS) and objective response rate (ORR) in patients treated with nirogacestat over
placebo including supportive patient‐reported outcome data for pain.
20
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Substantial evidence of effectiveness was established with one adequate and well controlled
investigation (i.e., Study NIR‐DT‐301), with highly persuasive results, that is considered to be
the scientific equivalent of two clinical investigations.
Patients (n = 142) in Study NIR‐DT‐301 were randomized 1:1 to receive nirogacestat or placebo
until disease progression, unacceptable toxicity, or withdrawal from the study for other
reasons. Cross‐over from placebo to nirogacestat was allowed at the time of radiographic
disease progression (patients remained blinded without allowing for cross over for clinical
progression events). Cross‐over was also permitted following the primary PFS analysis.
The primary efficacy endpoint was PFS determined by


Blinded Independent Central Imaging Review (BICR) using Response Evaluation Criteria
in Solid Tumors (RECIST) v1.1, or



The onset or worsening of symptoms resulting in a global deterioration of health status
causing the permanent discontinuation from study treatment and the initiation of
emergent treatment (e.g., radiotherapy, surgery, or systemic therapy including
chemotherapy or tyrosine kinase inhibitors) for DT/AF.

The key secondary endpoints were objective response rate by central review; and the following
patient‐reported outcomes (PROs) measures:


Brief Pain Inventory (BPI) short form,



GOunder/Desmoid Tumor Research Tumor Foundation (DTRF) DEsmoid
Symptom/Impact Scale (GODDESS),



Patient‐Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System Physical Function
(PROMIS PF) short form 10a plus 3 additional items from PROMIS item banks, and



European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer Quality of Life
Questionnaire‐Core 30 (EORTC) QLQ‐C30 (EORTC QLQ‐C30).

Additional secondary endpoints or objectives included duration of response, comparing change
in tumor volume measured by magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), and safety.
During the development of nirogacestat, FDA expressed concerns regarding the inclusion of
clinical progression as a PFS‐defining event (e.g., the potential for bias exists in classifying a
patient). Although bias may have been mitigated via the double‐blind design, toxicities related
to nirogacestat (e.g., GI toxicity, dermatological toxicities, ovarian suppression, or
hypophosphatemia) may have allowed patients or clinicians to determine treatment
assignment.

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The data submitted by the Applicant of an adequate and well‐controlled trial provided
substantial evidence for the effectiveness of nirogacestat administered at 150 mg BID versus
placebo for the treatment of adult patients with progressing DTs who require systemic
treatment and supports traditional the approval for the proposed indication. This conclusion is
based on results from the NIR‐DT‐301 study which demonstrated a clinically and statistically
significant improvement in PFS in patients who received nirogacestat compared to placebo with
a hazard ratio (HR) of 0.29 (95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.15, 0.55], p <0.001).
Concerns with respect to the inclusion of clinical progression events were mitigated via the
following factors:


Nirogacestat demonstrated improved PFS in a sensitivity analysis based on radiographic
assessment alone compared to placebo with a hazard ratio of 0.31 (95% CI: 0.16, 0.62).
Notably most events observed in the trial were radiographic progression and not clinical
progression.
Category of PFS event

Nirogacestat

Placebo

N=70

N=72

Radiographic progression

11 (16%)

30 (42%)

1 (1%)

6 (8%)

Death before progression

0

1 (1%)



The assessment of tumor shrinkage (i.e., response rate per RECIST) favored the
nirogacestat arm with an ORR of 41% (29.8, 53.8) for patients treated with nirogacestat
versus 8% (3.1, 17.3) in patients who received placebo (note, the 95% CIs are based on
FDA’s analysis which differ slightly from the Applicant’s).



A key component of the quality of life for patients with DT is improvement in pain.
Results from the BPI short form data were used as supportive evidence of the results of
PFS. FDA conducted analyses on patients with clinical responses at various thresholds,
and results were consistent in showing that more patients in the nirogacestat arm
experienced pain improvement. Although other PRO results were also generally
supportive of the nirogacestat treatment effect, missing PRO data precluded FDA from
determining that specific PRO treatment effect sizes were accurate.

In summary, although FDA had concerns regarding the inclusion of clinical progression events,
overall, the totality of the data were highly persuasive with respect to benefit following
nirogacestat treatment (in patients with DT who need systemic therapy).

DT is a rare, serious condition that has significant morbidity and mortality, and has a high
unmet medical need. A single randomized trial to support the approval of nirogacestat is
acceptable in the context of the unmet medical need in the indicated population and the
robustness of the efficacy results. Therefore, the review team recommends granting traditional
approval to nirogacestat for the treatment of adult patients with progressing desmoid tumors
who require systemic treatment.

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Benefit‐Risk Assessment (BRA)
Benefit‐Risk Summary and Assessment
Desmoid tumors (DTs) are a rare subtype of soft tissue sarcoma (STS) that are locally aggressive and characterized by infiltrative
growth which can affect surrounding vital organs causing significant impairment in the daily life of a patient due to chronic pain,
functional deficits, psychological problems and decrease in quality of life (Kasper 2020). DTs have also been known to
spontaneously regress. DTs have been reported to account for approximately 0.03% of all tumors (Reitamo 1982). They occur
between the age of 15 and 60 years and with a peak age of about 30 years (Kasper 2011) and are twice as common in females as
in males (Mishra). DTs are characterized by variable clinical behavior. Although most grow progressively larger over time, growth
is usually indolent, and periods of growth arrest/spontaneous regression are not uncommon (Bonvalot 2013). The largest patient
registry for DTs is the Desmoid Tumor Research Foundation Patient Registry and Natural History Study. In the first 15 months the
registry was launched the ethnicity of 329 patients in the registry with DT was 92% White, 1.6% Black or African American, 1.6%
Asian, 0.3% American Indian or Alaskan native, and 5% other. There have been two published DT studies that included the race
and/or ethnicity. A COG clinical trial of sulindac and tamoxifen recruited 80% non‐Hispanic (NH) or Latino, 14% Hispanic/Latino,
4% unknown ethnicity, 73% White, 16% Black or African American, 2.9% Asian, 1.4% Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander,
and 7.1% unknown race (Mercier 2019). On the molecular level, DT’s are characterized by mutations in the β‐catenin gene,
CTNNB1, or the adenomatous polyposis coli gene, APC (Kasper 2011). Most DTs arise sporadically, although between 5‐15% are
associated with familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP) caused by mutations in the APC gene. According to a retrospective review
published in the American Society of Clinical Oncology, 96% of patients with DTs are alive 5 years after diagnosis, 92% after 10
years, and 87% after 15 years. The survival rates for patients with desmoid tumor vary based on several factors. These include the
size of tumor, a patient’s age and general health, and how well the treatment plan works. Other factors that can affect outcomes
include where the tumor is located and how quickly the tumor grows. Intra‐abdominal desmoid tumors are the most likely to
cause potentially life‐threatening complications, such as intestinal obstruction. DTs in the head and neck region can also be life‐
threatening (Ballo 2016).
Diagnosis can be challenging as the clinical course can be unpredictable and typically requires a multidisciplinary team with
expertise in treating DTs. There is no treatment approved by the FDA for DT; however, treatment guidelines reference available
options, such as active surveillance, surgery, systemic therapy, and locoregional therapy. Choice of active treatment may depend
on tumor location, symptoms, and risk of morbidity (e.g., Bektas 2023 and NCCN guidelines 1.2023). Until recently, surgery had
24
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the
positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

been the mainstay of treatment; however, emerging data suggest a “wait and see” approach is reasonable for some patients. One
of the most compelling pieces of data supporting observation for patients with DT comes from the published report of the
placebo‐controlled trial of sorafenib, in which the placebo arm had a 20% response rate, suggesting the importance of
observation, especially in patients where symptoms are mild or absent (Gounder 2018). Surgery is offered to some patients who
have potentially resectable extra‐abdominal (extremity, trunk, breast) or abdominal wall DTs if they are medically able to tolerate
surgery and if resection can be accomplished without major functional or cosmetic deficit. For the patients treated with surgery,
recurrence rates have ranged from 19% to 77% with various factors affecting the rate of recurrence (Wang 2015). Primary
radiation therapy (RT) is an appropriate option for patients who need local treatment but are not good surgical candidates, those
who decline surgery, and those for whom surgical morbidity would be excessive. Systemic treatment is an alternative approach in
these patients, especially if they are young and there are concerns about the potential for late toxicity from RT including but not
limited to radiation‐associated malignancies. Although a number of systemic therapies have activity against DTs there is no
standard of care. There have been few prospective studies and most data from systemic treatment have come from case series or
retrospective analyses (Gounder 2018). Systemic treatments described in the literature have included NSAIDS, hormonal therapy,
conventional chemotherapy, and targeted therapy with tyrosine kinase inhibitors (e.g., Napolitano 2020 and NCCN v1.2023).
The data submitted by the Applicant to support the efficacy of nirogacestat in patients with DT come from the NIR‐DT‐301 study,
an international, multicenter, randomized, double‐blind, placebo‐controlled study in 142 adult patients with progressing DTs and
forms the primary basis on which the Applicant is seeking approval of nirogacestat in the proposed indication. The major efficacy
endpoint was progression‐free survival assessed by BICR via RECIST v1.1. Median PFS in patients who received nirogacestat
compared to placebo was non‐evaluable (NE, 95% CI: NE, NE) for patients on nirogacestat and 15.1 months (95% CI: 8.4, NE) for
patients on placebo with a hazard ratio (HR) of 0.29 (95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.15, 0.55], p <0.001). To support an overall
favorable risk:benefit based on the effect on PFS, FDA conducted analyses on patient reported outcomes for improvement in
pain, and results were consistent in showing that more patients in the nirogacestat arm experienced pain improvement over the
placebo arm. Although a number of issues precluded FDA’s ability to rely on the prespecified and tested PRO results, the overall
results and the additional analyses for pain demonstrated that nirogacestat had an effect on pain in those who had pain at
baseline.
Safety data supporting the proposed indication reflected exposure to nirogacestat in patients enrolled on the NIR‐DT‐301 study
(69 patients exposed to nirogacestat and 72 patients exposed to placebo), and additional supporting safety data from 17 patients
25
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the
positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

from Study 14‐C‐0007 (an NCI‐sponsored study), 9 patients from Study A8641014, and 19 patients from study A8641020 (refer to
the clinical results section of this review for details on these supportive studies). The primary analysis of safety was based on 141
patients enrolled in the NIR‐DT‐301 study who received either nirogacestat or placebo. An important identified risk of
nirogacestat is ovarian toxicity. Reports of primary ovarian toxicity (composite terms ovarian failure, premature menopause,
amenorrhea, and menopause) were noted prior to the implementation of Amendment 3 (January 27, 2020) of NIR‐DT‐301. In
Amendment 3, the Applicant included a requirement to ask women about their menstrual history and added monitoring of
hormone levels in men and women. For women of childbearing potential experiencing one of the composite terms for ovarian
toxicity, 75% experienced this adverse event (AE) versus 0% in the placebo arm. Although additional information was collected
following Amendment 3 regarding ovarian toxicity, this information was not sufficient to fully characterize ovarian toxicity,
resolution, and sequalae. In general, nirogacestat was well tolerated with most AEs reported as mild to moderate in severity.
Diarrhea and gastrointestinal toxicity were the most common AEs. Diarrhea occurred in 84% of the safety population receiving
nirogacestat versus 35% of patients receiving placebo. Diarrhea is a toxicity that oncologists are well‐trained to manage and are
acceptable for a population with a serious and life‐threatening condition
During the course of the review of the application, a major amendment was issued to the Applicant based in part on findings
during the Office of Scientific Investigation (OSI). FDA inspection revealed discrepancies in the Applicant’s reporting of sum of
target tumor measurements for 7 patients; and deficiencies in the financial disclosure, and conflict of interest provided by the
Applicant. Inspections of the clinical investigators revealed no discrepancies or regulatory violations. After a thorough review of
the updated data, FDA did not find any significant discrepancies that would alter the positive risk:benefit for the treatment of
patients with DT with nirogacestat.
The review team recommends granting traditional approval to nirogacestat for the treatment of adult patients with progressing
desmoid tumors who require systemic treatment.

Analysis of

Evidence and Uncertainties
 DTs are rare, locally aggressive and invasive tumors, but do
not metastasize.
 DTs are diagnosed in approximately 900‐1500 patients in the
US annually.
 Although extremity desmoids can also occur, most patients
develop abdominal tumors, one‐half of which are intra‐
abdominal, the remainder involving the abdominal wall
leading to symptoms of pain and impaired mobility that can
negatively impact their quality of life.
 The 5‐year survival of patients is dependent upon site of
tumor, size, proximity to vital structures. For all patients with
DT, nearly 96% of patients with DTs are alive 5 years after
diagnosis, 92% after 10 years, and 87% after 15 years.
 The are no FDA therapies approved for DT
 Published treatment guidelines describe use of surgery,
radiation, cytotoxic chemotherapy, or other targeted
therapies.
 Radiation may be an appropriate treatment but is reserved
for patients who cannot tolerate systemic therapy and
where surgery would be too morbid. Many factors play into
the role of the use of surgery.

Conclusions and Reasons
DT is a rare soft tissue sarcoma that is
locally aggressive, causing significant
morbidity and (less commonly) mortality
and represents a population with a high
unmet medical need that include early
and accurate diagnosis and approved
treatments.

There is an unmet medical need for new
effective systemic treatments for patients
with DTs. There are no FDA‐approved
therapies. Due to the nature of desmoid
tumors, complete surgical resection is
often challenging and the risk of
recurrence is variable and has been
reported to be as high as 70%. Radiation
therapy in combination with surgery may
reduce the recurrence rate but given the
high‐rate of abdominal tumors, side
effects may be a deterrent. For systemic
therapies, there has been no consensus
on standard of care.

Dimension

Benefit

Evidence and Uncertainties
 The mPFS in patients who received nirogacestat compared to
placebo was non‐evaluable (NE, 95% CI: NE, NE) and 15.1
months (95%CI: 8.4, NE), respectively. The HR was 0.29 (5%
CI: 0.15, 0.55). The results were both clinically and
statistically significant.
 PRO data was collected during the conduct of the study to
assess PRO measures from baseline over time. Due to
missing data, FDA was not able to confirm the accuracy of
PRO results submitted by the applicant, and therefore, the
results were considered exploratory. A significant effect on
patients is pain. Pain can be debilitating, require narcotic
medication to properly control, and affect one’s ability to
perform daily functions of life. FDA performed additional
analysis on the BPI short form and noted that nirogacestat
had an effect on pain in those who had pain at baseline
versus those who received placebo.

 Toxicity profile includes diarrhea and gastrointestinal
toxicities, dermatological toxicities, and wasting of
electrolytes (phosphorus and potassium). Toxicities were
Risk and Risk
generally manageable with supportive care or dose
Management
interruption; dose modifications, and discontinuations.
 Treatment with nirogacestat confers a risk of ovarian toxicity.

Conclusions and Reasons
For progressing DTs who require systemic
therapy there is no consensus on
standard of care treatment for these
patients in which the tumor may have
debilitating effects on day to day living,
significantly affecting their quality of life.
Improvements demonstrated in PFS and
ORR were supported by favorable findings
from patient‐reported pain. Reducing a
patient’s pain is an important component
in improving a patient’s quality of life.
The results of the study meet the
evidentiary standards for granting
traditional approval in the proposed
patient population.
A PMR will be issued to capture
appropriate data on women of child
bearing potential to characterize ovarian
toxicity in this patient population with
potential labeling updates in the future
for nirogacestat for ovarian toxicity.

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

2 THERAPEUTIC CONTEXT
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Desmoid tumors (DT), also referred to as aggressive fibromatosis (AF), are rare, locally invasive,
slow growing soft tissue tumors that can cause severe pain and functional impairment by
infiltrating or exerting mass effects on vital structures.
DT arise in the deep soft tissue and are characterized by infiltrative growth and a tendency
toward local recurrence (Kasper et al. 2011). DT cause significant morbidity by infiltrating or
exerting mass effects on vital structures, which can lead to severe pain, functional impairment,
and rarely, life‐threatening complications (Lewis et al. 1999; Penel et al. 2017;
Smith & Kransdorf 2000). The most common primary tumor sites include abdominal walls,
limbs, girdles, and mesenteric areas. Despite an inability to metastasize, they can behave
aggressively, causing significant morbidity, with elevated rates of local recurrence following
surgery (as high as 70%) despite wide excisions (Penel et al. 2017). Mortality is occasionally
observed owing to the local aggressive nature and vital structure involvement of some DT
(Smith & Kransdorf 2000).
DT most commonly occur in individuals between the ages of 15 to 60 years, with a peak age of
about 30 years, and a 2‐ to 3‐fold predominance in females (de Camargo et al. 2010;
Skubitz 2017; Anneberg et al. 2022). The global incidence of DT is about 3 to 5 cases per million
per year in the general population with approximately 1,000 to 1,650 cases diagnosed in the
United States per year (van Broekhoven et al. 2015; Prevalence of Rare Diseases: Bibliographic
Data, 2020).
The incidence of DT is reported to be about 800‐ to 1000‐fold higher in patients with familial
adenomatous polyposis (FAP [Gardner Syndrome]), in which the adenomatous polyposis coli
(APC) tumor suppressor gene is mutated (Skubitz 2017). FAP‐associated DT is more frequently
associated with abdominal tumors, especially in the Gardner variant of FAP, which is associated
with intestinal polyposis, osteomas, fibromas, and epidermal inclusion cysts (Skubitz 2017).
Intra‐abdominal DT are one of the leading causes of death in patients with FAP
(Quintini et al 2012). Although common in patients with FAP, most cases of DT occur
spontaneously in adults and are associated with a mutation in β‐catenin (CTNNB1)
(Bo et al. 2012; Lazar et al. 2008; Tejpar et al. 1999). β‐catenin is an integral component of the
Wnt/β‐catenin/T‐cell transcription factor signaling pathway, which is frequently dysregulated in
31
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

cancer. Patients with DT carrying β‐catenin mutations have a lower 5‐year recurrence‐free
survival rate than patients with wild‐type tumors (Kummar et al. 2017).
The clinical course of DT may be unusual and heterogeneous, characterized not only by tumor
growth, proliferation, and disease progression, but also by stabilization and spontaneous
remission (Desmoid Tumor Working Group [DTWG] 2020; Kasper et al. 2011). Compression of
vital structures by DT can result in severe pain, functional impairment, nerve damage, and
bowel obstruction or perforation (Constantinidou et al. 2012; Gounder et al. 2020;
Hosalkar et al. 2006; Penel et al. 2017; Quintini et al. 2012; Shinagare et al. 2011; Skubitz 2017).
Additionally, DT‐specific morbidities can negatively impact school, work, and psychosocial
functioning (Hosalkar et al. 2006; Schut et al. 2022).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of DT. It is a rare tumor with an incidence of 2‐4
cases per million per year in the United States (U.S.) and has been reported to comprise
approximately 0.03% of all cancers (or tumors), and <3% of all soft tissue sarcomas (Hosalkar et
al., 2006). Ethnicity of 329 patients from the Desmoid Tumor Research Foundation Patient
Registry noted the majority of patients with DT in the US to be 92% White, 1.6% Black or
African American, 1.6% Asian, 0.3% American Indian or Alaskan native, and 5% other. DTs may
occur during and after pregnancy, and regress during menopause, so hormones may play a role
in the pathophysiology of DTs, and most DTs express low levels of nuclear estrogen receptor‐β
(Janinis et al., 2003). As stated by the Applicant, most DTs occur spontaneously, and
approximately 85% are associated with a mutation in the CTNNB1 gene (most commonly in
exon 3) (Skubitz et al., 2017). Approximately 10% of DTs are associated with an APC gene
mutation, which is a tumor suppressor gene involved in β‐catenin degradation. Five to 15% of
DTs are associated with FAP (de Camargo et al., 2010; Skubitz et al., 2017). Somatic mutations
in CTNNB1 and germline mutations in APC genes are probably mutually exclusive in DT (Penel et
al., 2017). Although DT is rarely fatal, intra‐abdominal tumors which account for 5% of
spontaneous DTs are one of the leading causes of mortality. DTs associated with FAPs account
for a small proportion of DTs, 80% are intra‐abdominal, particularly in the Gardner variant of
FAP (Quinitini et al., 2012).

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
There is no Food and Drug Administration (FDA)‐approved therapeutic option for the treatment
of DT, nor is there a universal standard of care for patients with advanced progressing tumors.
Treatment options vary for each patient depending on the size, location, and morbidity
associated with the tumor.
Active surveillance is currently recommended as the first approach in DT (DTWG 2020). This
approach includes continuous monitoring via magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) (or computed
tomography [CT] if MRI is not feasible) within 1 to 2 months of diagnosis then at 3‐ to 6‐month
32
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

intervals. A decision regarding active treatment should be postponed until the occurrence of
subsequent progression or increase of symptomatic burden, assessed with ≥ 2 further
assessments and possibly not before 1 year from diagnosis in the absence of fulfilling Response
Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) progressive disease (PD; DTWG 2020).
Historically, surgery was the therapeutic option of choice for localized, extra‐abdominal, small
volume DT; however, surgery is no longer regarded as the cornerstone of DT treatment given
the associated morbidity and high rate of relapse after surgery (> 70%) (Penel et al. 2017).
Radiotherapy has been used both in the adjuvant setting after surgery and in the primary
setting, mainly for extra‐abdominal tumors. Radiotherapy after surgery is an independent
positive prognostic factor for local recurrence and overall survival, and radiotherapy alone or in
combination with surgery led to significantly lower recurrence rates (Kasper et al. 2011).
Various systemic therapies have been studied in patients with relapsed or recurrent DT,
patients with DT that are not amenable to surgery or radiotherapy, or patients for whom
surgery is potentially mutilating.
Traditional chemotherapy may be considered in patients requiring systemic therapy for DT.
Kasper et al. provided an overview of chemotherapy regimens that have been studied in
patients with advanced disease (Kasper et al. 2011). A combination of methotrexate and
vinblastine was one of the first chemotherapy regimens widely used for DT, with response rates
ranging from 30% to 50%. Low‐dose vinblastine‐methotrexate combination has demonstrated
disease stabilization and long‐term tolerance in pediatric patients with progressive DT
(Azzarelli et al. 2001; Fortunati et al. 2020; Penel et al. 2020; Skapek et al. 2007). Because this
regimen does have considerable toxicity, vinorelbine has largely replaced vinblastine in this
regimen. However, due to the toxicity of a prolonged course using either combination, this
regimen is not well tolerated by adults long‐term (Skubitz, 2017). It should be noted that these
studies were small and uncontrolled, and the most effective chemotherapeutic regimen has not
been established.
As a standard‐dose chemotherapy, anthracycline‐based chemotherapy (doxorubicin plus
dacarbazine) may be used in cases requiring rapid tumor response, e.g., intra‐abdominal and
head and neck tumors. The response rate is usually around 50% (Garbay et al. 2012), but it
usually occurs as late as several months after the start of therapy. Shrinkage can continue even
after treatment withdrawal and long‐term disease control is achieved in up to 50% to 70% of
the patients (DWTG, 2020).
Tyrosine kinase inhibitors (TKIs) have been investigated and demonstrated some benefit in
studies of adults with DT. Meaningful responses have been observed with TKIs, such as
imatinib, sorafenib, and pazopanib (DTWG 2020; Gounder et al. 2018; Kasper et al. 2017;
Toulmonde et al. 2019), although sorafenib is the only agent to have been studied in a
33
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

randomized, double‐blind (DB), placebo‐controlled clinical trial in patients with DT and despite
a positive Phase 3 study (hazard ration [HR] for progression or death = 0.13; 95% CI, 0.05 to
0.31; p < 0.001) in 87 patients with progressive, symptomatic, or recurrent DT, it is not FDA‐
approved for this indication (Gounder et al. 2018).
A benefit in both tumor response and patient‐reported outcomes (PROs) would be beneficial
and clinically meaningful to patients with DT particularly given the significant unmet need as
there are currently no FDA approved therapies for DT (DTWG 2020).
The Applicant’s Position:
DT are rare, locally‐aggressive, and invasive soft‐tissue tumors that can cause substantial
morbidity including severe pain, functional impairment, and disfigurement affecting everyday
activities. There is currently no accepted standard of care and no FDA approved therapies for
the treatment of DT (DTWG, 2020; National Comprehensive Cancer Network, 2020).
Nirogacestat has the potential to address a significant unmet medical need for this indication.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the current treatments offered in clinical
practice for DTs. A multidisciplinary approach to diagnosis and treatment is warranted given the
rarity and heterogenous presentation of patients with DT.
Due to the high recurrence rate after surgery as previously described by the Applicant, the
DTWG 2020 consensus guidelines recommend initial active surveillance for patients who do not
need immediate treatment (e.g., vital organ involvement, and intolerable symptoms). Data
from retrospective studies are conflicting regarding whether adding post‐operative
radiotherapy decreases the recurrence rate, and because surgical management is less frequent
in current practice, the combination of surgery and radiation is rarely done (DTWG 2020, NCCN
2.2023). Furthermore, a retrospective study in 142 patients with treatment‐naïve (n=74) or
previously treated (n=68) DT reported that 5‐year progression‐free survival was similar
between patients who were underwent observation (47%) to patients who were treated with
systemic therapy (54%, p=0.07); both patients with progressing and stable DTs were included in
this retrospective study (Fiore et al., 2009). NCCN consensus guidelines state that surgery is the
primary treatment for patients with resectable DTs, but that observation is an option for
selected patients with resectable tumors. NCCN concludes that patients with DT “can be
managed appropriately with a careful ‘wait and see’ approach if their tumors are asymptomatic
and are not located in an area that could lead to functional limitations if the tumor increases in
size” (NCCN 2.2023).
For patients with DT who do need to initiate treatment, management is heterogenous, but a
more conservative approach with systemic therapy is preferred in some patients over local
therapy, such as surgery or radiotherapy (DTWG 2020).

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s presentation of the U.S. FDA regulatory actions for
nirogacestat. An initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) was agreed upon between FDA and
SpringWorks on April 25, 2022, for a partial waiver of PREA requirements for patients 0‐<2
years of age and a deferral in older pediatric patients awaiting the results of Children’s
Oncology Group (COG) Study ARST1921, which will be submitted post‐approval. ARST1921 is an
ongoing Phase 2 study evaluating the safety, pharmacokinetics, and efficacy of nirogacestat in
pediatric patients >1 to <18 years‐old with progressive, surgically unresectable DT. Study
ARST1921 was activated in September 2020 and was expected to take approximately 3 years to
accrue; per clinicaltrials.gov, updated on 11/22/2023, this study is active but no longer accruing
patients. Patients are being followed for 2 years to evaluate outcomes including safety.





COG study requirements for non‐resectable tumors, progressing tumors, and previous
systemic treatment;



Observed risk of widening of growth plates, and theoretical risk on organ maturation
(however, this risk would be solely based on non‐clinical embryonic studies of the
NOTCH pathway and not on data observed in patients); and



Age distribution of 59 patients (over 63 months) in prior COG trial ARST0321 (Skapek SX,
et al, 2013) with 78% of 59 patients being 10 years of age or older (additionally
ARST0321 did not require previous systemic treatment).

Given the above factors, enrollment of a sufficient number of patients younger than 24 months
of age would be highly impracticable for this rare disease.

Based on the severity of the embryo‐fetal development (EFD) effects of nirogacestat observed
in the dose‐range finding study in rats (Study 01214026) and the importance of the gamma‐
secretase pathway in embryo implantation and development, SpringWorks requested a waiver
for conducting the definitive EFD study in rat, the range finding and definitive EFD studies in
rabbits, as well as the pre‐ and post‐natal development (PPND) study in rats.
A definitive EFD study in rat, the range finding and definitive EFD studies in rabbits, and the
PPND study in rats do not appear warranted to support an NDA.
SpringWorks should submit an integrated summary with the NDA using a weight‐of‐evidence
(WOE) approach for reproductive risk assessment. The WOE risk assessment may not rely on
product‐specific literature for which SpringWorks does not have right‐to‐reference. A final
determination of the acceptability of the nonclinical package will be a review issue.
In the 6‐month carcinogenicity study (Study 20174162), a significant increase in
hemangiosarcomas was noted in both male and female transgenic mice with the incidence
above historical control values of the test facility. Therefore, SpringWorks requested a waiver
for the 2‐year carcinogenicity study in rats and requested the Agency’s confirmation that this
study is unnecessary to support the NDA.

Clinical Microbiology
The CMC review indicated that "production scale batches of nirogacestat hydrobromide drug
substance were tested for TAMC, TYMC, and absence of E. Coli. The highest TAMC level
observed was 20 CFU/g and no TYMC levels have been observed above 10 CFU/g. Batch history
appears to indicate a low risk of microbial contamination or viability for the drug substance.
This and the fact that the DS is not hygroscopic justifies no controls in the release specification
for microbial limits.”
CMC also found that the microbial limit testing for DP at release and on stability to be
acceptable. The microbiology laboratory at SGS France had been covered in a previous GMP
inspection. Overall the Application was acceptable from a microbiological (manufacturing
perspective).

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

5 NONCLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY/TOXICOLOGY
Executive Summary
The established pharmacologic class of nirogacestat is gamma secretase inhibitor. By inhibiting
the activity of gamma secretase, nirogacestat blocks the proteolytic activation of Notch, which
has a role in cellular proliferation, differentiation, survival, and oncogenesis.
Nirogacestat inhibited the activity of gamma secretase in vitro with an IC50 of 6.2 nM. In a
cellular assay using human embryonic kidney (HEK) 293 cells that express a constitutively active
NΔE mutant of Notch, nirogacestat inhibited the activity of Notch intracellular domain (NICD)
with an IC50 of 7.7 nM. In human peripheral blood‐acute lymphoid leukemia (HPB‐ALL) cells
that harbor Notch mutations, nirogacestat inhibited the activity of NICD with an IC50 of 41 nM
and induced growth inhibition with an IC50 of 32 nM. Nirogacestat also demonstrated growth
inhibition in various Notch expressing patient‐derived desmoid tumor cell lines in vitro with
IC50s of 4‐160 µM. Nirogacestat exhibited anti‐tumor activity in numerous Notch‐driven tumor
xenograft models in mice but did not induce tumor growth inhibition in a non‐Notch‐driven
tumor xenograft model.
SpringWorks conducted stand‐alone GLP‐compliant safety pharmacology studies in rats and
dogs to assess the effect of nirogacestat on the central nervous system, pulmonary function,
and the cardiovascular system. There were no nirogacestat‐related adverse effects on the
central nervous system, pulmonary function, or the cardiovascular system at single doses up to
500 mg/kg. Nirogacestat blocked the hERG channel with an IC50 of 1 μM; however, this IC50 is
greater than 100 times the free Cmax in humans at the 150 mg BID dose. Consistent with these
findings, nirogacestat did not have effects on QT prolongation clinically or in in vivo nonclinical
studies.
The toxicity of nirogacestat was assessed in GLP‐compliant 3‐month repeated‐dose toxicity
studies in rats and dogs. Shorter duration repeated‐dose toxicity studies were also conducted
with nirogacestat and the findings were generally consistent with 3‐month repeated‐dose
toxicity studies. Rats received up to 25 mg/kg (50 mg/kg/day) oral nirogacestat, twice per day,
for 3 months, resulting in mean exposure levels (AUC) up to 3.4 times the AUC of 6740 ng.h/mL
in patients with desmoid tumors at the 150 mg BID dose. Dogs received up to 5 mg/kg (10
mg/kg/day) oral nirogacestat, twice per day, for 3 months, resulting in mean exposure levels
(AUC) up to 0.46 times the AUC of 6740 ng.h/mL in patients with desmoid tumors at the 150 mg
BID dose. Toxicity was generally consistent across species and consisted of intestinal
47
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

hyperplasia, fibrinoid necrosis of blood vessels, lymphoid depletion, nephropathy, liver toxicity,
and reproductive organ toxicity. Additional toxicities that only occurred in rats consisted of
thickening of the hypertrophic cartilage zone, fibrosis/necrosis in the heart, and epidermal
cysts. Male reproductive organ toxicity consisted of seminiferous tubule abnormalities, Sertoli
cell degeneration, and epididymal oligospermia. Female reproductive organ toxicity consisted
of ovarian degeneration/necrosis/atrophy, oocyte mineralization, and mammary acinar
hyperplasia.
In humans, nirogacestat can cause diarrhea, stomatitis, and ovarian dysfunction at the
recommended 150 mg BID dose. Loose stool and intestinal hyperplasia occurred in rats and
dogs administered nirogacestat. Intestinal hyperplasia was associated with epithelial
inflammation and necrosis in dogs. Consistent with these adverse intestinal findings, Notch has
been reported to regulate intestinal cell differentiation and inhibition of Notch drives goblet cell
proliferation (Fre, et al., 2005; Milano, et al., 2004; Van Es, et al., 2005; Wong, et al., 2004).
Notch also reportedly regulates gastric cell differentiation (Demitrack and Samuelson, 2016)
which may contribute to stomatitis in humans. Regarding ovarian dysfunction in humans,
ovarian degeneration/necrosis/atrophy and oocyte mineralization occurred in animals
administered nirogacestat and was consistent with Notch regulation of mammalian
folliculogenesis (Vanorny and Mayo, 2014).
SpringWorks evaluated the effect of nirogacestat on male and female fertility in a dedicated
GLP‐compliant study. Male rats received up to 40 mg/kg/day of oral nirogacestat for 65 days
including 28 days prior to mating with untreated female rats. Female rats received up to 40
mg/kg/day of oral nirogacestat for 35 days including 14 days prior to mating with untreated
male rats and through Gestation Day 7. Nirogacestat resulted in reduced fertility when
administered to male rats at ≥ 5 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.16 times the 150 mg BID dose
based on body surface area (BSA)) or female rats at ≥20 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.65 times
the 150 mg BID dose based on BSA). Nirogacestat resulted in a lack of fertility when
administered to male or female rats at ≥40 mg/kg/day (approximately 1.3 times the 150 mg BID
dose based on BSA). In pregnant female rats, nirogacestat increased pre‐implantation loss at
≥20 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.65 times the 150 mg BID dose based on BSA).
To address the potential for nirogacestat‐mediated reproductive activity, SpringWorks
conducted a preliminary embryo‐fetal development study with nirogacestat in rats. Daily oral
administration of nirogacestat to pregnant rats resulted in decreased fetal body weights, post‐
implantation loss, and fetal subcutis edema at doses ≥ 20 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.85 times
the exposure in patients with desmoid tumors based on AUC at the 150 mg BID dose). Based on
48
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

these adverse findings, the team recommends a warning for embryo‐fetal toxicity in Section 5
of the labeling. Because of the potential for embryo‐fetal toxicity, a recommendation for
females and males of reproductive potential to use contraception during treatment with
nirogacestat and, based on a half‐life of 25 hours, for 1 week after the last dose is included in
Section 8.3 of the labeling. Lactating women are advised not to breastfeed during treatment
with nirogacestat and, based on a half‐life of 25 hours, for 1 week after the last dose.
Nirogacestat was not considered genotoxic since it was not mutagenic in a bacterial reverse
mutation (Ames) assay and was not clastogenic in an in vitro human chromosome aberration
assay or in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assay.
To support continuous daily oral dosing of nirogacestat, SpringWorks conducted a 26‐week oral
carcinogenicity study with nirogacestat in transgenic rasH2 mice. Mice received up to 100
mg/kg/day of oral nirogacestat, resulting in mean exposure levels (AUC) less than those in
humans at the 150 mg BID dose. The results showed an increased incidence of whole body
hemangiosarcomas in 100 mg/kg/day males that was above the historical control value for the
test facility but did not reach statistical significance for trend and pairwise comparisons. The
study was negative for drug‐related neoplasms in female mice. Overall, the result of the
transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study was concluded to be negative.
Although activation of Notch signaling is associated with various cancers, literature reports that
Notch signaling may paradoxically suppress tumorigenesis in certain tumor types including
squamous cell carcinoma (SCC) and neuroendocrine tumors (Zhou, et al., 2022). Inhibition of
Notch signaling has been associated with an increased incidence of skin cancers in humans
(Coric, et al., 2012; Doody, et al., 2013; Penninkilampi, et al., 2016), and administration of
nirogacestat to patients with desmoid tumors resulted in cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma
and/or basal cell carcinoma in five patients, with no malignancies occurring in the placebo arm.
Based on these adverse clinical findings, the clinical team recommends a warning and
precaution for cutaneous malignancies in the nirogacestat label. No additional nonclinical
studies to assess the tumorigenic potential of nirogacestat in animals are warranted based on
the inclusion of the malignancy warning in the label.
Nirogacestat is not expected to demonstrate phototoxicity given that it does not absorb light in
the ultraviolet A and B (UVA‐UVB)/visible range from 290 to 700 nm.
There are no approvability issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective.

for nirogacestat.

Pharmacology
Primary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
Nonclinical pharmacology characterized the effects of nirogacestat in cell‐free assays, cell
culture experiments, and animal models of human disease. Early nonclinical and clinical
investigations were conducted with nirogacestat to evaluate potential therapeutic use in the
treatment of Alzheimer’s disease due to the involvement of gamma‐secretase (GS) in the
(b) (4)
cleavage of the amyloid β peptide (Aβ);
(b) (4)
in Study A8641002
Therefore, nirogacestat was evaluated in a variety of Notch‐driven tumor models
including T‐cell acute lymphoblastic leukemia (T‐ALL), breast cancer, colorectal cancer (CRC),
pancreatic cancer, liver cancer, and prostate cancer. Results of early studies in participants with
solid tumors demonstrated evidence of therapeutic activity in participants with DT, which led to
the selection of this indication for further clinical development.
In Vitro Pharmacology
In vitro evaluation of enzyme kinetics was completed with cell‐free assays using the human
isoform of GS extracted from HeLa cells and a recombinant human APP‐C100‐FLAG peptide
purified in E. coli (PF‐03084014‐Pharm‐001). Cellular assays were also conducted using the
human embryonic kidney (HEK) 293 stable cell line that expresses NΔE construct (containing
aa1727‐2813 of NOTCH1). Nirogacestat displayed potent activity with IC50 of 7.7 nM (0.34 nM
free) for NICD inhibition (PF‐03084014‐Pharm‐001). In human peripheral blood‐acute lymphoid
leukemia (HPB‐ALL) cells that harbor NOTCH1 mutations, the effects of nirogacestat on growth
and NICD inhibition were assessed with the key biochemical and cellular properties provided in
Table 2. For comparison to observed human serum values at steady state after a 150 mg BID
dosing regimen, free mean maximum observed concentration (Cmax) and minimum observed
concentration (Cmin) are estimated at 10.2 nM and 2.2 nM, respectively.

Nirogacestat and Notch Signaling in Desmoid Tumors
Nirogacestat was evaluated for antitumor effects in 7 patient‐derived DT cell lines
(Shang et al. 2015). NOTCH1, JAG1, and HES1 were shown to be present in all patient lines, with
high levels of nuclear localization of NICD confirming active Notch signaling. Dose‐dependent
decreases in NICD and HES1 were confirmed by Western blot in 2 of the cell lines and a varying
degree of growth inhibition was determined using cell proliferation assays in 5 of the cell lines
at concentrations ranging from 0.01 to 10 μM over a 27‐day treatment period. For these
patient‐derived cells, the IC50 for growth inhibition ranged from 4.19 μM in the most sensitive
line to 158.8 μM in the most resistant line. Cell migration and invasion assays were conducted
with 5 of the patient‐derived cell lines and all showed significant reductions in migration and
invasion after 7 days treatment with 10 μM nirogacestat. The IC50 values for growth inhibition

and the migration reduction observed at 10 μM are higher than the total and free maximum
serum concentrations observed at steady state in Study A8641014 (geometric mean: 2.54 μM
and 10.2 nM, respectively). However, 4 of the 5 evaluated cell lines showed statistically
significant growth inhibition at 1 μM, with 3 of the 5 showing statistically significant growth
inhibition at 0.01 μM. Therefore, while the migration experiments may not conclusively support
expected effects of nirogacestat at therapeutically relevant exposures, the effects on
Notch‐target expression and growth inhibition do support a role for GSI‐mediated inhibition of
Notch signaling as a potential mechanism for nirogacestat in the treatment of DT.

Antitumor Efficacy and Association with NICD Inhibition After Oral Administration
A series of experiments were performed with nirogacestat in human tumor xenograft models in
athymic nu/nu mice to evaluate its ability to reduce NICD levels in tumors and establish a
PK/PDx relationship for NICD reduction to plasma concentration of nirogacestat. In addition,
the ability of nirogacestat to inhibit tumor growth or exhibit cytoreductive activity in mouse
models and the relationship of antitumor efficacy to plasma concentration were also assessed.
PK/PDx relationships for NICD inhibition were assessed exclusively in the HPB‐ALL xenograft
model due to the robust and consistent level of NICD inhibition observed in this model.
To evaluate the NICD pharmacodynamic (PDx) response to nirogacestat, HPB‐ALL tumors were
harvested at several time points after oral administration at 2 dose levels (50 and 200 mg/kg) in
single‐dose studies. NICD levels in tumors were quantitated by Western blot analysis using an
antibody against NICD. At 50 mg/kg, > 50% reduction in NICD was observed by 4 hours and
approximately 70% reduction in NICD was obtained at 24 hours postdose. The 200‐mg/kg dose
level was observed for up to 72 hours and achieved maximal NICD inhibition of approximately
80%, with the duration of NICD inhibition remaining greater than 40% for up to 48 hours.
Similar results were also observed in the SupT1 T‐ALL xenograft model.
The HPB‐ALL xenograft model was utilized to evaluate the relationship of TGI to time,
dose‐dependent inhibition of NICD, and plasma exposure of nirogacestat. To assess antitumor
efficacy in HPB‐ALL model, initial repeat dose studies were performed at 3 dose levels (17, 50,
and 150 mg/kg BID for 14 days; PF‐03084014‐Pharm‐001). Tumor volume was measured
utilizing electronic Vernier calipers. Nirogacestat demonstrated robust antitumor activity in this
model after 14 days of treatment in tumor‐bearing mice (Figure 1A). Maximum TGI (> 100%) by
nirogacestat occurred at 150 mg/kg, with tumor regression observed in 6 out of 8 animals. A
similar repeat dose study in the HPB‐ALL xenograft using doses of 75 and 150 mg/kg BID was
reported for up to 11 days (Wei et al. 2010). Maximal median reduction in tumor volume of
approximately 75% and 92% was observed at the 75‐ and 150‐mg/kg dose levels by Day 11,
respectively (Figure 1B). Nirogacestat was well tolerated as no significant weight loss,
morbidity, or mortality was observed at doses up to 100 mg/kg BID. At 150 mg/kg, slight body
weight loss (10%–15%) was observed sporadically and this occurred usually after ~10 days of
dosing. Dosing holidays were given to mice that lost > 10% body weight until the body weight
returned to normal. Additional immunohistochemical and gene expression assays in these
studies supported the inhibition of Notch signaling and induction of apoptosis as associated
effects of nirogacestat administration (Wei et al. 2010). Tumor samples at Day 7 of 150 mg/kg
BID treatment showed significant reduction of NICD by Western blot relative to vehicle controls
53

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

and notable reduction of expression in Notch‐target genes HES1 and C‐MYC by reverse
transcription polymerase chain reaction (RT‐PCR) evaluation. Day 7 samples from the same
treatment also exhibited a notable reduction of Ki67 staining and an increase in activated
caspase‐3 levels.
Figure 1:

Tumor Volume Reduction in Notch‐driven HPB‐ALL Xenograft Models

The robustness of the HPB‐ALL models suggests the mechanism of TGI is partly mediated through cell growth
inhibition by nirogacestat. Tumor volume reduction was largely dose‐ and drug exposure‐dependent with an
exception at the lower dose levels (17 or 50 mg/kg). The exact reason for this observation is unclear although it
is likely due to the high variability in tumor size of this model.

Key conclusions:


At 150 mg/kg, inhibition of NICD between ~40%‐80% was required to achieve complete
inhibition of tumor growth (109% TGI at 150 mg/kg BID × 14d).



At 50 mg/kg, less NICD inhibition (20%‐40%) was consistent with partial inhibition of
tumor growth (43% TGI at 50 mg/kg BID × 14d).



Continuous NICD inhibition for the duration of the administration schedule at either
dose level is necessary to achieve corresponding TGI.

PK/PDx Modeling
The PK/PDx data used for the modeling were based on 2 different studies with testing dose
levels of 50 and 200 mg/kg over a 72‐hour period (PF‐03084014‐Pharm‐001). Based on this
PK/PDx modeling, the plasma free IC50 for NICD inhibition in vivo was estimated at 0.42 nM,
which is consistent with free cellular potency determined in HEK293 cells (Table 2). Single‐dose
PDx analysis revealed that the 50‐mg/kg dose level yielded ~40%‐50% NICD reduction, which
correlates with ~50% TGI upon continuous BID dosing at this dose level. At 50 mg/kg, the free
plasma area under the plasma concentration‐time curve from 0 to 24 hours (AUC0‐24) was 56

ng∙h/mL and free plasma steady‐state average concentration (Cavg) was 2.3 ng/mL (4.8 nM).
Taking into consideration protein binding differences between mouse and human, this target
free plasma Cavg of 2.3 ng/mL equates to a total human plasma Cavg of 575 ng/mL, and this free
plasma AUC0‐24 of 56 ng∙h/mL equates to a total human plasma AUC0‐24 of 14,000 ng∙h/mL.
These predicted efficacious exposures are most comparable to the daily AUC of 12,860 ng∙h/mL
observed at steady state in participants receiving 150 mg BID in Study A8641014.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. Note that the steady state AUC0‐tau (where
tau=12hrs) in patients with desmoid tumors was 3370 ng∙hr/mL after administration of
nirogacestat at 150 mg BID.
Secondary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat was evaluated for interaction with a number of other proteases, receptors, ion
channels, and kinases. Activity (IC50) in a broad panel of common receptors was > 1 μM in all
assays (757060). The IC50 was determined for all receptors where inhibition was greater than
50% at the screening concentration of 10 mM. Testing at other aspartic proteases (pepsin A,
BACE 1 and cathepsin D) or serine proteases (chymotrypsin A, trypsin) showed negligible
inhibition at 20 mM (PF‐03084014‐Pharm‐001). Nirogacestat also showed less than 25%
inhibition at 10 mM in a panel of 10 kinase enzymes, including ABL, CK1d, GSK3β, IKK2, IKKi,
LCK, MK2, P38, PKA, PKCzeta (PF‐03084014‐Pharm‐001). By comparison, the human serum free
Cmax observed at steady‐state in Study A8641014 was 4.98 ng/mL (10.2 nM) after oral
administration of 150 mg BID. Therefore, these off‐target hits pose a minimal risk at the current
therapeutic dose.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position; however, FDA notes the units in the
Applicant’s position that are in mM should be µM based on the study report.

the human ether‐à‐go‐go related gene (hERG) to assess the risk of QT prolongation. The 1.0 µM
hERG IC50 is 98‐fold over the human free geometric mean Cmax of 4.98 ng/mL (10.2 nM) after
administration of 150 mg BID as observed in Study A8641014. For the in vivo studies, a
respiratory and neurobehavior study were conducted in rats, while a stand‐alone

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
The in vitro and in vivo nonclinical pharmacokinetic (PK) studies were designed to characterize
the absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) properties of nirogacestat.
These studies enabled thorough evaluation of its pharmacological and toxicological profiles in
preclinical animal models. Toxicokinetic (TK) data for nirogacestat were derived from Good

Laboratory Practice (GLP) studies using liquid chromatography‐tandem mass spectrometry
(LC‐MS/MS) methods validated under GLP conditions. Plasma protein binding and blood‐to‐
plasma ratio were determined in mouse, rat, dog, and human. Brain penetration was evaluated
in mouse and tissue distribution and excretion was evaluated in rat and dog. Initial in vitro
metabolite profiling was performed across species to support selection of toxicology species.
The in vitro cytochrome P450 (CYP) inhibition and induction potential of nirogacestat was also
evaluated and nirogacestat was examined for effects on drug transporters in cell lines
expressing specific hepatic and renal transporters to guide drug interaction risk assessments.
Pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PDx) modeling was performed on
nirogacestat‐mediated inhibition of NICD levels in tumors as a biomarker for inhibition of tumor
growth. The ADME characterization of nirogacestat is sufficient to support the NDA.
Absorption
Nirogacestat exhibits moderate permeability in Madin‐Darby canine kidney (MDCK) cells (19SPRWP7), and at a
pH range of 1.2 to 6.0 a 150 mg dose will be 100% dissolved; solubility decreases at pH ≥ 6.5 (19SPRWP12).

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)
Distribution
Volume of Distribution

Estimates of apparent volume of distribution at steady state for nirogacestat
were greater than total body water in rats and dogs, suggesting that
nirogacestat readily distributes to tissues in these species.

Protein Binding

Initial plasma protein binding was reported in DM2006‐03084014‐020; however,
the human plasma unbound fraction from that study was later found to be
erroneously high. Serum protein binding was re‐evaluated (191159, 135033, and
21SPRWP4) for mouse (CD1), rat (Sprague‐Dawley), and dog (Beagle) with
unbound free fractions of 0.2% for all three species. The fraction unbound of
0.4% was determined for human plasma and serum in confirmatory studies
(2103037‐03 and 21SPRWP2).

Tissue Distribution

A whole‐body autoradiography study showed that radioactivity derived from
[14C] nirogacestat is widely distributed throughout the body as early as
30 minutes postdose (8299694). CNS exposure may be possible; however,
radioactivity was primarily concentrated in non‐circumventricular tissues. The
extensive metabolism observed in mass‐balance studies conducted in rat
(8299694), dog (SPW/02), and human (NIR‐DT‐101 and NIR‐DT‐102) may also
limit the interpretation of the autoradiography study with regard to tissue
distribution of unchanged nirogacestat. A CNS distribution study
(DM2006‐03084014‐017) showed that the brain‐to‐plasma AUC ratio was
approximately 6‐fold higher in mdr1a/1b(‐/‐) knockout mice compared with wild
type, suggesting that P‐gp efflux may serve to prevent accumulation of
nirogacestat in the brain.

Metabolism

Metabolite Profile

In vitro metabolism of nirogacestat is consistent across nonclinical species
and humans. All circulating metabolites observed in humans were present in
microsomal incubations of one or more of the evaluated nonclinical species.
In vitro, CYP3A4 was the major enzyme responsible for the metabolism of
nirogacestat. This observation was supported by a healthy volunteer clinical
DDI study (NIR‐DT‐103) where itraconazole‐mediated CYP3A4 inhibition
increased the serum AUC of nirogacestat by 8.23‐fold. In vivo mass‐balance
studies in rat and dog both identified M283 (PF‐03015273) as the most
abundant major metabolite, though human studies (NIR‐DT‐101 and
NIR‐DT‐102) found this metabolite to represent less than 10% of radioactivity
recovered in serum. In all mass‐balance studies, the largest fraction of
recovered radioactivity is recovered in serum and was consistently unretained
in high‐performance liquid chromatography systems. As such, the resulting
metabolites were not identified, but it is believed that they represent polar,
low molecular weight compounds that are the result of extensive metabolism
of the radiolabel into endogenous biomolecules.

CYP Induction and
Inhibition

Nirogacestat induced CYP3A4, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19 in
human hepatocytes. Nirogacestat also reversibly inhibited CYP3A.
Nirogacestat was determined to be a time‐dependent inhibitor of CYP3A.
Nirogacestat did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and
CYP2D6 and demonstrated little to no time‐dependent inhibitory potency for
these enzymes. In a healthy volunteer clinical DDI study (A8641002), the net

58

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)
effect of nirogacestat‐mediated CYP3A4 interaction increased the serum AUC
of midazolam by 58.9%.
Excretion
Nirogacestat is unlikely to be a substrate or inhibitor of MATE1, MATE2K, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, or OCT2 at clinically relevant concentrations (092718 and
19SPRWP5).

Transporters

Nirogacestat had an efflux ratio of 1.4 in BCRP‐MDCK cells and is not likely to be a
substrate of BCRP (14PFIZP1S1PF37P). Nirogacestat did not reach an IC50 for BCRP
inhibition in MDCKII‐LE cells at concentrations up to 120 µM (163120). Metabolite
M283 (PF‐03015273) exhibited poor permeability across MDR1‐MDCK cells and was
determined to be a substrate (efflux ratio 4.75) but not an inhibitor of P‐gp (2003103
and 2003104).
An observed efflux ratio of 4.6 in MDR1‐transfected MDCK cells suggests that
nirogacestat is a substrate of P‐gp (13PFIZP4S2‐PF‐8). Nirogacestat also inhibited
P‐gp‐mediated efflux of digoxin in vitro with an IC50 of 2.3 µM. However, in a
healthy volunteer clinical DDI study (NIR‐DT‐103), nirogacestat‐mediated P‐gp
inhibition had no effect on the exposure of dabigatran etexilate.

Excretion Route

In nonclinical studies, the primary route of excretion was the feces with more than
70% of the radiolabeled dose recovered in feces in both rats and dogs. In bile duct
canulated (BDC) rats, the primary elimination pathway following oral administration
is hepatic clearance and biliary excretion with more than 46% of radioactivity
recovered in the feces and between 28 to 31% excretion of radioactivity via biliary
excretion.
Mean urinary excretion in mass‐balance studies was 5.2% and 3.1% in rats and dogs,
respectively. A mean of 7.0% of radiolabel was recovered in expired air in rats.
In non‐radiolabeled rat studies where nirogacestat was administered orally or
intravenously, < 1% of unchanged nirogacestat was recovered in bile, urine, or feces.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. Additional notable findings are described
below.
Tissue distribution: Excluding excreta, the highest Cmax levels of radiolabeled [14C]
nirogacestat were uveal tract, liver, adrenal gland, spleen, and pituitary gland. Radiolabeled
[14C] nirogacestat exhibited an affinity for pigmented ocular tissues containing melanin.
Although low concentrations of [14C] nirogacestat‐derived radioactivity were present in non‐
circumventricular central nervous system (CNS) tissues, radioactivity was higher in the choroid
plexus than in the blood (approximately 7.5‐fold higher AUC and 4.4‐fold higher Cmax), which
may suggest that nirogacestat crosses the blood‐cerebrospinal fluid barrier. At 672 hours
postdose, radioactivity concentrations persisted in thirteen tissues (adrenal gland, bone
marrow, exorbital lacrimal gland, eyes, lymph nodes, meninges, pituitary gland, spleen,
stomach mucosa, thymus, thyroid, uveal tract, and vitreous humor), most notably the uveal

Toxicology

General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat was assessed in a series of nonclinical toxicology studies in alignment with
applicable ICH guidance documents. The oral route of exposure was selected for these studies
since the oral route is the intended clinical route of administration. The rat and dog were
selected as the toxicology species based on nirogacestat PK and metabolism properties
compared across species. The definitive studies were performed in the United States in
accordance with the United States Department of Health and Human Services, FDA, United
States Code of Federal Regulations, Title 21, Part 58: GLP for Nonclinical Laboratory Studies.
Nirogacestat was administered to rats and dogs in repeat dose general toxicology studies up to
3 months in duration followed by a 1‐month recovery. A 1‐month mouse study was conducted
to support a 6‐month carcinogenicity study in transgenic mice. Many of the
Nirogacestat‐related toxicities were attributed to GS inhibition and disruption of the Notch
signaling pathway. In the 1‐month mouse study, target organs in males and females included
the small intestine (duodenum, jejunum, and ileum), liver, femoral physis, sternal cartilage,
thymus, and sex organs in both male and female mice. The no‐observed‐adverse effect‐level
(NOAEL) in the 1‐month mouse study was 20 mg/kg/day. In the 3‐month rat study, ovarian
atrophy, alterations in the estrous cycle and decreased cellularity in gut‐associated lymphoid
tissue (GALT) in females, and decreased cellularity of mesenteric lymph nodes in males and
females at 5 mg/kg/day was observed. A NOAEL was not identified in this 3‐month oral toxicity
study in rats due to these effects. In addition, all dose levels evaluated in the rat study showed
chronic progressive nephropathy, pulmonary phospholipidosis, and salivary gland necrosis in a
dose‐dependent manner. In the dog study, treatment‐related effects were present within the
intestines, spleen, gall bladder, liver, kidney, testes, and ovary. The intestinal and liver findings
were associated with generalized inflammation and associated clinical pathology changes in
most of these animals. In the recovery dogs, only the intestinal, testicular, and ovarian findings
were persistent but at lower severity, suggesting evidence of reversibility. Due to oocyte
mineralization at the lowest dose in the dog study, a NOAEL was not identified.

Dehydration, hunched posture, thin or ungroomed fur in the high‐dose group.
Due to these findings, the high dose was suspended from Days 9 through 13 and
reinitiated on Day 14 at a lower dose of 200 mg/kg/day.

Elevations in white blood cell parameters in high dose male mice

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)
Hematology and
Flow Cytometry

Decrease in total lymphocyte count was observed at 150 mg/kg/day, along with a
decrease in T‐cell population at 20 mg/kg/day, and a decrease in natural killer (NK) and
B cells was observed at 150 mg/kg/day. IgD expression was decreased in B cells at

20 mg/kg/day

Clinical Chemistry

Slight increase in γ‐glutamyl transferase at 150 mg/kg/day

Organ Weight

Meaningful organ to body weight ratio change compared to control was limited to
decreases in ovarian weight at > 20 mg/kg/day.

Microscopic
Observations



Hypertrophic retention of bone in the sternum and joint at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day



Ovarian atrophy and vaginal asynchrony at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day



Lymphoid depletion in spleen, thymus, lymph nodes in 20 mg/kg/day females



Hepatocellular vacuolation consisting of lipid in the cytoplasm of periportal
hepatocytes was noted in a few individual rats at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day

Post‐Dose
Observations

Effects on body weights and food consumption recovered after cessation of dosing at
150 mg/kg/day. Partial reversibility of microscopic changes in the ovary and bone in the
150‐mg/kg/day group.

Day 30
Findings
Died or Sacrificed
Moribund

Moribund animals were euthanized early on Days 84 and 89 in the high dose females.
Due to mortality and adverse clinical signs in female rats at the 50‐mg/kg/day dose
level, dose administration was terminated for female rats at this dose level after 88
consecutive days. Two remaining female toxicokinetic animals at this dose level were
incorporated into the main study on Day 88 and a cohort of 50‐mg/kg/day female rats
were necropsied on Day 89, while the remaining female rats in this dose level
continued on study to assess recovery.

Body Weight and
Food Consumption

On Day 64 for male rats and Day 57 for female rats, decreases in mean body weight
gain were observed at 50 mg/kg/day, secondary to significant decreases in food
consumption in most female rats.

No Noteworthy
Findings

Ophthalmology and urinalysis

Clinical
Observations

Limited to the high dose group and consisted of decreased activity, hunched posture,
pallor, rough and stained fur, and chromodacryorrhea.

Hematology and
Flow Cytometry

Decrease red blood cell parameters and lymphocytes at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day; increases in
neutrophils at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day. Decreases in all T‐cell subsets and decreased IgD
expression on B cells, and increased NK cells at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day.

Clinical Chemistry

Increased alanine aminotransferase, aspartate aminotransferase, alkaline phosphatase,
gamma‐glutamyl transferase, blood urea nitrogen, creatinine, and total bilirubin in
females at 50 mg/kg/day. Slight increases in alanine aminotransferase, aspartate
aminotransferase, and cholesterol were elevated in mid‐dose females and high dose
males.

Findings
Hematology and
Flow Cytometry

Decreased red blood cell parameters (89‐96% of control) and lymphocytes (64–71% of
control) at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day; increases in neutrophils (2.27‐5.22x control) at ≥ 20

Clinical Chemistry

decreases in total protein and albumin (91‐93% of control mean) were observed in 50
mg/kg/day males compared to controls. In 20 mg/kg/day females, increases in ALT
(2.15x control), total protein (1.09x control), and cholesterol (1.37x control) and
decreases in sodium (98% of control) were observed compared to controls.

Findings
Died or Sacrificed
Moribund

Two high‐dose dogs and 1 mid‐dose dog were euthanized early for humane reasons
due to adverse clinical signs. Due to these effects in the high dose group after 5 to
7 days of administration, dosing was suspended for 26 days, and the dose was lowered

Food Consumption

period. This led to a slight decrease in body weight gain.

activity, watery/discolored stools, inappetence, weight loss, and overall poor condition.
Animals that survived clinical signs included emesis and decreased activity at
≥ 10 mg/kg/day.

Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The genotoxicity of nirogacestat was assessed in a battery of genotoxicity studies in alignment
with ICH S2(R1) guidance. Nirogacestat was assessed in vitro in the bacterial mutagenicity assay
(06GR106), the in vitro cytogenetic (human lymphocyte) assay (06GR107), and in vivo in a rat
micronucleus study (01214020). These in vitro tests were conducted with and without
exogenous metabolic activation using concentrations up to those limited by cytotoxicity or
insolubility, while in vivo nirogacestat was dosed up to the limit dose of 2000 mg/kg/day.

Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
The carcinogenicity of nirogacestat was assessed in a 6‐month repeat dose study in transgenic
rasH2 mice (20174162). Nirogacestat was administered orally BID (approximately 10 hours
apart) for a minimum of 26 weeks to cByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous mice at dose levels of 5, 15,
and 50 mg/kg/dose (10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day).
77
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Survival at 10 or 30 mg/kg/day nirogacestat ranged from 92% to 96% in males and from 96 to
100% in females and was comparable to controls, whereas survival at 100 mg/kg/day was
reduced to 76% in males and females at study termination. Although there were no statistically
significant effects on mortality when pairwise comparisons were made between the control
group and 100 mg/kg/day males or females, there was a statistically significant dose‐related
trend of increased mortality in the females.
Administration of nirogacestat BID by oral gavage to Tg RasH2 mice for 26 weeks at 10 or
30 mg/kg/day resulted in no effects on survival or tumor formation. At 100 mg/kg/day,
administration of nirogacestat resulted in an increase in the frequency of spontaneous
hemangiosarcoma in males and females. There were no significant increases in new neoplasms
associated with nirogacestat administration at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day. The systemic
exposures in mice at this dose level are below those in humans administered nirogacestat at
150 mg BID.
Based on the Agency’s feedback for Study 20174162 provided by email on 11 January 2022,
SpringWorks submitted an updated waiver request for its proposal that a 2‐year rat
carcinogenicity study was not required. As requested by the Agency, the updated justification
addressed duration and exposure, carcinogenic potential, genotoxicity, indication and
participant population, general toxicology study findings (e.g., histopathology, findings from the
26‐week mouse carcinogenicity study, and other relevant factors). See Table 14 for safety
margin calculations.

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The developmental and reproductive toxicology assessment of nirogacestat consisted of two
separate fertility studies in male and female rats and a preliminary embryo‐fetal developmental
(EFD) toxicity study in pregnant rats. The first fertility study was conducted by simultaneous
treatment of male and female rats before mating (01214013). The second fertility study was
conducted by treating male rats with nirogacestat then mating with untreated female rats,
along with treated female rats mated with untreated known breeding male rats (01214026).
Only Study 0124026 is tabulated below.
Nirogacestat produced embryo toxicity when administered to pregnant rats, and impaired
fertility in both male and female rats. In the EFD study in rats, nirogacestat induced significant
embryo loss, early resorptions, and decreased fetal weights in surviving embryos. These
negative effects on embryonic development were anticipated based on transgenic studies in
mice demonstrating that the loss of Notch signaling is embryonically lethal
(Donoviel et al. 1999; Swiatek et al. 1994). The changes in sex organs in nirogacestat‐treated
male and female rats were also anticipated based on the known role of the Notch pathway in
the ovary and testes. The ovarian changes in rats and dogs, along with altered estrous cyclicity,
are likely due to inhibition of Notch signaling in ovaries, as this signaling pathway is critical in
the regulation of mammalian folliculogenesis (Vanorny et al. 2017). Similar ovarian changes
were observed with another GS inhibitor (Simutis et al. 2018). In the testes, the Notch signaling
is critical for spermatogenesis (Murta et al. 2016). These effects on both male and female sex
organs could explain the effects of nirogacestat on fertility indices measured in both male and
female rats.
Based on the severity of the embryo‐fetal toxicities, and the known requirement for the Notch
pathway for normal embryo implantation and development, the weight of evidence provides
justification for not conducting the definitive embryo‐fetal toxicity studies in rats and in rabbits,
as well as not conducting the rat peri‐ and post‐natal development study. As shown by
Donoviel et al (1999), mice lacking Notch activity by inactivation of presenilin 1 and 2
(upstream of Notch and required for GS activity), leads to perinatal lethality due to multiple
early developmental effects. This finding supports earlier studies in mice where a loss of Notch1
is embryonically lethal (Swiatek et al, 1994). In addition, incubating zebrafish embryos with a GS
inhibitor led to impaired somite segmentation (Geling et al 2002), which is similar to other
lethal effects seen in the embryos of mice with altered Notch activity. The embryonic loss in the
80

with the known requirement of Notch for embryonic development. In addition, the exposures
at which these effects occur in rat are below (≤ 0.1‐fold) the human systemic exposure based
on AUC (12860 ng∙hr/mL) after administration of 150 mg of nirogacestat BID. See Table 14 for

Studies on Impurities
The Applicant’s Position:
A GLP 28‐day repeat dose toxicity study was conducted in Sprague Dawley rats to qualify
(b) (4)
(b)
impurities
at (4) % for a nirogacestat dose of 300 mg (91103‐19‐

(b)
each impurity was (4) mg/kg/day. Rats (5/sex/group) received either the vehicle control

Safety Margins
The Applicant’s Position:
The calculated safety margins for the definitive repeat dose toxicity studies with nirogacestat
were calculated by dividing the total AUC0‐24 from the respective toxicology study at the NOAEL
or LOAEL with the human total steady state AUC0‐24 (12860 ng∙hr/mL) dosed at 150 mg BID
(Table 14). The margins were based on exposures that were available at the end of the dosing
period for the toxicity study. Because of the sex difference in rats, the margins were
determined for both sexes, while for dogs and mice, margins were based on sex combined
group mean values. Total exposures were used given that nirogacestat is highly protein bound
in mouse, rat, dog and human plasma with fraction unbound of 0.2%, 0.2%, 0.2%, and 0.4%,
respectively.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s approach to the margin calculations in the table. The human
exposure margins in Table 14 were calculated using a total human steady state AUC of 12860
ng∙hr/mL after administration of nirogacestat at 150 mg BID. Note that the steady state AUC0‐tau
(based on a dosing internal of 12 hours) in patients with desmoid tumors was 3370 ng∙hr/mL (or
approximately 6740 ng∙hr/mL for comparison with animal AUC0‐24 data) after administration of
nirogacestat at 150 mg BID.
