6 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY
Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
Nirogacestat is a gamma secretase (GS) inhibitor that blocks proteolytic activation of the Notch
receptor. When dysregulated, Notch can activate pathways that contribute to tumor growth.
The proposed recommended dosage of nirogacestat is 150 mg BID with or without food.
The clinical pharmacology review focused on the evaluation of appropriateness of the proposed
dosage, administration of nirogacestat with food, use in patients with hepatic impairment (HI),
and potential drug‐drug interactions (DDI).
The proposed dosage of nirogacestat 150 mg BID with dose reduction to 100 mg BID for
selected adverse reactions is generally acceptable based on the integrated analysis of PK, PD,
efficacy and safety. The clinical effectiveness was demonstrated in pivotal Study NIR‐DT‐301
(See Section 8 for details). The overall safety of nirogacestat is considered acceptable and
clinically manageable with a relative high dose intensity (87%). In addition, exposure‐response
analysis supported that patients who underwent dose reductions had similar best overall
response (BOR) rate and duration of response (DoR) than those without dose reduction.
The review team agrees with the Applicant’s proposal of administering nirogacestat without
regard to food as the sub‐study of food effect in the EXPAND‐S phase of Study A8641014
showed that a high‐fat meal had no clinically significant effect on nirogacestat exposure.
The review team assesses that no contraindication of nirogacestat for hepatic impairment (HI)
is necessary based on the clinical AE evaluation on liver toxicity and the results from the HI
study. The review team recommends that no dosage adjustment is necessary in patients with
mild or moderate HI as the change in nirogacestat exposure in patients with moderate HI is not
considered clinically meaningful compared to healthy subjects in the HI study NIR‐DT‐104.
Insufficient data are available to characterize nirogacestat PK in patients with severe HI as
87
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

clinical data was only available in one patient with severe HI. However, the review team also
does not recommend a dosage adjustment in patients with severe HI.
The review team recommends a PMC to conduct a clinical PK study to assess the effect of a
proton pump inhibitor (PPI) on the PK of a single dose of nirogacestat as the in vitro study
demonstrated that the solubility of nirogacestat was reduced by more than 87% at pH values of
6.0 and above. However, the effect of coadministration of acid‐reducing agents (ARAs) with
nirogacestat on its PK, efficacy, and safety has not been characterized in clinical studies. In
addition, the review team recommends that concomitant use of PPIs and H2 receptor
antagonists should be avoided, and that administration of antacids be staggered relative to the
administration of nirogacestat.
The review team agrees with the Applicant’s position that (1) the concomitant use of
nirogacestat with strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers should be avoided based
on the results of the dedicated DDI study NIR‐DT‐103 and PBPK simulations and (2) the
concomitant use with certain CYP3A4 and CYP2C19 substrates should be avoided where
minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions (CYP3A4 substrates) or
significant decreases in efficacy (CYP2C19 substrates) based on the PBPK simulations.
6.1.1 Recommendations:
This NDA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with
specific recommendations and comments are summarized in Table 15 below.

6.1.2 Post‐Marketing Requirements and Commitments
A PMC is proposed to evaluate if proton pump inhibitors alter the bioavailability of nirogacestat
and to determine appropriate dosing recommendations for nirogacestat with regard to use of
concomitant gastric acid reducing agents. Refer to Section below describing PMCs/PMRs.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment

Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
Nirogacestat PK has been studied in 6 completed Phase 1 clinical studies conducted in healthy
adult human participants (Studies A8641001, A8641002, A8641008, NIR‐DT‐101, NIR‐DT‐102,
and NIR‐DT‐103). An additional Phase 1 study (NIR‐DT‐104) in participants with hepatic
impairment is ongoing; an interim PK analysis has been completed and included in this review.
In these studies, single oral doses of nirogacestat up to 150 mg and multiple doses up to 95 mg
(Study A8641002) QD for up to 14 days have been administered. Nirogacestat was studied in
participants with advanced cancers as a monotherapy in Study A8641014 utilizing doses ranging
from 20 mg to 330 mg twice daily (BID), continuously. Additional investigations as monotherapy
and in combination with other agents in breast cancer and pancreatic cancer were conducted;
however, data were limited with regard to understanding the PK and PDx of nirogacestat. In
addition, PK samples were collected in a Phase 3 study (Study NIR‐DT‐301; see Section 8.1.1)
that was conducted using nirogacestat 150 mg BID in adult participants with progressing DT/AF.
A pediatric study sponsored by the Children’s Oncology Group (COG) is currently ongoing in
participants aged > 12 months to < 18 years of age; data from this ongoing study is not included
in this NDA.
Evaluation of the nirogacestat PK characteristics in healthy participants and participants with
cancer diagnoses indicated that the compound was rapidly absorbed, with median time of
occurrence of maximum serum concentration (Cmax) (Tmax) values of 0.5 to 1 hour generally
observed. Nirogacestat exposure increased with escalating single and repeat doses, with
greater than dose proportional increases observed across the dose range evaluated in single
and multiple ascending dose studies. The terminal elimination half‐life (t1/2) of nirogacestat
was approximately 19 hours after single doses and 26 hours after multiple doses. Nirogacestat
is highly bound to both proteins, but with a greater affinity for serum human α1‐acid
glycoprotein (AAG) than for serum albumin. In vitro studies indicated that nirogacestat is
primarily metabolized by cytochrome P450 3A4 (CYP3A4). The impact of CYP3A4 metabolism on
nirogacestat bioavailability and elimination was confirmed in a drug interaction study with
itraconazole, leading to the conclusion that nirogacestat is a sensitive substrate for CYP3A4.
90
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Two human mass balance studies (NIR‐DT‐101 and NIR‐DT‐102) were conducted to examine
routes of excretion and identify potentially active metabolites. The primary route of excretion
was through feces and very little unchanged nirogacestat recovered in feces, urine, or expired
air. No major circulating metabolites were identified.
The PK of nirogacestat in pediatric populations has not been fully evaluated at this time. An
Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) is in place to inform use in children. Studies have not
been performed specifically in geriatric populations; however, age was a covariate in the
Population PK (PopPK) model and no significant effect of age was found on nirogacestat PK.
Because renal elimination is not the primary clearance pathway, studies have not been
performed for a renally impaired population. A study is currently ongoing to evaluate
nirogacestat in participants with hepatic impairment (Study‐NIR‐DT‐104); interim PK data from
this study establish that the PK of nirogacestat is altered by hepatic function. Specifically,
participants that met the moderate impairment criteria by Child‐Pugh but did not have total
bilirubin levels > 1.5 × upper limit of normal (ULN) (National Cancer Institute – Organ
Dysfunction Working Group [NCI‐ODWG] moderate severity Group C) did not show significantly
altered nirogacestat PK. Therefore, it is reasonable to conclude that patients with mild or
moderate hepatic impairment and total bilirubin levels ≤ 1.5 × ULN (NCI‐ODWG normal or mild)
(b) (4)
can be treated with nirogacestat without dose modification.
Study A8641014 evaluated participants for a
food effect on PK and reported that the geometric means were similar in the fed versus fasted
condition for both Cmax and the area under the plasma concentration‐time curve over the
dosing interval (AUCtau); however, significant variability was observed for both PK parameters.
Given the observed efficacy and safety parameters from Study NIR‐DT‐301 combined with
reasonable variability from the food effect assessment in Study A8641014 relative to other
assessments of nirogacestat PK, nirogacestat is recommended for administration without
regard to food.
Nirogacestat also demonstrated evidence for both reversible and time‐dependent inhibition of
CYP3A4. A clinical drug interaction study (NIR‐DT‐103) evaluating the effects of nirogacestat on
midazolam identified a weak inhibition effect on CYP3A4. In vitro studies indicated that
nirogacestat inhibited the efflux transporter P‐glycoprotein (P‐gp) at clinically relevant
concentrations; however, exposure of total dabigatran was not affected by nirogacestat
administration in a clinical drug interaction study, supporting the absence of clinically
meaningful P‐gp inhibition by nirogacestat. A physiologically based pharmacokinetic model
(PBPK) was developed based on in vitro and clinical data for nirogacestat and the effects of
CYP3A induction on nirogacestat, as well as potential for nirogacestat to induce several CYP
enzymes, were predicted using this model. Nirogacestat was predicted to have a potential
91

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)
(b)

induction effect on CYP2C19 in a sensitivity analysis using (4) times the maximum induction rate
(IndMax) identified through in vitro evaluations; however, a minimal effect was predicted at the
IndMax. The PBPK model was also applied to simulate the effects of various dose modification
strategies in the event of a need to prescribe strong or moderate inhibitors of CYP3A4.
A population PK (PopPK) analysis was conducted using studies from 6 healthy participant
studies (A8641001, A8641002, A8641008, NIR‐DT‐101, NIR‐DT‐102, and NIR‐DT‐103), a Phase 1
study in participants with advanced solid tumors (A8641014), and the Phase 3 study in
participants with DT (NIR‐DT‐301). A three‐compartment structural model, with two peripheral
distribution compartments, first‐order absorption with lag time, and central clearance was
selected to describe nirogacestat PK. Notable covariates included itraconazole administration
on bioavailability and clearance, participant population on clearance, and weight on the volume
of distribution for the 2 peripheral compartments. Clearance was reduced approximately 44%
in the advanced solid tumor and T‐ALL/lymphoblastic lymphoma (LBL) population relative to
healthy participants and participants with DT, suggesting that PK in participants with DT is more
similar to healthy participants than participants with advanced malignancies.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat is rapidly absorbed, highly protein bound, and extensively metabolized by CYP3A4.
No major circulating metabolites with activity against GS are known to occur and very little
unchanged nirogacestat is recovered in feces, urine, or expired air. A PopPK analysis using
several healthy participant and participant studies found that nirogacestat PK in participants
with DT is more similar to PK observed in healthy participants than to PK observed in
participants with advanced malignancies. Given the observed efficacy and safety parameters
from Study NIR‐DT‐301 combined with reasonable variability from the food effect assessment
in Study A8641014 relative to other assessments of nirogacestat PK, nirogacestat is
recommended for administration without regard to food. The clinical PK of nirogacestat has
been well characterized.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the available PK data. However, FDA
notes that there is a significant difference in PK of nirogacestat administered to patients with
DT and nirogacestat administered to patients with other solid tumor malignancies. Thus, the
listed PK parameters such as, Cmax, AUCtau, and terminal elimination half‐life of nirogacestat at
steady state following 150 mg BID in patients from Study A8641014 may not be representative
of patients with DT. Section 12 in the nirogacestat USPI reflects PK data from patients with DT.
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that the concomitant use of nirogacestat with strong
or moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers should be avoided. FDA also agrees with the

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
General Dosing
Applicant’s Position:
The selection of 150 mg BID as the RP2D was made using the totality of evidence from
Study A8641014, a Phase 1 dose‐finding study of nirogacestat in participants with advanced
solid tumors (see Section 8.1.7). This included the identification of the maximum tolerated dose
(MTD) at 220 mg BID and data from PK and PDx measurements, as well as review of objective
responses among a broad range of solid tumor types. The high response rate in the participants
with DT (objective response rate [ORR]: 71.4%) led to the initiation of a Phase 2 investigator‐
initiated study that was conducted at 150 mg BID (Study 14‐C‐0007; Kummar et al. 2017). The
rate of response for participants with DT in these two studies (ORR: 71.4% [Study A8641014]
and 31% [Study 14‐C‐0007]), and the relative tolerability of the 150 mg BID dose level
compared to the 220 mg BID dose level were primary factors in selection of the 150 mg BID
dose in Study NIR‐DT‐301. For selected toxicities, dosing was interrupted and/or reduced to
100 mg BID.
The FDA’s Assessment:
See FDA’s assessment in the section 6.3.2.2 below for details.

Therapeutic Individualization
The Applicant’s Position:
As described in Section 6.2.3, dosing will be interrupted and/or reduced to 100 mg BID for
patients who experience selected toxicities or for patients who experience life‐threatening
adverse reactions.

Nirogacestat PK has been characterized based on extrinsic and intrinsic factors. No therapeutic

Outstanding Issues
The Applicant’s Position:
As discussed during the Pre‐NDA meeting, the Phase 1 study NIR‐DT‐104 evaluating the effects
of hepatic impairment on the PK of nirogacestat is ongoing. An interim PK analysis was
submitted in the NDA.
As described in Section 6.2.1 and Section 10, the pediatric study, ARST1921, is currently
ongoing. As stated in the Agreed iPSP letter, SpringWorks has requested a deferral of pediatric
studies in the NDA.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant submitted an interim PPK report, which was sufficient to evaluate the effect of
hepatic impairment on nirogacestat PK; thus, hepatic impairment is no longer an outstanding
issue.
The review team will issue a PMC to evaluate the effects of a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) on the
PK of nirogacestat.

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review

General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
Data:
Drug‐drug interaction (DDI) risks are summarized in Section 6.2.4. Clinical pharmacology and an
overview of ADME properties are provided below.
94
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Clinical Pharmacology: Study A8641001 demonstrated that, following single oral doses of
nirogacestat in healthy participants, mean exposures as measured by AUC and Cmax increased
proportionally up to the 5‐mg dose group, with greater than dose proportional increases
observed from 10 to 40 mg. A less than proportional increase in AUC and Cmax was noted
between the 40 mg and 65 mg doses and a slightly greater than dose proportional increase was
observed during the final dose escalation from 65 mg to 120 mg. In Study A8641002,
nirogacestat exposures (AUC and Cmax) in healthy participants increased with increasing dose
over the 7 mg BID to 95 mg QD dose range following repeat administration for 14 days.
Nirogacestat systemic exposures were slightly greater than dose proportional across all dose
groups. In Study A8641014, nirogacestat exhibited generally dose proportional behavior (AUCtau
and Cmax) over the dose range tested in participants with advanced solid tumor. In summary,
nirogacestat has a tendency toward slightly greater than dose proportional increases in
exposure below 100 mg and somewhat dose proportional increases between 100 mg and
220 mg.
PK parameters tend to have substantial inter‐subject variability as most coefficient of variation
(CV) values were > 30% and even approached 100% in some cases. The geometric mean for Cmax
was 612 ng/mL in Part 3 of Study NIR‐DT‐103 and 534 ng/mL in Part 2 of Study NIR‐DT‐102 but
ranged from 822 to 943 ng/mL in the other studies. Similar patterns are observed with these
studies for other parameters as well, with AUCinf being lower and apparent clearance (CL/F)
higher than in other studies. While these deviations from the other studies are notable, the
geometric mean values from these study parts are within the range of variability observed in all
studies. The median Tmax was approximately 1 hour across studies. In general, the central
tendency and large inter‐subject variability for single‐dose PK parameters for nirogacestat at
150 mg have been consistent across studies. Rich PK sampling in multiple dose studies using
doses at or greater than 100 mg BID has only been conducted in Study A8641014. Therefore,
the generalized values for Cmax (1246 ng/mL) and AUCtau (6430 ng∙h/mL) at steady‐state are
taken from the 150 mg BID evaluation at C1D21 in Study A8641014.
In Study A8641002, the median accumulation ratio at Day 14 for healthy participants was 1.12,
1.35, and 1.73 for regimens of 95 mg QD, 40 mg QD, and 7 mg BID, respectively. In
Study A8641014, the median accumulation ratio at Day 21 for participants with advanced solid
tumors ranged from 1.18 to 1.6 between 20 mg BID and 80 mg BID and ranged from 1.98 to
2.84 between 100 mg BID and 220 mg BID.
Absorption: Nirogacestat is rapidly absorbed, with median Tmax observed approximately 1 hour
following administration across all studies. Comparison of exposures from an intravenous dose
of radiolabeled nirogacestat and orally administered nirogacestat in Study NIR‐DT‐102
determined a mean absolute bioavailability of 19.2% (range: 16.2% to 24.3). Based on the high
95
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

level of radiolabel recovery in the mass balance assessments from this study and the increase in
bioavailability observed when concomitantly administered with itraconazole in
Study NIR‐DT‐103, it is likely that the absolute bioavailability of nirogacestat is limited by a
strong first pass metabolism effect in the gastrointestinal tract.
Distribution: Data suggest that the distribution of nirogacestat may be limited by serum AAG
levels and that nirogacestat may be more widely distributed when AAG is low. The potential for
wide distribution is also supported by the results of the PopPK analysis. Observations of low
exposure in the central nervous system (CNS) may be due to low bioavailability of free
nirogacestat and insufficient distribution throughout the body after a single dose.
Metabolism: Nirogacestat is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4. Nirogacestat is extensively
metabolized and leaves no major structural metabolites that would be active against GS. No
major circulating active metabolites are believed to be active against GS.
Excretion: Nirogacestat is extensively metabolized in the gastrointestinal tract and by hepatic
metabolism. Feces is the primary route of excretion for drug‐related material. While renal
excretion is an insignificant route of elimination for unchanged nirogacestat, renal clearance
may be an important route of elimination for some metabolites of nirogacestat.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat is rapidly absorbed with the potential for wide distribution. Nirogacestat is
extensively metabolized, and the primary route of excretion is through feces, with very little
unchanged nirogacestat recovered in feces or urine. No major circulating metabolites were
identified.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.
FDA emphasizes that the reported PK parameters may not be directly applicable to patients
with DT. The population PK model reported that clearance was more than two‐fold higher in
patients with DT (9.1 L/hr) compared to that of patients with other diseases (solid tumor, T‐cell
acute lymphoblastic leukemia/lymphoblastic lymphoma, 4.4L/hr).

(LTS) window of 520 days (refer to OSIS review below). Therefore, these PK samples from 4

FDA agrees with the Applicant to report the nirogacestat excretion results in Part 1 of Study
NIR‐DT‐102 in the labeling given the excretion pathway is not affected by the dosage forms and
formulation (see drug formulation information in Table 17). Following oral administration of 5
μCi of [14C]‐nirogacestat, the mean cumulative recovery in urine and feces combined was 55%,
with 17% being excreted in urine and 38% in feces. Only a small fraction (0.07%) of the dose
was recovered unchanged in urine. The results of recovery in feces and urine were similar to
what was observed in Study NIR‐DT‐101 in which 44% and 18% was recovered in the feces and
urine, respectively. In addition, radioactivity collected in expired air trapping solutions
contributed a further 9.7% for a total cumulative recovery of 65% over the course of 312 hours
post‐dose, while in Part 2 of Study NIR‐DT‐102, radioactivity collected in expired air trapping

excretion and absolute bioavailability.

Although the Applicant did not conduct a relative bioavailability study between PiB, CBS and
tablets, sufficient data were submitted to support using all of the data collected for these
formulations in PK evaluation. PK data from the studies in which PIB and CBS were
administered were useful to inform the population PK modeling and describe the absorption
information at the lower end of the dose range.
Relative BA between uncoated tablet and film‐coated tablets is not necessary as the dissolution
profiles between uncoated and film‐coated tablets are nearly identical at pH conditions of 1.2,
4.5 and 6.8 (refer to CMC review for details). Therefore, the absolute BA of nirogacestat (19%)
in Part 2 of Study NIR‐DT‐102 following oral administration of 150 mg in uncoated tablets is
included in the labeling. Generally, PK parameters of the film‐coated tablets should be that of
the PK parameters estimated for the uncoated tablets.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive
evidence of effectiveness?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes. The effectiveness of nirogacestat 150 mg BID in participants with DT was demonstrated by
the statistically significant improvement in progression‐free survival (PFS) (HR = 0.22; 95% CI:
0.11, 0.42; one‐sided p < 0.001) and response rates of 31% in Study 14‐C‐0007 and 41% vs 8%
for nirogacestat vs placebo in Study NIR‐DT‐301 and the relative tolerability.
Exposure‐response relationships were explored for several efficacy and safety endpoints using
data from Studies A8641014 and NIR‐DT‐301, and the final model from the PopPK analysis. An
exposure‐response plateau was achieved within the predicted exposure ranges (AUCtau and

with allowance for dose modification based on tolerability to ensure optimal exposure for each
individual.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Yes, FDA agrees that the clinical pharmacology program provides supportive evidence of
effectiveness by exposure‐response (E‐R) analyses for efficacy and tumor size.
E‐R analyses were performed between nirogacestat exposure and progression‐free survival
(PFS) based on clinical data in Study A8641014 and Study NIR‐DT‐301. The PFS probability for all
tumors was not associated with nirogacestat exposures at the evaluated dose range. Higher
exposure appeared to correlate with a longer response, though no exposure response
relationship was observed for the duration of response which could be due to small exposure
range for a single dose level. Nirogacestat‐mediated treatment effect on tumor size was
independent of nirogacestat exposure at the evaluated dose range in participants with DT and
non‐DT solid tumors. Predicted change in tumor size following treatment with nirogacestat at
doses ≥ 100 mg BID after 24 months was ‐22% (RECIST sum of longest diameter) and ‐55%
(tumor volume), respectively. In addition, the PK/PD model for tumor size summarized by real
time dose in Study NIR‐DT‐301 indicated comparable slope of tumor size decrease between
patients with and without dose reduction. Refer to the pharmacometrics review in appendix
19.4.3 for details.

Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient
population for which the indication is being sought?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes. The proposed dose of 150 mg BID is effective for patients with DT, with a positive
benefit:risk. Subgroup analyses did not reveal the need for different doses based on sex, race,
age, or weight. In the PopPK analyses, no demographic categories were selected as covariates
100
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

that may alter PK. Most AEs were effectively managed with dose interruptions until recovery,
dose reduction to 100 mg BID, and/or supportive care.
Exposure‐response relationships were explored for several efficacy and safety endpoints using
data from Studies A8641014 and NIR‐DT‐301, and the final model from the Population PK
Analysis. Exposure‐response relationships were explored between nirogacestat exposure and
Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3 rash, treatment‐related TEAEs, SAEs, and TEAEs leading to
discontinuation. No significant relationships were found with these endpoints.
A statistically significant relationship was found between nirogacestat exposure and Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia. Compared to treatment, Cmax was found to be the best predictor of
Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia. Based on PopPK predicted median steady‐state Cmax values for
participants with DT, this exposure‐response model predicts a 6.3% probability of experiencing
a Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia event at the 100 mg dose and a 7.2% probability at the 150 mg
dose. This implies that the 150 mg dose carries a 0.9% absolute increased risk of Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia compared to the 100 mg dose.
Additional analyses were performed for ovarian dysfunction (OD) and follicle‐stimulating
hormone (FSH). No exposure‐response relationship could be identified with OD at the
evaluated dose ranges, but a linear relationship between nirogacestat concentration and FSH
levels in women of childbearing potential (WOCBP) was established. FSH increased linearly with
increasing nirogacestat serum concentrations; however, high variability in the drug effect,
presumably due to menstrual cycle oscillation and sparse sampling, made precise estimation of
drug effects difficult.
A statistically significant relationship was found between exposure and dose modifications.
Participants with higher predicted exposures (AUCtau and Cmin) at steady‐state were more likely
to have had dose modifications than those with lower steady‐state exposures. Given that the
majority of dose modifications were due to TEAEs, this relationship may suggest that
participants with lower nirogacestat clearance were more likely to experience TEAEs that
required a dose modification.
The exposure‐response data support a recommended starting dose of 150 mg for all patients
with allowance for dose modification based on tolerability to ensure optimal exposure for each
individual.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Clinical pharmacology data support the proposed recommended dosage of 150 mg BID with
reduction to 100 mg BID for selected adverse reactions based on the following evidence.



A statistically significant improvement in the primary endpoint of PFS was observed for



ORR, as one of the secondary endpoints, was statistically significantly higher (p < 0.001)
in the nirogacestat arm (41%) versus the placebo arm (8%) in Study NIR‐DT‐301.



PRO assessments evaluated as part of the secondary endpoints at the prespecified
timepoint of Cycle 10 generally favored the nirogacestat arm.



The overall safety profile of nirogacestat is considered acceptable and clinically
manageable. Most frequently (> 30%) reported TEAEs in Study NIR‐DT‐301 and all
patients with DT were diarrhea, nausea, fatigue, hypophosphatemia event, and rash
maculopapular, which are expected with GS inhibition.



Despite high rate of dose interruption and reduction (65%) in the nirogacestat arm, the
relative dose intensity is high (87%) indicating the duration of dosage interruption was
likely short.



E‐R analysis showed that BOR rate and DoR were similar between patients with DT with
dose reductions and those w/o dose reduction.



PK/PD model for tumor size summarized by real time dose in Study NIR‐DT‐301
indicated comparable tumor shrinkage between patients with and without dose
reduction.



Ovarian dysfunction (OD) is a known risk for GS inhibitors based on animal study and is
one of the adverse events of special interest. The Applicant reported OD in 27 of the 36
women of childbearing potential (WOCBP, 75%) in the nirogacestat arm and none in the
37 WOCBP in the placebo arm in Study NIR‐DT‐301. However, there is uncertainty
regarding the characterization of ovarian toxicity, including reversibility. The clinical
team issued a PMR of ovarian toxicity to better characterize this toxicity and clinical
outcome (refer to clinical review section 8 for more details).



PK/PD analyses based on nonclinical and clinical data indicated that 100 mg BID may
have a similar effect in tumor reduction as 150 mg BID, but the sample size for patients
who received 100 mg BID is limited.



E‐R analyses showed positive relationships between nirogacestat exposure and
decreases in serum phosphate, Grade 3+ TEAE and dosage modification as well as a
positive trend between nirogacestat exposure and FSH/ovary disfunction; however, the
dose range used in the E‐R analysis is narrow and the sample size of patients who

meaningful efficacy in patients DT compared to that of placebo. Dosage modifications for
selected AEs are included in the approved labeling to manage adverse reactions.

Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required
for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors (e.g. race, ethnicity,
age, performance status, genetic subpopulations, etc.)?
The Applicant’s Position:
No alternative dosing regimen or management strategy is required for subpopulations based on
intrinsic patient factors in the PopPK analyses.
As described in Section 6.2.4,

(b) (4)

Renal Impairment: Renal elimination of nirogacestat does not appear to be a primary clearance
pathway as supported by the PK characteristics of nirogacestat. No dose adjustments are
required for renal impairment.
Age: No age‐specific differences in TEAEs among adults were observed that would require a
dosing management strategy. Nirogacestat PK has not been evaluated in pediatric populations
to date. A clinical study of nirogacestat in pediatric participants with DT aged > 12 months to
< 18 years of age is ongoing in collaboration with the COG (Study ARST1921). It is generally
assumed that the mechanistic basis for nirogacestat in DT is the same between adults and
children. Age was evaluated as a covariate in the PopPK analysis, which included 25 participants
aged 65 years or older. No significant effect of age on PK was identified.

Hepatic Impairment
(b) (4)

Instead, FDA recommends that no dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with mild,
moderate, or severe HI.
Study NIR‐DT‐104 evaluated nirogacestat PK in patients with impaired hepatic function.
Severity of HI was determined using both the Child‐Pugh Classification system and the NCI‐
ODWG criteria for liver dysfunction. Patients with Child‐Pugh Class A (mild) HI or NCI‐ODWG
Group D (severe) HI were not included in the PK analysis. In total, 11 patients had Child‐Pugh
Class B (moderate) HI and 1 patient Child‐Pugh Class C (severe) HI. Of note, 5, 2, and 4 out of 11
patients in the group of Child‐Pugh Class B (moderate HI) were classified as NCI‐ODWG Group A
(normal hepatic function), Group B (mild HI) and Group C (moderate HI), respectively. The one
patient in the group of Child‐Pugh Class C (severe HI) was classified as NCI‐ODWG Group C
(moderate HI). Table 18 lists the geometric mean ratio [GMR (90% CI)] of nirogacestat exposure
following a single dose of 100 mg in patients with moderate HI by either Child‐Pugh
Classification or NCI‐ODWG criteria compared to that of 12 subjects with normal hepatic
function. Overall, Cmax is lower in patients with moderate HI by either Child‐Pugh Classification
or NCI‐ODWG criteria comparing to healthy subjects.

Safety data from Study NIR‐DT‐104 were also assessed. Only 1 TEAE was reported in a patient,
who had moderate HI by Child‐Pugh and mild HI by NCI‐ODWG criteria. This adverse reaction
was assessed by the investigator as Grade 1 in severity, and the patient recovered the same day
with no intervention. No Grade 3 or 4 adverse reactions were reported. In addition, no clinically
significant shifts in clinical laboratory values or vital signs were noted following administration
of nirogacestat. Although data are limited to a single dose, moderate hepatic impairment did
not appear to significantly increase toxicity.
In addition, per the communication with clinical team, the liver toxicity (AST or ALT elevation)
caused by nirogacestat was considered mild as most AEs were low grade and can be reversed
by dosage modifications.
Despite limited clinical data in patients with severe HI (only one patient per NCI‐ODWG criteria),
the review team does not recommend a dosage adjustment in patients with severe HI. A large
increase in nirogacestat exposure is not expected in patients with severe HI because there was
no significant change in patients with mild or moderate HI. The proposed dosage modifications
(e.g., down to 100 mg BID) are expected to be sufficient to manage any emerging toxicity. A
PMR for a severe HI study was not issued due to feasibility concerns and because severe HI is
not expected to be common in the indicated patient population.
Therefore, combing the clinical AE evaluation on liver toxicity and the results of Study NIR‐DT‐
104, dosage adjustments do not appear to be necessary for patients with mild or moderate HI
classified using either Child‐Pugh or NCI‐ODWG criteria and a contraindication for HI is not
justified given the lack of data in patients with severe HI.

CTNNB1 mutations and treatment efficacy
In study NIR‐DT‐301, genetic mutations (germline and somatic) in the CTNNB1 and APC genes
were centrally evaluated using blood sample and tumor biopsy collected at baseline. T41A
(34.3%) and S45F (18.6%) were the most frequently observed mutations. Similar frequencies for
mutations were observed in treatment arms and placebo arms. Among the 105 participants
with evaluable tumor samples (of 142 enrolled), 85 participants (81%) had a CTNNB1 mutation

Are there clinically relevant food‐drug or drug‐drug interactions,
and what is the appropriate management strategy?
The Applicant’s Position:
Due to strong dependency on CYP3A4 for elimination of nirogacestat, concomitant
administration with strong or moderate inhibitors of CYP3A4 should be avoided. If a strong or
106
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

moderate inhibitor of CYP3A4 must be prescribed, a patient should cease administration of
(b) (4)
nirogacestat for the duration of use of the interacting drug.
There is no expectation of clinical benefit
from nirogacestat if taken concomitantly with strong CYP3A4 inducers. Exposure to
nirogacestat is predicted to be greatly reduced when administered concomitantly with
moderate CYP3A4 inducers, but it is unknown if therapeutic benefit from nirogacestat could be
expected. The effects of P‐gp inhibition on nirogacestat were not evaluated in a clinical study,
however any clinical effect is believed to be less important than the effect of first‐pass
metabolism by CYP3A4.
Nirogacestat is a weak inhibitor of CYP3A4 with clinical usage. Concomitant usage of
nirogacestat with CYP3A4 substrates that have a narrow therapeutic window should be avoided
as nirogacestat may increase the exposure to a CYP3A4 substrate. Nirogacestat may cause
weak‐to‐moderate induction of CYP2C19 in some cases. In a clinical evaluation with dabigatran
etexilate, nirogacestat showed no meaningful effect on P‐gp inhibition.
The solubility of nirogacestat is substantially reduced at pH greater than 6.0, thus gastric acid
(b) (4)
reducing agents may reduce the bioavailability of nirogacestat.

In an evaluation of food effects on nirogacestat PK in Study A8641014, Cmax and AUCtau were
similar between fed and fasted states but carried significant variability. In Study NIR‐DT‐301,
nirogacestat was dosed without regard to food. Given the observed efficacy and safety
parameters from Study NIR‐DT‐301, nirogacestat is recommended for administration without
regard to food.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position regarding the labeling recommendation for the
concomitant use of nirogacestat with strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers and
drugs that are substrates of CYP3A4 and CYP2C19. However, FDA does not agree with the
Applicant’s position regarding the labeling recommendation for the concomitant use of
nirogacestat with gastric acid‐reducing agents (ARAs). FDA’s rationale and supportive data are
summarized below.
Effect of Concomitant Medications on Nirogacestat
a.

Strong and moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The effects of a strong CYP3A4 strong inhibitor increased AUClast, AUCinf and Cmax of a single
dose 100 mg of nirogacestat by approximately 6‐, 8‐ and 2.5‐fold, respectively.
The Applicant developed a PBPK model to predict the potential DDI of nirogacestat under
various scenarios following a single and multiple doses (100 mg or 150 mg) when co‐
administered with inhibitors of CYP3A4 in healthy subjects and patients. These simulations
included the effect of coadministration of strong inhibitors (itraconazole, ketoconazole and
clarithromycin), moderate inhibitors (fluconazole and erythromycin), and a weak inhibitor
(cimetidine). Nirogacestat AUC is predicted to increase by 3.5 to 6.3 fold following
coadministration of multiple doses of nirogacestat (150 mg BID) with strong inhibitors and
increase by 2.7 to 3.2 folds with moderate CYP3A inhibitors (Refer to the PBPK review in
appendix 19.4.4 for details).
Considering the magnitude of the increase in AUCs of nirogacestat when co‐administered with
strong and moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors and the positive relationship between nirogacestat
exposure and adverse reactions in the E‐R analysis, FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s
position that concomitant use with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors should be avoided.
(b) (4)
However, FDA does not agree with the proposed labeling language;
FDA revised the
labeling to avoid co‐administration of strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors or inducers with
nirogacestat.
b.

Strong and moderate CYP3A4 inducers

A PBPK model was used to predict the potential DDI between nirogacestat and a strong inducer
(rifampicin) or a moderate inducer (efavirenz) following a single dose and multiple doses (100
mg or 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects and patients. The AUC of nirogacestat was predicted to
decrease by 85% when co‐administered with rifampicin and by 67% when co‐administered with
efavirenz with multiple doses of nirogacestat at 150 mg BID (Refer to PBPK review in appendix
19.4.4 for details).
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position to avoid the coadministration of strong or
moderate CYP3A4 inducers with nirogacestat. However, FDA does not agree with the proposed

P‐gp inhibitors
108
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The effect of concomitant use of itraconazole (a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and a P‐gp inhibitor)
on nirogacestat PK was evaluated. The mean Cmax of a single dose 100 mg of nirogacestat
following multiple doses of itraconazole days was increased by approximately 2.5‐fold and
median Tmax was delayed from 1 to 1.5 hours. The mass balance study revealed the absence of
unchanged parent drug in feces after oral administration of 150 mg nirogacestat. Additionally,
the nirogacestat PK exhibited linearity across the dosage range of 1‐120 mg (SD) and 20‐220 mg
(BID) following oral administration. These findings indicated that the observed DDI with
itraconazole was most likely a result of nirogacestat mediated CYP3A4 inhibition rather than
the inhibition of P‐gp. FDA agrees with the applicant that a P‐gp inhibitor is unlikely to have a
clinically meaningful effect of nirogacestat exposure.

d.

Gastric pH‐altering agents

Nirogacestat demonstrates pH‐dependent solubility. The Applicant did not conduct clinical
studies to evaluate the effects of gastric acid‐reducing agents (ARAs), but the Applicant
evaluated the effect of ARAs in the population PK model; the effect was inconclusive due to the
low number of patients on ARAs. Therefore, a clinical pharmacology PMC is issued to evaluate
the impact of concomitant use of a proton pump inhibitor on nirogacestat PK. Proton pump
inhibitors were selected to best describe the worst‐case scenario as they are the ARA with the
strongest effect on pH relative to antacids and H2 receptor antagonists.
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s proposed recommendations. FDA recommends:


Avoiding concomitant use with proton pump inhibitors and H2 receptor antagonists.



Staggering administration of antacids (e.g., administer nirogacestat 2 hours before or 2
hours after antacid use).

Effect of Nirogacestat on Concomitant Medication
a.

Substrates of CYP3A4

Co‐administration of nirogacestat with oral midazolam, a sensitive probe CYP3A substrate,
increased midazolam plasma AUC by 59%, indicating that a nirogacestat‐is a weak CYP3A
inhibitor.
The PBPK model was used to predict the magnitude of interaction between nirogacestat and
midazolam following multiple doses of nirogacestat of 150 mg BID. The Cmax and AUC of
midazolam was predicted to increase by 1.8 and 2.1 folds, respectively, suggesting that
nirogacestat is a weak inhibitor of CYP3A4 at the proposed recommended dosage.

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Taken together, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s labeling proposal that concomitant use with
certain CYP3A4 substrates should be avoided where minimal concentration changes may lead
to serious adverse reactions.

b.

Substrates of CYP2B6, 2C8, 2C9 and 2C19

The Applicant did not conduct any clinical studies to evaluate the effects of nirogacestat‐
mediated induction of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19. No meaningful induction was
predicted for nirogacestat on substrates of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, or CYP2C9 based on in vitro data
and PBPK simulation. A moderate interaction was predicted for S‐mephenytoin (a sensitive
CYP2C19 substrate) when co‐administering with nirogacestat at 100 mg and 150 mg BID using a
10‐fold increase in maximum fold induction to assess a worst‐case scenario, although the in
vitro study indicated that the risk of DDI between nirogacestat and CYP2C19 substrates is low.
Therefore, the potential induction of nirogacestat on certain CYP2C19 substrates cannot be
ruled out.
Taken together, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s labeling proposal that concomitant use with
certain CYP2C19 substrates should be avoided where minimal concentration changes may lead
to significant decreases in efficacy.

c.

Substrates of P‐gp

The results of Study NIR‐DT‐103 showed that nirogacestat is unlikely to inhibit P‐gp. The Cmax
and AUC for total dabigatran was similar when administered alone (Cmax = 76 ng/mL and AUCinf
= 600 h*ng/mL) or with a single dose of nirogacestat at 150 mg (Cmax = 73 ng/mL and AUCinf =
587 h*ng/mL). Labeling states that exposure to dabigatran (P‐gp substrate) Cmax and AUC were
not affected by concomitant use with nirogacestat.

Food Effect
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that a high fat and high calorie meal had no clinical
meaningful effect on nirogacestat PK based on the results from the food‐effect sub‐study in the
EXPAND‐S phase of Study A8641014. Briefly, dose normalized mean Cmax (n=13) and AUCtau
(n=11) were similar in the fed (5.2 ng/mL and 22.5 ng*hr/mL, respectively) and fasted condition
(6.6 ng/mL and 23.7 ng*hr/mL, respectively) following 150 mg and 220 mg BID (Table 20).
Considering the magnitude of the change in nirogacestat exposure and the range of 90%

The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical studies summarized in Table 21 provide the totality of data to support an NDA
submission for nirogacestat in the treatment of adult patients with DT.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Table 21 accurately describes the 6 clinical trials relevant to this NDA. However, the Applicant
did not include Study NIR‐OGT‐201, which is investigating nirogacestat in patients with
recurrent ovarian granulosa cell tumors; as of October 23, 2022, 1 patient has been dosed in
this trial (source: Development Safety Update Report #12, reporting period October 24, 2021,
through October 23, 2022).
Not including patients who received nirogacestat at cross‐over in NIR‐DT‐301, a total of 95
patients with DT received nirogacestat (n=69 in NIR‐DT‐301, n=9 in Study A8641014, and n=17
in Study 14‐C‐0007).

8 STATISTICAL AND CLINICAL EVALUATION
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Studies A8641014, 14‐C‐0007, and NIR‐DT‐301 are the relevant studies used to support efficacy.

Study NIR‐DT‐301
Study Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study NIR‐DT‐301 was a randomized, double‐blind, placebo controlled, Phase 3 study to
compare the efficacy, safety, and tolerability of nirogacestat and placebo in adult participants
with RECIST v1.1 progressing DT. This study consisted of 2 phases: a DB phase and an optional
open‐label extension (OLE) phase. Nirogacestat was administered orally at a dose of 150 mg BID
continuously, in 28‐day cycles.
In the DB phase, participants were randomized (stratified by target tumor(s) location
[intra‐abdominal or extra‐abdominal]) to study treatment (nirogacestat 150 mg BID or placebo)
in a 1:1 ratio. Participants were stratified based on target lesion locations at Baseline
(intra‐abdominal versus extra‐abdominal) as participants with intra‐abdominal tumors have
been found to have a less favorable outcome as compared to extra‐abdominal tumors
(Howard & Pollock 2016). If the participant had multiple target tumors located in both the
116
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

intra‐ and extra‐abdominal locations, the tumor was classified as intra‐abdominal. Participants
remained in the DB phase until death, disease progression, discontinuation from study
treatment for any reason, or the estimated number of progression events were observed and
the primary PFS analysis was completed. The primary efficacy endpoint was PFS, which was
defined as the time from randomization until the date of assessment of progression or death by
any cause (whichever occurs first). Progression was determined radiographically by
independent, blinded Central Imaging Review using RECIST v1.1 on target tumors identified by
the investigator at Screening or clinically as assessed by the investigator and confirmed by
blinded independent central review.
Participants were eligible to enter the optional OLE phase if they were enrolled in the DB phase
when the primary PFS analysis was completed, or they met the criteria of PD as defined by
RECIST v1.1 (confirmed by central radiological review). During the OLE phase, all participants
received nirogacestat 150 mg BID and remained on study until death, radiographic PD per
RECIST v1.1, clinical progression, premature discontinuation, end of study, qualification for
Continued Access Plan, or nirogacestat becomes commercially available. The DB phase of the
study has been completed; however, the OLE phase is ongoing.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the description of the trial design for Study NIR‐DT‐301. FDA adds that patients
who experienced clinical progression were not eligible to enroll in the OLE phase of the trial,
and these patients were not unblinded. Patients who enrolled into the OLE phase were
unblinded. Cycle 1 Day 1 (C1D1) visit for the OLE phase was within 24 hours of the end of
treatment (EOT) visit for the DB phase of the trial, and assessments and determination of
causality of all ongoing AEs and SAEs that occurred during the DB phase were conducted during
the EOT visit prior to unblinding the patient. A longer window between the EOT and C1D1 visit
of OLE was allowed with medical monitor approval.
Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
Key inclusion criteria for the DB phase included:


Participants ≥ 18 years of age with histologically confirmed DT (by local pathologist prior
to informed consent) that progressed by ≥ 20% as measured by RECIST v1.1 within
12 months of the screening visit scan.



Were treatment naïve or recurrent or refractory to treatment with measurably
progressing DT.



Had ECOG performance status ≤ 2.
117
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)



Had a DT tumor where continued PD did not result in immediate significant risk to the
participant and had adequate organ and bone marrow function.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant highlighted the key inclusion criteria. The following are additional important
aspects of Study NIR‐DT‐301’s eligibility criteria:


Eligibility for enrollment was not dependent on central review of tumor specimens. The
patients were enrolled based on local diagnosis of DT, but tumor specimens were used
to reconfirm the diagnosis of DT by central review for all patients. It is appropriate that
the diagnosis of DT was reconfirmed by central review because it is a rare tumor for
which expert diagnosis is important.



Given that the clinical course of DT is heterogenous, and active surveillance is
recommended for patients with indolent and/or asymptomatic DT, it is appropriate for
Study NIR‐DT‐301 to enroll patients with RECIST 1.1‐defined progressing DT. FDA revised
the indication statement consistent with this eligibility criterion.



To be eligible for Study NIR‐DT‐301, patients who were receiving chronic nonsteroidal
anti‐inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) as treatment for conditions other than DT must have
been receiving them prior to the documentation of DT progression and have been on a
stable dose for at least 28 days prior to the first dose of nirogacestat. Patients who were
on any treatment (including NSAIDs) for DT must have completed this treatment at least
28 days (or 5 half‐lives, whichever is longer) prior to the first dose of nirogacestat. It is
unclear whether NSAIDs have anti‐tumor efficacy against DT (the data is based on case
series with very small sample sizes), but these eligibility criteria in Study NIR‐DT‐301 are
appropriate given NSAIDs are used to treat DT, and the possibility that NSAIDs may have
anti‐DT effects has not been ruled out.



Patients who had current or prior treatment with gamma secretase inhibitor or anti‐
Notch antibody, current or chronic history of hepatobiliary disease (except for Gilbert’s
syndrome, or gallstones), positive human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) test, positive
hepatitis B surface antigen, or positive hepatitis C antibody or RNA, were excluded.
Patients were also excluded with clinically significant heart disease or prolonged QTc
interval.

Study Efficacy Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
Primary


The primary efficacy endpoint was PFS, which was defined as the time from
randomization until the date of assessment of progression or death by any cause
(whichever occurs first). Progression was determined radiographically by independent,
blinded Central Imaging Review using RECIST v1.1 on target tumors identified by the
investigator at Screening or clinically as assessed by the investigator and confirmed by
blinded independent central review.

Secondary


ORR, defined as a confirmed BOR of CR or PR by RECIST v1.1 during the DB phase
 Duration of responses (in months), as supportive, descriptive analyses of ORR,
defined as the duration in months from the time measurement criteria are met
for CR or PR (whichever comes first) until the date of progression or death
(whichever comes first).



Change in PRO measures from baseline over time:
 GOunder/Desmoid Tumor Research Foundation Desmoid Symptom/Impact Scale
(GODDESS)
 Brief Pain Inventory (BPI) short form
 European Organization for Research and Treatment of Cancer (EORTC) Quality of
Life Questionnaire‐Core 30 (QOQ‐C30)

The FDA’s Assessment:
Amendment 4 (dated July 7, 2020) of the protocol for Study NIR‐DT‐301 added clinical
progression to the definition of PFS. During the review of this NDA, FDA inquired how the
Applicant determined which patients experienced clinical progression among the patients who
enrolled prior to implementation of this amendment. The Applicant responded that since the
initiation of Study NIR‐DT‐301, the electronic data capture system collected “clinical
progression” as a reason for the discontinuation of trial treatment, but there was not a
protocol‐specified criterion for “clinical progression.” A specific criterion was added in
Amendment 4, which defined events of clinical progression as follows:
when the investigator determines the participant needs to be withdrawn from the study
to start emergent treatment (i.e., radiotherapy, surgery, or systemic therapy including
chemotherapy and/or tyrosine kinase inhibitors) for DT/AF due to the development of
119
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

DT/AF related intractable pain, vital structure involvement, and/or progression of
symptoms resulting in a global deterioration of health status
However, Amendment 4 was submitted to FDA but not activated, and Amendment 5 (dated
February 9, 2021) was where the addition of clinical progression to the definition of PFS was
implemented. Clinical progression was defined as follows in Amendment 5:
the onset or worsening of symptoms resulting in a global deterioration of health status
causing the permanent discontinuation from study treatment and the initiation of
emergent treatment (e.g., radiotherapy, surgery, or systemic therapy including
chemotherapy or tyrosine kinase inhibitors) for DT/AF.
FDA inquired who experienced clinical progression (regardless of confirmation of clinical
progression by central review) prior to the implementation of the amendment. The Applicant
responded that all 9 investigator‐determined events of clinical progression (in this blinded trial)
were reported prior to the implementation of Protocol Amendment 5 (dated February 9, 2021),
and all were adjudicated by a blinded, independent, central clinical review (Source:
SpringWorks’s response to FDA Information Request in SDN 19, dated 3/23/2023).
Although SpringWorks changed the definition of the PFS endpoint to allow clinical progression
events, FDA communicated on multiple occasions (e.g., September 18, 2020, and January 22,
2021, meetings) concerns regarding the change in endpoint (also that inadvertent unblinding
due to toxicity could result in bias in the assessment of clinical progression). Ultimately, the FDA
review team remains concerned with the use of this endpoint for regulatory purposes.
Although FDA acknowledges that patients may clinically progress, the lack of objective
assessment (and noting the events are clinician adjudicated and not patient‐derived [i.e., via a
PRO]) may increase the risk of bias, precluding a definitive determination of a drug’s effect in
the clinical trial.
These concerns were overcome; however, in this nirogacestat application via the totality of the
following information:


Assessment of PFS results excluding clinical progression events (most events in Study
NIR‐DT‐301 were based on objective radiographic findings)



Assessment of ORR (tumor shrinkage) between arms



Assessment of patient reported outcomes

Finally, Study NIR‐DT‐301 did not evaluate OS as a primary efficacy endpoint. FDA did not object
to this approach because DT is rarely fatal and the existing safety data for nirogacestat
indicated that it would not be expected to lead to safety‐related deaths. Because DT can be

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

symptomatic however, treatment to induce delay progression, induce tumor shrinkage, and
improve symptoms including pain can be beneficial and warranted.
Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The original SAP (v1.0) was approved on 07 April 2022 and was amended on 12 May 2022
(v2.0).
The study sample size was based on the primary PFS endpoint. A total of 51 events was
estimated to provide 90% power and a 1‐sided type 1 error rate of 0.025 (1‐side hypothesis) to
detect a difference between nirogacestat and placebo, assuming the median PFS in the
nirogacestat arm was 20 months and 8 months in the placebo arm (corresponding to a HR of
0.4 relative to placebo). Assuming a 10% dropout rate and a 20% spontaneous regression rate,
118 participants needed to be randomized in a 1:1 ratio to observe the estimated number of
events.
While the study planned to randomize approximately 118 participants, all eligible participants
in Screening at the time of achieving the target enrollment (n = 118) were allowed to enroll,
resulting in a total of 142 participants randomized. A relatively high number of participants
were in Screening at the time of achieving the target enrollment rate of 118 participants due to
the impact of COVID‐19. Specifically, many institutions delayed study enrollment until summer
2020, resulting in a bolus of participants in Screening when the target enrollment rate was
achieved.
The intent‐to‐treat (ITT) population was the primary analysis population for the efficacy
analyses. The ITT population consisted of all participants who were enrolled and randomized to
study treatment (nirogacestat or placebo). Participants were analyzed according to the
treatment they were randomized to and the strata to which they were assigned. Participants
who were randomized but did not subsequently go on to receive study treatment were
included in the ITT population. The Safety population was the analysis population for the safety
analyses and consisted of all participants randomly assigned to study treatment who received
at least 1 dose of study treatment. Participants were analyzed according to the treatment they
actually received. The Per‐Protocol (PP) population consisted of those participants who
received study treatment and had no major protocol deviations; this analysis population was
used for sensitivity analyses.
The primary and secondary efficacy endpoints were tested in the following order: PFS, ORR,
disease symptoms, impact, and quality of life evaluations by PRO. The primary efficacy analysis
was performed after 49 events had been observed.
121
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

If the null hypothesis was rejected at the specified significance level, the testing could proceed
to the next endpoint, but if the null hypothesis was not rejected, all subsequent results were
considered descriptive only. All data collected after crossover to nirogacestat in the OLE phase
of the study will be analyzed and reported separately.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The major changes in SAP V2 were summarized below:
a) Clarified that the formal statistical testing on PRO‐related endpoints is conducted at the
1‐sided, 0.025 level of significance.
b) Supplementary analysis added where GODDESS Total Symptom Score is derived
excluding item 2 (dull pain) and item 3 (shooting pain).
c) Proportion of participants with improvement in BPI‐SF API score at Cycle 10 was
removed from the hierarchical testing of secondary endpoints and added as an
exploratory endpoint.
FDA acknowledges that the primary analysis for PFS was conducted at 49 PFS events, which was
earlier than the pre‐specified 51 PFS events due to the longer than expected follow up time.
According to clinical study report, no new events were reported during Sep 20, 2021, and April
07, 2022, and the study was approaching 3 years since the first patient enrolled. The Applicant
performed the primary analysis of PFS at 49 of 51 planned events. As the primary analysis of
PFS was strongly statistically significant and clinically meaningful, FDA concurs with the
reported PFS results.
Protocol Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The original global study protocol was finalized on 03 August 2018 and amended 5 times; the
final protocol (Protocol Amendment 5) was dated 09 February 2021. None of the amendments
impacted the integrity of quality of the study and/or its outcomes.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA outlines the key protocol amendments below. All the amendments were activated except
for Amendment 4. Some of the amendments were made to clarify PRO assessments and
analyses. The change to include the clinical progression events could have impacted the
outcome of the trial; however, this was addressed in the analyses and interpretation of the data
below (and in the discussion of endpoints above). Additionally, although additional information
was collected following Amendment 3 regarding ovarian toxicity, this information was not
sufficient to fully characterize ovarian toxicity and resolution.

Amendment 1 (dated November 27, 2018)
1. The Applicant revised PRO assessments:


To capture concepts important to patients with DT, the Applicant proposed to replace
the MD Anderson Symptom Inventory (MDASI) with the EORTC QLQ‐C30.



To supplement the DTIS tool, the Applicant added Patient‐Reported Outcomes
Measurement Information System Physical Function (PROMIS PF) short form 10a and 3
items from the PROMIS item banks.

2. To describe the localization of disease more precisely, the Applicant changed randomization
stratification factor by tumor location from “favorable versus (vs.) nonfavorable” to “intra‐
abdominal vs. extra‐abdominal,” where intra‐abdominal includes mesentery and pelvis.
Amendment 2 (dated October 14, 2019)
1. Because DTs can spontaneously regress, the Applicant increased the sample size from 105
to 135 screened patients and from 94 to 118 randomized patients to account for a
projected spontaneous regression rate of 20%.
2. The Applicant clarified inclusion criterion #3 pertaining to DT (added bold font, and deleted
font with strikethrough):
Participant has:
a. Treatment naïve Newly diagnosed, measurably progressing DT/AF that is deemed
not amenable to surgical resection or radiation therapy surgery without the risk of
significant morbidity;
OR
b. Recurrent, measurably progressing DT/AF following at least one line of therapy
CR to initial therapy;
OR
c. Preexisting Refractory, measurably progressing DT/AF and has previously
received therapy following at least one line of therapy. and the residual tumor has
progressed.”
3. The Applicant updated MSK/DTRF DTSS and DTIS to GOunder/Desmoid Tumor Research
Tumor Foundation (DTRF) DEsmoid Symptom/Impact Scale (GODDESS) to reflect the new
name of the PRO
Amendment 3 (dated January 27, 2020)
1. Because of reports of primary ovarian dysfunction, the Applicant included a requirement to
ask women about their menstrual history and added monitoring of hormone levels in men
and women in the Schedule of Activities. The hormones to be monitored were thyroid
stimulating hormone (TSH), prolactin, anti‐mullerian hormone (AMH), luteinizing hormone

Amendment 4 (dated July 7, 2020) – not activated
1. The Applicant added clinical progression into the definition of PFS definition as described in
“Study Efficacy Endpoints” in Section 8.1.2 of this NDA review.
2. To minimize the potential for bias in the assessment of clinical progression, the Applicant
added that patients who discontinue due to clinical progression are not eligible for
participation in the optional OLE phase.
3. The Applicant added that patients who discontinue treatment due to clinical progression
will be unblinded at the EOT visit for the DB phase to help inform future treatment
decisions.
4. The Applicant added that patients who discontinue due to reasons other than disease
progression will not be unblinded and will not be eligible for participation in the optional
OLE phase of the trial.
Amendment 5 (February 9, 2021)
1. The Applicant revised the definition of clinical progression as described in “Study Efficacy
Endpoints” in Section 8.1.2 of this NDA review. See comments above in FDA discussion of
endpoints regarding the addition of clinical progression events.
2. The Applicant clarified that only patients who discontinue due to radiographic progression
will be eligible for unblinding and participation in the OLE.
3. To minimize the potential for bias in the assessment of clinical progression, the Applicant
changed the protocol to state that patients who discontinue treatment due to clinical
progression will NOT be unblinded.

Study Results
Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
Data
This study was conducted in accordance with the protocol and the consensus ethical principles
derived from international guidelines including the Declaration of Helsinki and Council for
International Organizations of Medical Sciences International Ethical Guidelines, applicable ICH
GCP guidelines, and all applicable local laws and regulations.

The Applicant’s Position:

the clinical investigators. However, FDA inspection revealed that four investigators had financial
arrangements, none of which were disclosed by the Applicant. See Section 4.1 for more details.
Patient Disposition
Data
The data cutoff and database freeze dates for this CSR were 07 April 2022 and 17 May 2022,
respectively. Only assessments conducted through 07 April 2022 were analyzed for the primary
analysis.
A summary of the disposition of all participants enrolled is presented in Table 22. A total of
142 participants were randomized, with 141 participants receiving study treatment
(nirogacestat or placebo) in the DB phase. The majority of randomized participants had
extra‐abdominal (109 participants [77%]) versus intra‐abdominal (33 participants [23%])
tumors.
As of the data cutoff, 59 participants were ongoing in the DB phase and 47 participants
discontinued study treatment due to progression events meeting the study endpoint. In the
table below, progression events meeting the study endpoint reflected the total number of
events resulting in the discontinuation of study treatment, not in the analysis of PFS. In the
placebo arm, 1 participant discontinued study treatment due to an AE prior to death, which
counted as an event in the PFS analysis. Another placebo participant discontinued due to other

A total of 35 participants prematurely discontinued DB treatment. The most common reasons

At the time of the data cut‐off date, 32 of 40 eligible participants enrolled in the OLE phase of

count of participants eligible for the OLE phase.

The Applicant’s Position:
Participants by randomized strata were balanced between treatment arms. A higher proportion
of participants in the nirogacestat arm than in the placebo arm were ongoing at the time of the
data cut, largely due to a lower proportion of participants with events meeting the study
endpoint in the nirogacestat arm compared with the placebo arm, including participants with
radiographic PD.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the data presented in Table 22. Most of the ITT population received at least 1
dose of trial treatment (99% vs. 100% in nirogacestat, and placebo arms, respectively). FDA
agrees with the Applicant’s statement that the randomized strata (intra‐abdominal vs. extra‐
abdominal tumor) were well‐balanced between the arms, which is important given that

Protocol Violations/Deviations
Data:
A total of 38 participants (54%) in the nirogacestat arm and 47 participants (65%) in the placebo
arm had at least 1 CSR‐reportable protocol deviation. At least 1 major CSR‐reportable protocol
deviation occurred in 4 participants (6%) in the nirogacestat arm and 4 participant (6%) in the
placebo arm. These major CSR‐reportable protocol deviations included mis‐stratification based
on incorrect tumor location and enrollment without meeting inclusion/exclusion criteria.
The Applicant’s Position:
A CSR‐reportable protocol deviation is considered a significant deviation that may impact the
accuracy, and/or reliability of the study data or participants rights, safety, or well‐being.
A major CSR‐reportable protocol deviation would exclude the participant from the PP
population and include participants that did not meet inclusion/exclusion criteria,
129
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

mis‐randomizations, mis‐stratifications, or may impact accuracy and reliability of the study
data.
The 8 participants with major CSR‐reportable protocol deviations excluded from the PP
population were not expected to impact the data integrity for the analysis, which was
confirmed by the sensitivity analysis of PFS comparing the ITT population (HR = 0.29
[95% CI: 0.15, 0.55]; p < 0.001) versus the PP population (HR = 0.26 [95% CI: 0.13, 0.53];
p < 0.001).
The FDA’s Assessment:
Table 25 outlines the protocol deviations in Study NIR‐DT‐301, including the details regarding
the major deviations. Based on the protocol deviations dataset (ADDV), most patients had
protocol deviations, and 8 patients (6%) had major protocol deviations.
In addition, FDA noted that five patients underwent concomitant excisions of DTs and one
patient underwent concomitant radiotherapy of a bleeding DT (see “Treatment Compliance,
Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use” in Section 8.1.2); 3 patients were in the
nirogacestat arm, and 3 patients were in the placebo arm. These events were not reported as
protocol deviations, and the protocol for Study NIR‐DT‐301 states that potentially curative
radiotherapy, and surgical resection of DT were prohibited concomitant procedures. It is
possible that these procedures were not reported as protocol deviations because the excisions
were not curative, i.e., the patients likely had other remaining DT lesions that were not excised,
and the radiotherapy was not considered therapeutic. However, these procedures still have the
potential to effect efficacy outcomes, particularly imaging‐based outcomes such as PFS and
ORR.
For FDA’s sensitivity analysis of PFS comparing the ITT population to the population of patients
who did not have major protocol deviations, FDA excluded the five patients who had excisions
of DT, and the one patient who underwent radiotherapy from the PP population (i.e., revised
PP population). The PFS HR was 0.25 [95% CI: (0.12, 0.52)] with median PFS was not reached for
the nirogacestat and 15.4 months for the placebo arm in the revised PP population. FDA agrees
with the Applicant that the presence of these protocol deviations did not impact the
interpretation of the PFS results.

premature unblinding was not a protocol deviation. FDA’s OSI did not observe issues related to
accidental unblinding during the inspection of the three clinical sites and the Applicant.

Table of Demographic Characteristics

A summary of the demographic characteristics of the ITT population is presented in Table 26.

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, baseline characteristics were similar between treatment groups. The study randomized
142 participants, of which 65% were female, which is consistent with the overall DT population
that is more commonly reported in females (Anneberg et al. 2022; Skubitz 2017;
de Camargo et al. 2010; Reitamo et al. 1982). Given the eligibility requirement for ≥ 20%
disease progression per RECIST v1.1, the study selected a participant population with more
severe disease than previously reported in the literature. This is evidenced by the high rate of
multi‐focal disease (40%) as compared to prior studies (~10%) (Peng et al. 2012), relatively large
median Baseline target tumor(s) size (92 mm), and high rate of prior treatment (74%) including
133
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

a median of at least 2 prior lines of therapy with the majority of participants being considered
refractory or recurrent following prior treatments.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The trial population is adequately representative of the general population of patients with DT
in that the ratio of female to male is approximately 2:1, and the average age of the patients was
in the mid‐30s, which is the age of peak incidence for DT. The trial population’s baseline
characteristics were generally well‐balanced across the arms, except for the numerical
differences in race and ethnicity. DT does not have a known predilection for a certain race or
ethnicity, so the slight imbalance in the distribution of race and ethnicity in Study NIR‐DT‐301
does not impact the integrity of the trial. However, because DT is not known to have a
predilection for a certain races or ethnicities, it may have been feasible to enroll more racial
and ethnic minorities into this trial to better represent the U.S. population, particularly when
most of the patients were enrolled in the U.S. (n=90, 63%). For example, in a cohort study of
patients with DT across three academic centers in the U.S. (over a 28‐year period), 69% were
White, 23% were Black, and 9% were reported as “Other race” (Peng et al., 2012).
Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
Data
A summary of disease characteristics in the ITT population is presented in Table 27. A higher
rate of multifocal disease was observed in this study as compared with prior studies in DT
(40% vs ~10% observed in prior studies with DT) (Peng et al. 2012). The majority of participants
had received prior treatment for DT and were classified as refractory or recurrent, 69% and 8%,
respectively; the treatment arms were well‐balanced.
Genetic results were determined per central review of tumor tissue (somatic) and blood
(germline) mutations. The majority (81%) of participants who were evaluable for somatic
mutations had somatic mutations in CTNNB1. A slightly higher percentage of evaluable
participants reported mutations in APC (21% versus ~10%) than typically reported in the
literature (Riedel & Agulnik 2022; Kasper et al. 2011). A total of 15 participants reported
germline mutations of APC, which were consistent with their tumors. Twenty‐four participants
had a family history of FAP including 12 with confirmed APC mutations (Table 27). The
remaining 12 participants with FAP did not have germline mutations identified (n = 2) or did not
have samples available for germline analysis (n = 10) .
The median Baseline target tumor size per RECIST was 100.35 mm, with a range from 19.9 to
400.9 mm with a slightly larger target tumor size of 115.7 mm in the placebo arm as compared
to the nirogacestat arm 91.6 mm.
