NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The Applicant’s Position:
Disease characteristics were generally balanced between treatment arms and consistent with
the disease under study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
There were small numerical differences regarding tumor characteristics across the treatment
arms (Table 28, and Table 29). Slightly more patients in the placebo arm had protocol‐defined
refractory disease, larger number and size of target lesions based on RECIST 1.1, and more prior
therapy (specifically, NSAIDs, tamoxifen, and surgery). However, slightly more patients in the
nirogacestat arm had recurrent disease and larger tumor volumes based on MRI. Patients in the
placebo arm had shorter time between diagnosis and first treatment, including first systemic
treatment, but patients in the nirogacestat arm had shorter time between last treatment and
randomization. Patients in both arms had similar time between diagnosis and randomization.
Thus, the disease severity across the two arms appears generally balanced across the two arms.

The genetic characteristics were similar across the treatment arms (Table 29). Most patients
(n=110, 77%) in the ITT population had a mutation in CTNNB1 (n=85, 60%) or APC (n=29, 20%).
Two additional patients had a somatic mutation in a different gene (data not presented in
dataset). FDA agrees with the Applicant in that the proportion of patients with the APC
mutation is higher compared to the general population (approximately 10%) although we were
not able to confirm the specific numbers described in “data” above (FDA’s analysis is shown in
the table below). All detected CTNNB1 mutations were somatic, and 76% (n=22) of all detected
APC mutations were somatic. The proportion of patients with germline APC (n=21, 15%) in this
trial is slightly higher than the proportion in the general DT population (approximately 5‐10%)
(de Camargo et al., 2010; Skubitz et al., 2017). The majority, but not all, of the patients with

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Data
Study treatment exposure is presented in Table 30. Median duration of study treatment
exposure was 20.57 months in the nirogacestat arm, which was nearly double that observed in
the placebo arm (11.40 months) likely due to the greater number of events observed in the
placebo arm. The majority of participants receiving nirogacestat (65%) remained on study
treatment for more than 13 cycles, suggesting sustained tolerability over an extended period of
treatment. The total duration of exposure was 99.18 participant‐years for the nirogacestat arm
and 79.42 participant‐years for the placebo arm.
A total of 139 participants (99%) received any concomitant medication, including
69 participants (100%) in the nirogacestat arm and 70 participants (97%) in the placebo arm.
The most frequently reported (≥ 30% overall) Anatomical Therapeutic Chemical (ATC)
classifications were anilides (67 participants [48%]), other viral vaccines (50 participants [35%]),
serotonin antagonists (48 participants [34%]), antipropulsives (47 participants [33%]), natural
opium alkaloids (47 participants [33%]), and propionic acid derivatives (44 participants [31%]).
A total of 55 participants (39%) had any concomitant procedures, including 33 participants
(48%) in the nirogacestat arm and 22 participants (31%) in the placebo arm. Overall,
concomitant procedures by system organ class (SOC) were surgical and medical procedures
(42 participants [30%]) and investigations (33 participants [23%]).

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The Applicant’s Position:
The median duration of study treatment exposure in the nirogacestat arm was nearly twice that
of the placebo arm, likely due to the greater number of events observed in the placebo arm.
Consistent with the protocol, which mandated a dose reduction to 100 mg BID for the
management of select, persistent Grade ≥ 3 AEs (diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, skin toxicity,
hypophosphatemia, hematologic/nonhematologic toxicities) and was optional for Grade 2 OD,
42% of participants receiving nirogacestat had dose reductions. The median duration of study
drug exposure after reduction was 19.06 months. The majority of participants receiving
nirogacestat (65%) remained on study treatment for more than 13 cycles, suggesting sustained
tolerability over an extended period of treatment.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s statement that patients in the nirogacestat arm had longer
treatment exposure than the patients in the placebo arm, likely due to fewer progression
events in the nirogacestat arm. Patients in the nirogacestat arm had lower dose intensity (both
actual and relative), likely due to dose reductions and interruptions being more frequent in the
nirogacestat arm. Most patients in the nirogacestat arm had dose modifications (65%), with
dose interruptions (65%) being more common than dose reductions (42%). TEAEs were the
most common reasons for dose modifications in both treatment arms.
FDA conducted additional analysis to investigate the time to first dose modification and total
time of dose interruption (Table 31). Patients in the nirogacestat arm interrupted dosing earlier
than patients in the placebo arm (mean 4.5 cycles vs. 6.3 cycles, respectively). Patients in the
nirogacestat arm had their first dose reduction at approximately the same time as their first
dose interruption, i.e., mean of 4.9 cycles; no patients in the placebo arm had a dose reduction.
The total duration of dose interruption was similar across the two arms (mean 22.3 vs. 26.7
days for nirogacestat vs. placebo arm, respectively). Note, FDA sent an Information Request to
the Applicant to clarify why the sample size for “time to first dose interruption” and “total time
interrupted” were different for the nirogacestat arm (see Table 31). The Applicant responded
(b) (6)
that one patient who had a dose interruption due to an adverse event (ID
in the
nirogacestat arm) discontinued treatment, so this patient was not counted in the “total time
interrupted” because the patient did not restart nirogacestat.
In conclusion, even though the patients in the nirogacestat arm tolerated long exposure to
nirogacestat, the earlier and more frequent dose modifications suggest that many patients
were not able to tolerate nirogacestat 150 mg PO BID.

Most patients in Study NIR‐DT‐301 received concomitant medications (100% and 97% of the
safety population in the nirogacestat and placebo arms respectively). FDA analyzed the
common indications for concomitant medications (see table below). The most common
indication for concomitant medications was pain, and tumor pain occurred more frequently in
the placebo arm (46%) than the nirogacestat arm (33%). Diarrhea, nausea, rash or dermatitis,
hypophosphatemia, and headache occurred more frequently in the nirogacestat arm, likely due
to the adverse reactions associated with nirogacestat. Patients in the nirogacestat arm were
also more likely to have received concomitant medications for infections, both COVID‐19 and
non‐COVID‐19 related, than patients in the placebo arm. FDA analysis of treatment‐emergent
adverse events (TEAEs) also demonstrated that patients in the nirogacestat arm had more
TEAEs in the System, Organ, Class (SOC) of Infections and Infestations than patients in the
placebo arm, 57% (n=39) vs. 33% (n=24), respectively. However, the rate of serious TEAEs in the
SOC of Infections and Infestations were similar across the two arms: 6% (n=4) in nirogacestat
and 7% (n=5) in placebo arms. The serious infections in the nirogacestat arm were appendicitis,
infected cyst, abdominal infection and abscess, and groin abscess (n=1 for each serious
infection). The serious infections in the placebo arm were COVID‐19 (n=2), sepsis (n=2), and
143
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

infection, and sepsis (n=1).
Analysis of the common indications for use of concomitant medications suggest that
nirogacestat has anti‐tumor efficacy that may lead to decreased pain due to DT but has
associated adverse reactions that require medical management. Patients in the nirogacestat
arm appeared to require more treatment for infections compared to patients in the placebo
arm, but it is reassuring that the rate of serious infections requiring treatment was similar
across both arms.

FDA also analyzed the use of concomitant medications that have potential anti‐DT effects (e.g.,
based on literature), or whose mechanism of action affects the reproductive hormone levels
(see table below). Patients in the nirogacestat arm were less likely to receive medications with
potential (reported) anti‐DT efficacy, so it is unlikely that use of these concomitant medications
detracts from the anti‐DT efficacy demonstrated by nirogacestat in Study NIR‐DT‐301. The
concomitant medications whose mechanism of action affects the reproductive hormones were
estrogen‐ and progesterone‐analogues, and clomiphene, none of which are known to have anti‐
DT efficacy. See Section 8.1.2 for the efficacy subgroup analysis for patients who received these
concomitant medications.

Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
A summary of PFS from randomization is presented in Table 35. A Kaplan‐Meier plot of PFS
from randomization is presented in Figure 2.

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The number of PFS events were lower in nirogacestat compared to placebo, leading to
significantly higher PFS in the nirogacestat arm (12 participants [17%] with an event) versus the
placebo arm (37 participants [51%] with an event), demonstrating a 71% reduction in the risk of
disease progression or death (HR = 0.29 [95% CI: 0.15, 0.55]; p < 0.001).
Assessment of qualified clinical progression events were included in the analysis of PFS.
Radiographic progression and qualified clinical progression each occurred at a lower rate in the
nirogacestat arm versus the placebo arm (16% vs 42% and 1% vs 8%, respectively). For both
treatment arms, the majority of participants with a progression event had radiographic
progression (11 of 12 participants in the nirogacestat arm; 30 of 36 participants in the placebo
arm). A sensitivity analysis excluding events of qualified clinical progression found results
consistent with the primary analysis and supported the inclusion of these events in the analysis
of PFS (HR = 0.31 [95% CI: 0.15, 0.62]; p < 0.001).

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The Applicant’s Position:
A statistically and clinically significant improvement in the primary endpoint of PFS was
observed for nirogacestat over placebo, with a 71% reduction in the risk of disease progression
or death (HR = 0.29 [95% CI: 0.15, 0.55]; p < 0.001). The probability of being event free was
higher in the nirogacestat arm compared to the placebo arm at Months 6 (91.8% vs 69.8%),
12 (84.7% vs 52.8%), and 24 (76.4% vs 44.0%), supporting a sustained clinical benefit over time.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that nirogacestat led to a statistically and clinically
significant improvement in PFS in patients with DT compared to placebo, where PFS included
both clinical and radiographic progression as assessed by blinded independent review. The
median PFS was non‐evaluable (NE, 95% CI: NE, NE) for patients on nirogacestat, and 15.1
months (95% CI: 8.4, NE) for patients on placebo, with a hazard ratio of 0.29 (95% CI: 0.15, 0.55;
p‐value <0.001). Due to the discrepancy identified by the OSI (see Section 4.1), an updated
figure of Kaplan‐Meier plot of PFS is presented below. Only a censored date for PFS in 1 patient
was affected. Therefore, the PFS HR and medians in arms remained the same, and the Kaplan‐
Meier plot of PFS is generally consistent with the original plot reported by the Applicant.
Figure 3:

analyzed (Figure 4).

assess for bias (in the determination of clinical progression) because the committee was not
necessarily reviewing clinical data for all patients in the trial in a real‐time, continuous basis.

Since clinical progression is a more subjective endpoint than radiographic progression, it was
reassuring that nirogacestat also demonstrated improved PFS based on radiographic
assessment alone compared to placebo, with a median PFS of NE (95% CI: NE, NE), and NE (95%
CI: 11.0, NE), with a hazard ratio of 0.31 (95% CI: 0.16, 0.62). This sensitivity analysis in
combination with ORR results and PRO analyses (see section below for Efficacy Results –
Secondary and other relevant endpoints and section for Efficacy Results – Secondary or
exploratory COA [PRO] endpoints) provided confidence that nirogacestat provides benefit to
patients, overcoming the weakness of the primary endpoint in Study NIR‐DT‐301. Note that
although the determinations of clinical progression below appeared reasonable, information
was not provided (particularly for patients in the experimental arm) regarding whether there
was bias in delaying a determination for a clinical progression event.

Data Quality and Integrity
Data:
All participant data relating to the study was entered into the electronic CRFs unless
transmitted to the sponsor or designee electronically (e.g., laboratory data).
The investigator was responsible for verifying that data entries were accurate and correct by
electronically signing the CRF. The investigator maintained accurate documentation
(source data) that supported the information entered in the CRF. The investigator permitted
study‐related monitoring, audits, Institutional Review Board (IRB) / Independent Ethics
Committee (IEC) review, and regulatory agency inspections and provided direct access to
source data documents.
Monitoring details describing strategy methods, responsibilities, and requirements, including
handling of noncompliance issues and monitoring techniques (on‐site, remote, or central
monitoring) were indicated in the monitoring plan to ensure the protocol and GCP was
followed.

independently of the study monitors. The audits within the clinical program were aimed at
study documentation, investigator sites, and CSRs.
The audit program, together with SpringWorks’ internal quality control procedures, provide
reassurance that study conclusions were based on valid procedures for data management and
analysis, and that the clinical study program was carried out in accordance with GCP guidelines.

Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
Data
A summary of ORR is presented in Table 37. ORR was calculated based on the confirmed best
overall response per central review. ORR was significantly higher (p < 0.001) in the nirogacestat
arm (29 participants [41%]) versus the placebo arm (6 participants [8%]). The ORR in the
placebo arm (8%) was lower than the previously reported (20%) in another Phase 3 study of
sorafenib in participants with DT (Gounder et al. 2018), which likely reflected the eligibility
requirement for RECIST v1.1 progression in the participants in this study.
The complete response (CR) rate was higher in the nirogacestat arm (5 participants [7%]) versus
the placebo arm (no participants) (Table 37). The disease control rate (DCR) was similar
between the treatment arms (91% vs 85%), which was due to the higher rate of stable disease
in the placebo arm.

The Applicant’s Position:
The ORR was significantly higher (p < 0.001) in the nirogacestat arm (29 participants [41%])
versus the placebo arm (6 participants [8%]), including 5 (7%) participants with a CR in the
nirogacestat arm versus no participants with a CR in the placebo arm. The DCR was similar
between the treatment arms (91% vs 85%), which may have been due to the higher rate of
stable disease in the placebo arm. Median time to objective response was shorter for
participants receiving nirogacestat as compared to placebo at 5.55 and 11.14 months,
respectively.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Nirogacestat has direct anti‐DT effect based on the
difference in ORR between the two treatment arms. FDA also agrees that the difference in ORR
between in the placebo arms of Study NIR‐DT‐301 and the trial conducted by Gounder et al. is
likely due to the latter trial having a less restrictive criteria for radiographic progression for
enrollment (i.e., ≥10% progression), and permitted enrollment of patients who did not have
radiographic progression but had DT that was deemed inoperable or would require extensive
surgery, or had symptomatic disease.
FDA also notes that the 95% CI for ORR and DCR in nirogacestat arm are different from FDA’s
analyses. FDA used Clopper‐Pearson Method, an exact method, to calculate the 95% CI for
response rate. The 95% CI of ORR in nirogacestat is (29.8, 53.8), and the 95% CI of DCR in
nirogacestat is (82.3, 96.8) from FDA’s analyses. FDA’s calculation of 95% CI for both ORR and
DCR in the placebo arm are the same as the numbers reported by the Applicant. Since the DCR
was not pre‐specified in the hierarchical testing plan, FDA considers the reported p‐value for
DCR was nominal.
Dose/Dose Response
The Applicant’s Position:
All participants were dosed at 150 mg BID; therefore, this is not applicable for this submission.
The FDA’s Assessment:
See Section 6 of this review.

Durability of Response
Data:
Median duration of response (DOR) for participants with CR or partial response (PR) was not

was not reached.
The Applicant’s Position:
The Kaplan‐Meier median DOR and median DoSD could not be estimated for either treatment
arm due to the low number of responses with subsequent progressions or deaths on either
arm.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The median DOR was not reached for both arms,
while the range of the DOR is (2.8, 27.7+) months in nirogacestat and (2.8, 16.4+) months in the
placebo.
Persistence of Effect
Data:
Refer to Table 35 and Figure 2.
The Applicant’s Position:
Refer to the primary endpoint position.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The anti‐DT effect of nirogacestat appears durable based on the probability of patients not
having a progression event at 2 years from initiation of nirogacestat (76% [95% CI: 61, 87])
compared to placebo (44% [95% CI: 32, 56]) and the difference in median PFS between
nirogacestat (15 months [95% CI: 8.4, not evaluable]) and placebo (not evaluable) (Table 35 and
Figure 4).

Mixed Model Repeat Measures: Change from Baseline in PRO Assessments (ITT Population)

161
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the
positions of the FDA.

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The Applicant’s Position:
Clinically meaningful and statistically significant improvements were observed with nirogacestat
treatment for all PRO assessments evaluated as part of the secondary endpoints at the
prespecified timepoint of Cycle 10. Statistically and clinically significant improvements occurred
as early as Cycle 2 and were generally sustained throughout the DB phase (Osoba et al. 1998;
Mease et al. 2011). Overall, results from the PRO analyses demonstrated improved health‐
related quality of life (HRQoL) with nirogacestat as compared to placebo. These findings are
notable in DT which has been associated with substantial morbidity including pain and impaired
physical functioning (Constantinidou et al. 2012; Quintini et al. 2012; Penel et al. 2017; Skubitz
2017; Gounder et al. 2020; Schut et al. 2022). Improvements in patient‐reported symptoms and
impacts have been specifically cited by the Desmoid Tumor Working Group (DTWG) as a
treatment goal in their consensus‐based guidelines on the management of DT (DTWG 2020)
and highlight the benefit of nirogacestat for patients. The early and consistent benefits
observed across patient‐reported symptoms and impacts contribute to the robust radiographic
findings and demonstrate a clear benefit for patients.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The GODDESS (DTSS and DTIS), BPI‐SF and EORTC QLQ‐C30 instruments were used to collect
PROs as secondary endpoints. The average pain intensity (API) score was derived using the
average of the daily BPI‐SF Item 3 score (“Worst Pain”) over the 7‐day period prior to each visit
with range from 0 to 10, where higher scores indicate greater pain intensity. The DTSS TSS was
calculated as the mean of the daily average TSS over a 7‐day period if at least 4 out of 7 days
were non‐missing. The daily average TSS was calculated by averaging the three pain items
(items 1‐3) first and then averaging with items 4‐7. Higher scores indicate greater symptom
severity. DTIS Physical Functioning domain was calculated by averaging items 1, 2, 6, 7, and 8
with a 7‐day recall period. Higher scores indicate worse physical functioning. The EORTC QLQ‐
C30 Physical Functioning, Role Functioning, and GHS/QoL domain scores were scored according
to the EORTC scoring instructions (i.e., the average of the items that contribute to each scale
taken as the raw score). Raw scores are linearly transformed to a 0‐100 scale, where higher
scores indicate a higher (”better”) level of functioning or quality of life. PRO endpoints (mean
change from baseline at cycle 10) were formally tested in the following order: BPI‐SF API, DTSS,
DTIS, EORTC QLQ‐C30 GHS/QoL domain, EORTC QLQ‐C30 Physical Functioning domain, and
EORTC QLQ‐C30 Role Functioning domain.
The PRO results reported by the Applicant in the CSR and in the section above were calculated
using all data from baseline to cycle 25, which was different from the pre‐specified analysis
method as described in the SAP. In response to an FDA information request, the Applicant
provided corrected analyses on April 21, 2023, redoing the PRO analyses based on data from

In general, FDA concluded that the results from the pre‐specified PRO endpoints were
challenging to interpret for the following reasons:
1. There were issues with comprehensibility of the DTSS and DTIS:
A definitive conclusion could not be made whether the DTSS and DTIS measures were
well‐understood and interpreted appropriately due to the limited approach used by the
Applicant to evaluate participant comprehension. Specifically, the Applicant did not:


query participants about understanding and appropriateness of response
options for all DTSS and DTIS items, but rather at time of first appearance of
the response option type (i.e., queried participants only on DTSS Item 1 and DTIS
item 1)



consistently query participants about recall period across instructions and items.

2. There was substantial amount of missing data and differential missing data between
treatment arms which makes it difficult to reliably interpret the PRO results in terms of
efficacy:
The available data rates (number of PRO completed/ITT population) at baseline and
cycle 10 among PRO endpoints are summarized in Table 39 below. Figure 6 also shows
the available data rate and reasons for data missingness over time using EORTC QLQ‐

Using EORTC QLQ‐C30 as an example: Available data rates were high at baseline for
both nirogacestat and placebo arms; however, less than half of randomized patients
were included in the analysis at cycle 10 (Table 39). The available data rate remained
above 70% at Cycle 2 and Cycle 3, dropped to 50% from cycle 4 to cycle 7, and

differential at cycle 10. The high missing data and differential missing rate may not allow

mean change from baseline at cycle 10.

Evaluation of BPI‐SF API
Although available data rates for BPI‐SF at cycle 10 were low, FDA noted higher available data
rates for BPI‐SF at cycle 2 and cycle 3 (> 80%). Also, because of the issues outlined above with
the DTSS and DTIS, FDA performed additional descriptive analyses on the BPI results. FDA
performed additional analyses on change from baseline at cycle 2 and change from baseline at
cycle 3 on BPI‐SF API to further evaluate the pain improvement for patients in the study. For
patients who reported pain at baseline with API >=2 score, the pain improvement at cycle 2 was
2.7‐points and 1‐point in the nirogacestat and placebo arms, respectively. Similarly, for the
same group of patients, pain improved by 3.5‐points in the nirogacestat arm and 1.2‐points in
the placebo arm at cycle 3.
Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
Data:
Analysis of exploratory efficacy endpoints showed the following:


Mean tumor volumes were larger in the nirogacestat arm (421.166 mL vs 371.999 mL) at
Baseline as compared to the placebo arm. There were significantly greater decreases in
tumor volume in the nirogacestat arm than in the placebo arm at Cycles 7, 13, 19, 25,
and 31 (p < 0.003), and numerically smaller tumor volume at EOT (p = 0.065).



Mean MRI T2 hyperintensity was numerically more reduced from Baseline in the
nirogacestat arm than the placebo arm at Cycles 7, 13, 25, and 31, and at EOT. A total of
23 participants receiving nirogacestat reported a change in T2 hyperintensity from
> 90% to < 90%, which has been shown to be prognostic in participants with DT (Cassidy
et al. 2020).



Efficacy analyses were performed within the nirogacestat arm for participants who had
a dose reduction to 100 mg BID versus those who did not have a dose reduction. There
was no notable difference in PFS and ORR between participants who dose reduced
versus those who did not. PRO analyses of BPI, DTSS, DTIS, and EORTC showed no
apparent loss of efficacy with a dose reduction to 100 mg BID.
 The genetics exploratory endpoint of frequency and distribution of each
mutation (APC and CTNNB1 genes) showed that among the 105 participants with
evaluable tumor samples, 85 participants (81%) had a CTNNB1 mutation and
22 participants (21%) had an APC mutation. Mutation frequencies were similar
between treatment arms and generally consistent with the expected disease
etiology although a slightly higher percentage of participants had APC mutations
166
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

than previously reported in the literature (Kasper et al. 2011; Riedel & Agulnik
2022). There were no apparent correlations between identified genetic
mutations and PFS, suggesting that nirogacestat is an effective treatment for DT
regardless of genetic mutation.


Exploratory analyses of PRO endpoints supported the clinically meaningful benefit of
nirogacestat.

The Applicant’s Position:
Given the limitations of RECIST in DT, other imaging modalities were evaluated as exploratory
endpoints including volumetric MRI and T2 hyperintensity. These supportive analyses
demonstrated a greater reduction in tumor volume and T2 hyperintensity among participants
receiving nirogacestat as compared to placebo and may represent a more precise measure of
tumor changes in DT as compared to RECIST v1.1 (Kummar et al. 2017). Volumetric MRI may
also allow an earlier estimate of disease progression than RECIST v1.1 (Lefkowitz 2015;
DTWG 2020; Subhawong et al. 2021). Data from the volumetric MRI and T2 hypersensitivity
analyses provide additional radiographic evidence supporting the benefit of nirogacestat in the
treatment of DT as compared to placebo.
Exploratory analyses did not show differences in PFS, ORR, or PRO endpoints between
participants who dose reduced to 100 mg BID versus those who did not have a dose reduction.
The FDA’s Assessment:
No difference in efficacy was detected between the subgroup of patients who reduced the dose
of nirogacestat to 100 mg BID and the subgroup who did not have a dose reduction; however,
the analysis of PFS considering dose reduction should be interpreted with caution because
there is not a comparable group in the control arm (and, more importantly, the analyses would
have been a post randomization analysis). Note that the p‐values reported for exploratory
endpoints in this section were considered nominal.
FDA interpretation of the statement regarding PRO analyses (in Data above) is that these
analyses provided support for the primary assessment of PFS and ORR. The PRO analyses,
however, have limitations as described in FDA’s review above.

Study A8641014
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study A8641014 was an open‐label, non‐randomized, Phase 1 dose‐finding study (Messersmith
et al. 2015) in participants with advanced solid tumors conducted to determine the MTD and
167
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

recommended Phase 2 dose (RP2D) for the future clinical development of nirogacestat. In the
dose‐finding portion of the study, the MTD of nirogacestat administered BID continuously for
21 days was established at 220 mg BID in participants with advanced solid tumors. Additional
participants were subsequently enrolled in the expansion cohort at 150 mg or 220 mg BID. The
RP2D in participants with advanced solid tumors, including DT, was determined to be 150 mg
BID by comparing the tolerability, PK, and PDx profile of nirogacestat at these 2 doses.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Study A8641014 was a first‐in‐patient, multicenter, open‐label, non‐randomized, dose‐finding
Phase 1a/1b trial that employed a 3+3 dose‐escalation design. The primary objective in the
original protocol was to determine the MTD of nirogacestat BID as a single agent or in
combination with dexamethasone in patients with advanced solid tumors. In the Phase 1b
portion of the trial, the primary objective was to determine the safety and tolerability of
nirogacestat at the MTD in patients with advanced solid tumors and acute T‐cell lymphoblastic
leukemia/lymphoblastic lymphoma (T‐ALL/LBL). Amendment 6 (dated October 26, 2011) of the
protocol removed the plan to investigate nirogacestat with dexamethasone from both the
dose‐escalation and dose‐expansion portions of the trial because after analysis of the data of
nirogacestat as a single‐agent, the Applicant decided that the addition dexamethasone was not
necessary for patients with advanced solid tumors.
Eight patients with T‐ALL/LBL and 64 patients with advanced solid tumors received nirogacestat
on this trial. Based on the results of the Phase 1a portion of the trial, the Applicant determined
that the MTD of nirogacestat was 220 mg BID for patients with advanced tumors and
investigated nirogacestat 150 mg BID and 220 mg BID in the Phase 1b portion of the trial. The
Applicant determined that there were more treatment‐related Grade 3 TEAEs with the 220 mg
BID dose compared to the 150 mg BID and chose nirogacestat 150 mg BID as the RP2D for
patients with advanced solid tumors. The MTD was not determined for patients with T‐ALL/LBL
because dose escalation was terminated early due to low enrollment rate and early data
suggesting that the proof‐of‐mechanism was unlikely to be achieved in T‐ALL/LBL.
Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
Key inclusion criteria included:


Men and women ≥ 16 years old.



Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) performance status of:
 0 or 1 for participants with advanced solid tumor.
 0, 1, or 2 for participants with refractory or relapsed T‐ALL.



Participants with advanced solid tumor malignancy (with measurable disease as per



Participant was > 3 weeks (advanced solid tumor malignancy) or > 2 weeks (T‐ALL/LBL)
from last anticancer treatment (except hydroxyurea, and intrathecal prophylaxis in
T‐ALL population) at study entry and all acute adverse events (AEs) of any prior therapy
had resolved or, if persisting, were not considered to be clinically important.

The FDA’s Assessment:
Patients with T‐ALL/LBL must have had refractory disease, disease that was resistant to current
treatment options, or had no standard therapy available. Patients who previously had their
cancer treated with a gamma secretase inhibitor were not eligible. Patients taking tamoxifen
were excluded. Patients with the following diseases were also excluded: clinical evidence of
CNS disease, active hepatitis B infection, hepatitis C infection, or known HIV or acquired
immunodeficiency syndrome (AIDS)‐related illness.
Study Efficacy Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
Primary:
No efficacy endpoint was defined as primary endpoint in this study.
Secondary:


Objective response (OR) was defined as confirmed CR or confirmed PR according to
RECIST (version 1.0).



Time to tumor progression (TTP) was defined as the time from Cycle 1 Day 1 to date of
first documentation of objective progression.



DOR was only defined for participants with an OR. It was defined as the time from start
date (date of first documentation of PR or CR) to date of first documentation of
objective progression or death



PFS was defined as the time from Cycle 1 Day 1 to date of first documentation of
progression or death due to any cause.



Time to response (TTR), defined as the time from first date of receiving study treatment
until the first OR date for participants whose best overall response is a CR or PR.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA would like to clarify that evaluation of TTP, TTR, and PFS were included as a secondary
endpoints in the protocol for Study A8641014. However, time‐to‐event endpoints are not

interpretable in nonrandomized trials, and the primary objective of this trial was to determine

Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The final statistical analysis plan (SAP), Amendment 3, was dated 26 March 2012.
The number of participants to be enrolled in the study depended on the observed safety
profile, which was to determine the number of participants per dose level and the number of
dosing cohorts in the study. Each dose level in the dose escalating components was to enroll at
least 3, and up to 6 DLT eligible participants. Once the MTD for solid tumors had been
determined, approximately 22 additional participants with advanced solid tumor, including
participants with DT, soft tissue sarcoma, lung cancer, breast cancer and other solid tumors,
were enrolled to help determine RP2D.
All enrolled participants who were eligible, received study treatment, and had baseline
assessments and at least 1 on study tumor assessment prior to any new anticancer therapies
were considered evaluable for response. Participants who were treated and discontinued from
treatment prior to on study tumor assessment because of clinical disease progression were
considered evaluable for efficacy and counted as failures.
Due to the exploratory nature of the study, no confirmatory inferential analyses were planned.
Summary tables were produced by dose level. If there were sufficient numbers of participants
accrued in certain malignancies, the efficacy endpoints were also to be tabulated for those
malignancies separately. Descriptive statistics such as means, medians, standard deviations and
ranges for continuous data and frequencies and percentages for categorical data were
presented. Tumor response was tabulated and displayed graphically, where appropriate.
A point estimate of the ORR and a 2‐sided 95% confidence interval (CI) were calculated using an
exact method. Survival endpoints, i.e., TTP, TTR, duration of response, and PFS, were
summarized using the Kaplan‐Meier method, and the Brookmeyer Crowley method was used to
calculate the 95% CI for the median.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that this study should be considered exploratory and supportive. The primary
efficacy assessment supporting the approval of nirogacestat for patients with progressing DTs
was based on Study NIR‐DT‐301.
Protocol Amendments
170
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the

Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The Applicant’s Description:
The original protocol dated 15 December 2008 was amended 8 times. None of the amendments
impacted the integrity of quality of the study and/or its outcomes.
This study was originally designed to have 4 additional components: an expansion cohort in
participants with T‐ALL/LBL; an optional dose‐finding component in participants with solid
tumor for combinational treatment of nirogacestat plus dexamethasone; an optional
dose‐finding component in participants with T‐ALL/LBL for combinational treatment of
nirogacestat plus dexamethasone; and an optional expansion component in participants with
advanced solid tumor for combinational treatment of nirogacestat plus dexamethasone.
These components were later removed due to operational and scientific reasons. As agreed
upon at the Pre‐NDA meeting, participants with T‐ALL/LBL were excluded from the integrated
safety analyses as these hematologic malignancies were anticipated to be sufficiently different
from DT to preclude any comparisons regarding safety data.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA outlines below the key protocol amendments that pertained to the advanced solid tumor
cohorts. Thus, it excludes descriptions of Amendment 7 (dated February 2, 2012) and
Amendment 8 (dated May 14, 2012) whose revisions pertained to the T‐ALL/LBL cohorts,
including halting of enrollment into the T‐ALL/LBL cohorts in Amendment 8 due to low
enrollment rate, and early data suggesting that proof‐of‐concept mechanism unlikely to be
achieved for T‐ALL/LBL. The following amendments are also not described below because the
revisions were not substantive and did not lead to major changes to the trial conduct or trial
objectives: Amendment 2 (dated March 11, 2010), Amendment 4 (dated August 27, 2010), and
Amendment 9 (dated April 19, 2013). All the amendments were activated except for
Amendment 3; Amendment 4 was activated, and it was similar to Amendment 3. The original
protocol was dated December 15, 2008.
Amendment 1 (dated November 5, 2009)
1. The Applicant changed the dosing and schedule of dexamethasone.
2. The Applicant allowed enrollment of patients who were 16 years old (previously enrolled
only patients who were older than 16 years old).
Amendment 3 (dated August 16, 2010)
1. The Applicant amended the protocol to focus on characterizing the safety and tolerability of
nirogacestat as a single‐agent.
2. The Applicant added a food effect analysis for patients with solid tumors treated at the MTD
in the expansion cohort.

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Amendment 5 (dated May 2, 2011)
1. The Applicant increased the number of patients to be enrolled in the expansion cohort from
12 to approximately 22, and enrollment into this cohort was restricted to patients with
measurable disease based on RECIST.
2. The Applicant added collection of hair follicles and matched peripheral blood samples from
patients with solid tumor for biomarker evaluation.
Amendment 6 (dated October 26, 2011)
1. In the dose expansion cohort, the Applicant allowed enrollment of patients with solid
tumors to dose levels lower than the MTD.
2. The Applicant updated the list of tumors that may be enrolled into the dose expansion
cohorts.
3. The Applicant removed the plan to investigate nirogacestat with dexamethasone from both
the dose‐escalation and dose‐expansion portions of the trial because after analysis of the
data of nirogacestat as a single agent, the Applicant decided that the addition
dexamethasone was not necessary for patients with advanced solid tumors.

Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
Data:
This study was conducted in compliance with Good Clinical Practice (GCP) guidelines and,
where applicable, local country regulations relevant to the use of new therapeutic agents in the
countries of conduct, including the archiving of essential documents.

Study A8641014 was GCP compliant.

Section 12.2 of the protocol stated that the trial will be conducted “in accordance with the
Declaration of Helsinki on Ethical Principles for Medical Research Involving Human Subjects,
adopted by the General Assembly of the World Medical Association (1996). In addition, the
study will be conducted in accordance with the protocol, the International Conference on
Harmonisation guideline on Good Clinical Practice, and applicable local regulatory requirements
and laws.”

The Applicant’s Position:
All 64 participants assigned to treatment received treatment and all 64 are accounted for as
either ongoing or permanently discontinued. Data from all 64 participants are contained in the
safety analysis set.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify the results presented in this Assessment Aid by the Applicant
for early phase trials, such as Study A8641014, because the evaluation of efficacy of
nirogacestat for the treatment of patients with progressing DTs is based on the pivotal trial NIR‐
DT‐301.
Based on the information presented by the Applicant on Table 71, FDA agrees with the
Applicant’s position. Most patients received the nirogacestat 150 mg BID dose (n=23), followed
by 220 mg BID (n=16). The highest dose investigated was nirogacestat 330 mg BID (n=3). The
most common reason for discontinuing nirogacestat at all dose levels was objective progression
or relapse.
Among the 9 patients with DT, the reasons for discontinuation from treatment were withdrawal
by patient (n=3), adverse event (n=2), unqualified clinical progression (n=1), and other (n=3).

The FDA’s Assessment:
In addition to the protocol deviations the Applicant listed in Table 41, FDA considers the
following protocol deviations important because an increased frequency of these deviations
may impact the trial’s ability to achieve its objective of determining the MTD, and the
subsequent determination of the RP2D: patients were not adherent to documentation of
nirogacestat administration (n=11/72, 15%), patients missed one or more doses of nirogacestat
(n=7/72, 10%), tumor assessments were not conducted on one or more occasion (n=3/72,
4.2%), a trial clinical visit was not conducted (n=3/72, 4.2%), and end of treatment visit was not
conducted (n=1/72, 1.4%).
Data from Study A8641014 were adequate for FDA to review the analysis that led to the
Applicant’s determination that the MTD for nirogacestat was 220 mg BID. FDA agrees that the
MTD for nirogacestat appears to be 220 mg BID. Thus, the protocol deviations did not appear to
impact the ability of Study A8641014 to achieve its objective.

The Applicant’s Position:
The population in this study had a range of age, weight, and height that was appropriate for
evaluation of nirogacestat in a Phase 1 dose‐escalation study.

FDA did not independently verify the results presented in this Assessment Aid by the Applicant

DT‐301.
Based on the results presented by the Applicant on Table 42, among the patients with solid
tumors, the distribution of male and female patients was well‐balanced, the mean weight
reflected the U.S. population, and the spread of ages was appropriate for a first‐in‐patient trial.
This trial, like Study NIR‐DT‐301, enrolled mostly White patients, and the proportion of non‐
White patients did not reflect the diversity of the U.S. population.

The Applicant’s Position:
The study design included an expansion cohort that specified the inclusion of DT and soft tissue
sarcomas, among other solid tumor types. The demographics reflect this study design feature.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s Table 74 does not combine the diagnostic terms that may be a description of
similar cancers. For example, in Study A8641014, 12 patients had a diagnosis of colorectal
cancer (colon cancer [n=7], colorectal cancer [n=4], and rectal cancer [n=1]), 5 patients had a
diagnosis of lung cancer (lung neoplasm malignant [n=3], non‐small cell lung cancer [n=1], and
small cell lung cancer [n=1]), 3 patients had liver cancer (hepatic cancer metastatic [n=1], and
hepatic neoplasm malignant [n=2]), 3 patients had leiomyosarcoma (leiomyosarcoma at an
unspecified site [n=2] or uterine leiomyosarcoma [n=1]), and 2 patients had kidney cancer
(renal cancer [n=1] or renal cell carcinoma [n=1]). One patient each had a diagnosis of the
remaining unlisted cancers.
Among the patients with DT, 1 patient had a diagnosis of FAP. The APC and CTNNB1 status of
the tumors were unknown for all the patients. All 9 patients with DT had previous therapy:
radiotherapy (n=9), systemic therapy (n=7), and surgery (n=6). None of the patients had
previous treatment with a tyrosine kinase inhibitor, and 3 patients had prior treatment with
chemotherapy.
Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use

Data:
All participants received concomitant drug treatments during the study. The most commonly
reported concomitant drug treatments included antiemetics and antinauseants (for example,
prochlorperazine and ondansetron), anti‐diarrheal (for example, diphenoxylate hydrochloride
and atropine [Lomotil]) and analgesics (for example, oxycodone and ibuprofen).
A total of 9 solid tumor participants (1, 4, 3, and 1 in the 130 mg, 150 mg, 220 mg and 330 mg
BID cohorts, respectively) had planned dose reductions and 3 of them had reductions in the
first cycle. All of these dose reductions were caused by treatment‐related AEs, which were
Grades 1 to 3, not reported as SAEs, and resolved following temporary discontinuations or dose
reductions.
The Applicant’s Position:
Commonly reported concomitant drug treatments were appropriate supportive care for known
adverse reactions to nirogacestat. Treatment compliance was high, with dose reductions
caused by treatment‐related AEs as mandated by the protocol.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Patients with DT were treated in the following dose levels of nirogacestat (all BID dosing): 80
mg (n=2), 150 mg (n=2), 220 mg (n=3), and 330 mg (n=2). One patient each had a dose
reduction at the higher dose levels: 150 mg, 220 mg, and 330 mg. Patients with DT had a high
relative dose intensities and long treatment exposures with nirogacestat (Table 44 ).

Data Quality and Integrity
Data:
No issues were identified that could potentially impact data integrity, prevent an adequate
assessment of the data, and change the conclusions drawn.
The Applicant’s Position:
All study sites were initiated during a sponsor investigator meeting or a site visit by the sponsor
or designated representative. Each site was provided with an instructional manual to ensure
the use of standard terminology and the collection of accurate, consistent, complete, and
reliable data. Central laboratory services were used in this study for consistency.
The sponsor monitored the study through routine center visits. At these visits, study
procedures were reviewed, CRF data compared to original clinical records, data queries
resolved, and protocol deviations discussed with the investigator. Telephone and e‐mail contact
was maintained with the investigators between center visits. In addition, the overall study
conduct was subject to internal quality review by the sponsor.
Quality assurance audits were performed at 2 sites by the sponsor’s own independent quality
assurance group under the group’s discretion. These audits were conducted according to the
sponsor’s procedures and GCP guidelines.
The CSR was subject to quality control review in accordance with the sponsor’s standard
operating procedures (SOPs).

An independent third‐party data monitoring committee was not established for this study.
But an internal safety review team comprising physicians, safety specialists, and statisticians
was set up to review individual and summary data collected in the safety and clinical databases
periodically.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that Applicant has provided all the datasets and supporting metadata
documentation required to validate the results.
Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
Data:
There were 46 participants with solid tumors evaluable for response. Overall, the ORR (95% CI)
was 13.0% (4.9%, 26.3%) for participants with solid tumors and 71.4% (95% CI [29.0%, 96.3%])
in participants with DT (Table 45). Six participants had an OR: 1 CR was in a participant with
thyroid cancer and 5 out of 7 participants with DT had PRs. There were no other responders. As
of the time of data cutoff, all 6 responders were censored in the calculation of DoR. All 5 DT
responders had not progressed and were censored at the time of data cutoff, with 4 still on
study and 1 discontinued due to non‐compliance. The participant with thyroid cancer with a CR
later had recurrence of disease but was censored at the last disease assessment of CR due to
missed tumor assessments.
Median DoR for the 6 participants with ORs was not reached due to censoring, with a range of
1.7+ to 24.2+ months, at the time of data cutoff on 09 January 2013. Overall, the median (95%
CI) of TTR for the 5 responders with DT was 8.5 (2.9, 30.4) months (Table 45).
For median (95% CI) of both TTP and PFS, the Kaplan‐Meier estimates were
1.6 (1.4, 6.0) months for the 150 mg BID cohort and 1.5 (1.1, 4.2) months for the 220 mg BID
cohort. The probability (95% CI) of TTP at 6 months and of PFS at 12 months for the 150 mg BID
cohort was 33.6% (13.8%, 54.8%) and 26.8% (9.2%, 48.5%), respectively. The probability
(95% CI) of TTP at 6 months for the 220 mg BID cohort was 20.0% (3.1%, 47.5%) but the
probability of PFS at 12 months was not available for this dose level.

The Applicant’s Position:

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify the efficacy results because FDA considered Study NIR‐DT‐301
to be the primary basis for determining efficacy of nirogacestat. Responses were reported at all
dose levels that were tested in patients with DT; therefore, FDA cannot determine based on this
assessment in a limited number of patients which dose of nirogacestat is most promising for
patients with DT. Refer to the clinical review of pharmacology for a more thorough discussion
of the dose.

Dose/Dose Response
Data:
Table 45 shows the best overall response for participants with DT by dose group.
The Applicant’s Position:
Responses in DT were seen across all dose levels spanning 80 mg BID to 330 mg BID, and
therefore a conclusion on dose response cannot be drawn.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that there were responses at dose levels spanning 80
mg BID to 330 mg BID. However, given the small number of patients per dose cohort in patients
with DT, FDA cannot determine based on an assessment of this trial whether there is a dose‐
response relationship for patients with DT. Refer to clinical pharmacology review above for
additional details regarding FDA assessment of the dosing regimen.
Durability of Response
Data:
Median DOR for participants with ORs was not reached.
The Applicant’s Position:
Response was durable among participants with ORs.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position, but this conclusion is limited by the small sample size
of patients with DT in Study A8641014.
Persistence of Effect
Data:
The median (95% CI) PFS for participants with solid tumors was 1.6 (1.4, 4.2) months and the
probability (95% CI) of PFS at 12 months was 29.8% (17.1%, 43.7%). The median (95% CI) of TTP
for participants with solid tumors was 1.6 (1.4, 6.0) months for the 150 mg BID cohort and
1.5 (1.1, 4.2) months for the 220 mg BID cohort.
The Applicant’s Position:
No obvious trend was observed in the analysis of TTP and PFS.

Study 14‐C‐0007
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 14‐C‐0007 is an NCI‐sponsored, open‐label Phase 2 study conducted to determine the
ORR (CR + PR) of nirogacestat in participants with DT; no randomization or blinding was
performed. Nirogacestat was administered orally at a dose of 150 mg BID continuously in
21‐day cycles. This is an ongoing study; results are based on a data cutoff of 04 November 2020.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s Description.
Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
Key inclusion criteria included:


Participants ≥ 18 years of age with histologically confirmed DT confirmed by the
Laboratory of Pathology, NCI, that progressed after receiving ≥ 1 line of standard

186
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

treatment and that was not amenable to surgical resection or definitive radiation
therapy.


Had measurable disease by CT scan defined as those that could be accurately measured
in at least one dimension (longest diameter to be recorded) as ≥ 20 mm by chest x‐ray,
as ≥ 10 mm with CT scan, or ≥ 10 mm with calipers by clinical exam.



Had ECOG performance status ≤ 2.



Had a life expectancy > 3 months with normal organ and marrow function.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s Description but clarifies that patients must not have been
amenable to curative surgical resection to be eligible to enroll and must have had adequate
organ and marrow function (e.g., patients with anemia or mild renal dysfunction were eligible
to enroll if hemoglobin ≥9 g/dL, or creatinine clearance ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m2 if creatinine levels
>1.5 mg/dL, respectively). Patients who were previously treated with gamma secretase
inhibitors or anti‐Notch antibodies were not eligible to enroll. Patients with HIV who were being
treated with combination antiretroviral therapy were ineligible.
Study Efficacy Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
Primary:


The primary endpoint was the ORR, defined as the proportion of participants who have
objective response as CR or PR assessed by RECIST v1.1 using CT scans or MRI scans.

Secondary:


ORR and DCR by mutation type of APC and CTNNB1 genes.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA clarifies that Study 14‐C‐0007 did not have secondary endpoints, but did have exploratory
objectives, which included correlation of response to nirogacestat with genotype.
Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The original SAP, dated 08 December 2021, was amended once; the final version is dated 01
April 2022. As of the data cut, there were no changes between the protocol‐defined statistical
analyses and those presented in the SAP.

187
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

This study was conducted as a single stage Phase 2 study to rule out an unacceptably low 10%
clinical response rate (PR + CR; p0 = 0.10) in favor of a modestly high response rate of 35%
(p1 = 0.35). A type I error rate (alpha, probability of accepting a poor treatment) of 0.10 with a
response rate of 0.10 and a statistical power of 0.90 (beta = 0.10, probability of rejecting a good
treatment) when the true response rate was 0.35 was assumed, which justified a sample size of
17 participants. With 16 evaluable participants and all other assumptions the same, the
statistical power is still above 0.90.
The analysis of disease response was derived based on actual tumor measurements reported in
the eCRF. This analysis may have differed from the clinical review of RECIST, which was
performed by the NCI during the conduct of the study and used to make treatment decisions
on‐study.
ORR was presented along with frequency for best overall response (BOR) and for confirmed
BOR. The BOR was derived using tumor measurements per RECIST v1.1. BOR is defined as the
best response recorded from the start of the treatment until disease progression or recurrence,
in the order of CR, PR, stable disease (SD), not evaluable, and PD. The BOR was considered
confirmed if both the measurement and confirmation criteria were achieved. The overall ORR
was calculated using the confirmed BOR, which was based on the derived analysis of tumor
response.
ORR for mutation type APC and CTNNB1 genes were determined in the same manner as the
primary analysis.
Important analyses performed by Kummar et al (2017) such as change in target tumor size,
DOR, and DoSD were conducted for the data cut dates used in this interim CSR, for the purpose
of updating and confirming the results. At the time of the interim CSR data cut, tumor volume
data were not centrally databased through NCI, so this analysis was not performed for this
interim report.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify the results presented in this Assessment Aid by the Applicant
for early phase trials, such as Study 14‐C‐0007, because the evaluation of efficacy of
nirogacestat for the treatment of patients with progressing DTs is based on the pivotal trial NIR‐
DT‐301.

The study protocol was amended 12 times; the final protocol (Protocol Amendment 12) was
dated 10 April 2020. Consistent with NCI standard practice, the protocol was amended

There are 15 versions of Study 14‐C‐0007, Amendments A through O. The original protocol was



Amendment H (dated April 27, 2018) – NCI clarified that a treatment cycle is considered

“missing a small percentage of doses is unlikely to have a large effect on patients.”

for this trial are exploratory and changed them from secondary objectives to exploratory
objectives.

Amendment L (dated April 10, 2020) – added information regarding the risk of “primary
ovarian insufficiency in women of childbearing potential based on an IND/CIOMS safety
report from the randomized phase 3 trial of Nirogacestat”

Protocol Violations/Deviations
Data
Per the protocol, a deviation was defined as any change, divergence, or departure from the IRB
approved study procedures in a research protocol.
There were two cases of incorrect dosing of nirogacestat by the participants. One participant
was approved for a 1‐week delay to the start of cycle due to the participant’s work schedule.
The participant reported using “leftover pills” to dose with nirogacestat on three days during
the scheduled delay period. Another participant notified the research nurse that for the last
4 days of Cycle 5, the participant was out of town and did not take enough nirogacestat for the

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

duration of the trip. The participant took 100 mg nirogacestat BID instead of the prescribed
150 mg BID.
The Applicant’s Position:
The deviations were reviewed and GCP compliance was maintained. None of the observed
protocol deviations impacted the primary objective of the study and did not affect the overall
safety and efficacy outcomes of the study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that the protocol deviations do not affect the trial’s
ability to achieve its objective of determining the ORR in patients with DT. Based on the list of
protocol deviations provided by the Applicant for Study 14‐C‐0007, only one patient had a delay
in tumor imaging (one month delay not due to adverse events).
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the two dosing errors. In addition, there were 6
dosing delays, where 4 delays were due to adverse events, and 2 delays were not due to
adverse events. The 4 adverse events that led to dose delays were motor vehicle accident,
squamous cell carcinoma, swelling of clitoris, and bronchitis (n=1 per event). For the 2 dosing
delays not related to adverse events, one patient did not receive nirogacestat as planned, and
the other patient wanted to discuss with the Principal Investigator whether to continue
treatment with nirogacestat. One patient was not adherent or inconsistent self‐reporting of
nirogacestat administration and returning leftover nirogacestat supply. These are minor
protocol deviations and are unlikely to affect the trial’s ability to determine the efficacy, safety,
and tolerability of nirogacestat in patients with DT.
Table of Demographic Characteristics
Data:
Of the 17 participants, 3 (18%) were male and 14 (82%) were female. The median age at entry
was 34 years (range: 19 to 69 years). The majority (15 [88%]) of participants reported race as
white. A total of 9 participants [53%]) had a CTNNB1 T41A tumor mutation, 3 participants (18%)
had a CTNNB1 S45F tumor mutation, 2 participants (12%) had an APC germline tumor mutation,
and 1 participant had an APC somatic tumor mutation. There were no cases of more than one
mutation type reported per participant. Two participants (12%) reported no mutation types.
Twelve (71%) participants had a single tumor, and 5 participants (29%) had multi‐focal disease.
Focality was determined programmatically based on the target tumor and imaging modality
used for the evaluation for RECIST. The median time from diagnosis to start of nirogacestat was
89.2 months (range: 14.5 months to 223.3 months).

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

The Applicant’s Position:
Demographic characteristics were consistent with the general population with DT.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The distribution of female to male was modestly higher than the distribution in the general
population of patients with DT. The distribution of ages in this trial is reflective of the age
distribution of patients with DT. This trial, like Study NIR‐DT‐301 and Study A8641014, enrolled
mostly White patients (the remaining two patients were Black), and the proportion of non‐
White did not reflect the diversity of the U.S. population. The proportion of patients with
CTNBB1 mutation (71%) is consistent with what is found in the general population with DT.
Most patients had extra‐abdominal disease (82%).
All the patients had previous therapy: systemic therapy (100%), surgery (76%), and
radiotherapy (35%). Most patients received chemotherapy (82%), and the remainder of the
patients received “drug and/or immunotherapy.” Stable disease was the most common best
response to previous therapy (76%), and one patient (6%) had a partial response to previous
therapy.
Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Data:
All 17 participants received at least one dose of nirogacestat. As of this data cut, the median
duration of nirogacestat was 49.7 months (range: 2‐82). The majority (10 [59%]) of participants
were on nirogacestat for at least 3 years or longer, 2 participants (12%) were on nirogacestat
for at least 1 year but less than 2 years and 5 participants (29%) were on nirogacestat for less
than one year. The data are mutually exclusive and represent the longest time on study. The
median number of cycles completed was 13 (range: 0 – 102). A cycle was considered completed
if 90% of the planned dose was administered. The planned dose intensity was 300 mg/day. The
median dose was 243 mg, less than the prescribed dose, which was not unexpected in a study
of this duration given the number of dose modifications.
Fifteen participants had a total of 48 AEs leading to dose modifications. Four participants had
4 AEs that led to dose reductions (PTs included diarrhea, fatigue, memory impairment, and
weight decreased). All 4 participants had a confirmed BOR of SD and 3 of the 4 had sustained
(b) (6)
response with a lower dose of nirogacestat. Participant
experienced a > 20% increase
in tumor size from nadir and is shown as a PD in the derived response that occurred after the
dose was reduced.
Fifteen participants had 37 AEs that led to drug interruptions, 1 participant had 1 AE that led to
drug withdrawal (hypersensitivity), and 5 participants had 6 AEs that led to drug interruption

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

followed by dose reduction. The 5 participants with dose interruptions that led to dose
reductions were also counted in the drug interruptions total but were not counted in the total
for dose reductions. A total of 9 participants out of 17 (53%) had eventual dose reductions.
All participants received concomitant treatments during the study. The most commonly (> 50%)
reported ATC Class 3 of concomitant medications included: antipropulsives, antiemetics and
antinauseants, antiinflammatory and antirheumatic products (non‐steroids), other mineral
supplements and opioids.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat was well tolerated with a median (range) duration of treatment of 49.7 months
(2, 82). The most commonly reported concomitant medications were antipropulsives,
antiemetics and antinauseants, antiinflammatory and antirheumatic products (non‐steroids),
other mineral supplements and opioids to manage diarrhea, nausea, and pain.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of treatment adherence. The relative dose intensity
was high (median 96%, range 0.8‐263%; mean 86%, standard deviation 21%).
The AEs that led to dose interruption followed by dose reduction in the 5 patients were breast
tenderness, QT prolongation, hypocalcemia, myalgia, and rash maculopapular (one patient
each experienced these AEs except for rash, which was experienced by 2 patients). Only three
AEs that led to a dose modification were experienced by more than 1 patient: rash
maculopapular (n=3), hypophosphatemia (n=2), and nausea (n=2).
The most common indications for use of concomitant medications were pain (n=16, 94%),
diarrhea (n=14, 82%), nausea and prophylaxis of nausea (n=13, 76%), hypophosphatemia (n=12,
71%), infection (n=10, 59%), rash (n=10, 59%), and supplement (n=9, 53%).
The most common indications for the use of concomitant medications in Study 14‐C‐0007 were
similar to those of Study NIR‐DT‐301 and Study A8641014, and consistent with the effects of
the tumor (i.e., pain) or with the adverse reactions associated with nirogacestat, such as
diarrhea, nausea, hypophosphatemia, rash, and infection.
All the patients underwent concomitant procedures. Procedures that were performed on more
than one patient that were not imaging or electrocardiograms were biopsy (n=6), incision and
drainage (n=4), culture (n=2), and curative surgery (n=2; for non‐DT reasons). Three patients
underwent procedures due to DT (n=1 each for incision and drainage, surgery, and
hysterectomy). Because Study 14‐C‐0007 was not a randomized trial and had a small sample
size, the data from the concomitant procedure dataset did not contribute to our assessment to
the safety and efficacy of nirogacestat.

Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
Among the 17 participants included in the study, there were 16 participants evaluable for
response. One participant was not included in the efficacy analysis because the participant did
not have any tumor measurements taken after starting nirogacestat. The ORR (95% CI) was 31%
(11%‐58.7%). Five participants achieved a confirmed BOR of PR and 10 participants achieved a
confirmed BOR of SD by RECIST v1.1. One participant had tumor imaging performed post‐dose
and met the target lesion measurement criteria to be an SD; however, because tumor imaging
was assessed only 3 weeks after starting study drug, this participant was considered not
evaluable for BOR because a minimum of 4 weeks was required between scans to confirm a
response.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat was effective at sustained disease stabilization, as shown by the ORR of 31%, SD of
63%, and PD of 0%.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify the results presented in this Assessment Aid by the Applicant
for early phase trials, such as Study 14‐C‐0004, because the evaluation of efficacy of
nirogacestat for the treatment of patients with progressing DTs is based on the pivotal trial NIR‐
DT‐301.
Data Quality and Integrity
Data:
All data were collected in a timely manner and reviewed by the investigator or a lead associate
investigator. Any safety concerns, new information that affected either the ethical and or
scientific conduct of the study, or protocol deviations and violations were immediately reported
to the IRB using iRIS and to the sponsor.
The investigator reviewed AE and response data on each participant to ensure safety and data
accuracy. The investigator conducted or supervised the investigation and provided appropriate
delegation of responsibilities to other members of the research staff.
This study was monitored by personnel on contract to the NCI, NIH. Monitors were qualified by
training and experience to monitor the progress of this clinical study. Personnel monitoring this
study were not affiliated in any way with the study conduct.
At least 25% of enrolled participants were randomly selected and monitored at least biannually
or as needed, based on accrual rate. The participants selected had 100% source document

Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
Data:
ORR by Mutation Type
Of the 9 participants (53%) who had a CTNNB1 T41A tumor mutation, 3 had a BOR of PR and
5 had a BOR of SD; one additional participant was not evaluable for disease response. The
ORR (95% CI) for the CTNNB1 T41A somatic mutation was 33% (7.5%‐70.1%). The DCR (95% CI)
was 89% (51.8%‐99.7%).
Three participants had the CTNNB1 S45F tumor mutation, and all 3 had a BOR of SD. The
ORR (95% CI) for the CTNNB1 S45F somatic mutation was 0% (0.0%‐70.8%). The DCR (95% CI)
was 100% (29.2%‐100.0%).
Two participants had the APC A972G germline tumor mutation, and one participant had a BOR
of PR. One participant was not evaluable as the participant did not have any tumor
measurements taken. With only one evaluable participant, ORR and DCR were
100% (2.5%‐100.0%)
The one participant that had the APC somatic tumor mutation had a BOR of SD. With only one
evaluable participant, the ORR was 0% and the DCR was 100% (2.5%‐100.0%)
Two participants did not have any mutation type and one participant had a BOR of PR and one
had a BOR of SD. The ORR was 50% (1.3%‐98.7%) and the DCR was 100% (15.8%‐100.0%).
DOR
Among the 5 participants with a confirmed BOR of CR or PR, the median duration of response
was not reached due to censoring (range: 41.1 – Not evaluable). One participant died before

Duration of Disease Control
The DCR based on confirmed BOR (95% CI) was 94% (69.8%‐99.8%).
Among the 15 participants with a confirmed BOR of CR, PR, or SD, the median duration of
response was not reached due to censoring. One participant died before disease progression
and one participant, with a BOR of SD, experienced a > 20% increase in tumor size from nadir.
Four participants were censored due to no documented progression or death and 9 participants
went off study due to reasons other than disease progression.
The Applicant’s Position:
Clinical benefit was observed regardless of mutational status of CTNNB1 or APC genes
(Kummar et al. 2017). The DOR, DoSD, and DCR suggest that treatment with nirogacestat at
150 mg BID results in long‐term disease stabilization and support the continued evaluation of
nirogacestat for the treatment of DT.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify the results presented in this Assessment Aid by the Applicant
for early phase trials, such as Study 14‐C‐0007, because the evaluation of efficacy of
nirogacestat for the treatment of patients with progressing DTs is based on the pivotal trial NIR‐
DT‐301. In addition, the sample size per type of mutation is too small in Study 14‐C‐0007 for
FDA to draw any conclusions on effect of mutation on the efficacy of nirogacestat in patients
with DT.
Dose/Dose Response
The Applicant’s Position:
All participants were dosed at 150 mg BID; therefore, this is not applicable for this submission.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Nine patients underwent dose reduction, among whom 8 were evaluable for efficacy. Among
the 8 evaluable‐patients, one patient had a partial response, and the remaining patients had
stable disease (n=6) or were not evaluable (n=1). All 9 patients who did not undergo dose
reduction were evaluable for efficacy, among whom 4 patients had a partial response, and 4
patients had stable disease. The ORRs among patients who had dose reductions versus who did
not were 12.5% (95% CI: 0.003, 0.527), and 50% (95% CI: 0.16, 0.84), respectively. The
difference in ORR between these two subgroups suggests that there might be a dose‐response

Data:
Median DOR for participants with ORs was not reached.

Response was durable among participants with ORs.
The FDA’s Assessment:

presented by the Applicant for Study 14‐C‐0007 because the evaluation of efficacy of
nirogacestat is based on the pivotal trial NIR‐DT‐301.

Data:
Refer to efficacy results – secondary and other relevant endpoints above.

The study has demonstrated persistence of effect over time.
The FDA’s Assessment:

because the evaluation of efficacy of nirogacestat is based on the pivotal trial NIR‐DT‐301.
Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints
Data:
All participants were evaluable for PROs by using the MD Anderson Symptom Inventory.
Results from this analysis were not centrally databased for inclusion in the CSR; however, they
were published in Kummar et al. (2017). As described in this publication, partial responders had
a 1.65‐point improvement in mean symptom severity (p = 0.008). The five participants with
stable disease as their best response had a 0.8‐point improvement in mean symptom severity
(p = 0.08). Of the 7 participants whose treatment was discontinued or who withdrew from the
study, mean change in symptom severity was negligible.

Integrated Review of Effectiveness

Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
Primary Endpoints
Data
Integrated efficacy analyses are based on the Full Analysis Set (FAS). The FAS population
consisted of all participants in the DB phase of Study NIR‐DT‐301 who were randomized to
study treatment (nirogacestat or placebo) per the primary analysis data cut (07 April 2022), all
participants enrolled in Study 14‐C‐0007 per a 20 November 2020 data cut, and the
2 participants from Study A8641014 with DT who received nirogacestat at the 150 mg BID dose
level per a 09 January 2013 cutoff. The FAS population included 89 participants assigned to
nirogacestat (1 participant did not receive nirogacestat) and 72 participants assigned to
placebo.
In the Full Analysis Set (FAS) population, there was a statistically significant improvement in PFS
in the nirogacestat arm versus the placebo arm, with a 78% reduction in the risk of disease
progression or death (HR = 0.22; 95% CI: 0.11, 0.42; p < 0.001); this was consistent with the ITT
population in Study NIR DT‐301 (Section 8.1.1). Of the 13 (15%) participants in the nirogacestat
199
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

arm and 36 (50%) participants in the placebo arm who progressed, most were due to
radiographic progression (11 [12%] participants and 30 [42%] participants, respectively). One
participant in each arm died before PD.
The probability of being event‐free at Months 12 and 24 was higher in the nirogacestat arm
(88.0% and 82.2% respectively) than the placebo arm (52.8% and 44.0%, respectively)
supporting a sustained clinical benefit over time.
The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment with nirogacestat across studies resulted in a clinically meaningful and statistically
significant improvement in PFS compared with placebo.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Among the three trials used to support this NDA, only Study 14‐C‐0007 and Study NIR‐DT‐301
evaluated efficacy as a primary endpoint. The primary objective of the remaining trial, Study
A8641014, was to determine the MTD and RP2D of nirogacestat for further development.
Because Study NIR‐DT‐301 was the only randomized trial investigating nirogacestat, only the
data from this trial can be used to evaluate PFS since time‐to‐event endpoints such as PFS
cannot be evaluated in single‐arm trials. FDA does not agree with pooling data across these
three trials as they all had different research objectives and patients in the trials may have
different characteristics. In addition, pooling data from non‐randomized trials with data from
randomized trials is not an appropriate approach as it breaks randomization such that patients’
characteristics will be imbalanced between arms. This pooling approach will make results
difficult to interpret as the treatment effect estimates will be unreliable. Thus, FDA does not
agree with the Applicant’s pooled analysis for PFS.
Study 14‐C‐0007 was a single‐arm trial in 16‐evaluable patients with DT, with a reported ORR of
31% (95% CI: 11, 59) and the median DoR was not reached (range: 41 months ‐ NE).
Study NIR‐DT‐301 was a randomized trial in patients with DTs progressing based on RECIST 1.1,
and it demonstrated a statistically and clinically significant improvement in the primary
endpoint of PFS in patients treated with nirogacestat compared to placebo. The median PFS
was not reached (95% CI: NE, NE) for patients on nirogacestat, and 15.1 months (95% CI: 8.4,
NE) for patients on placebo, with a hazard ratio of 0.29 (95% CI: 0.15, 0.55; p‐value <0.001). This
PFS included both clinical and radiographic progression as assessed by blinded independent
review, and FDA agrees with the adjudication for clinical progression.
