NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Secondary and Other Endpoints
Data:
ORR
In the FAS population, the ORR was statistically significantly higher (p < 0.001) in the
nirogacestat arm (35 [40%] participants) versus the placebo arm (6 [8%] participants), including
5 (6%) participants with CR in the nirogacestat arm versus no (0%) participants in the placebo
arm. Results in the FAS population were similar to the ITT population of Study NIR‐DT‐301.
DOR
Similar to the Study NIR‐DT‐301 ITT population, the Kaplan‐Meier estimates of median DOR
were not reached for either treatment arm (Messersmith et al. 2015; Kummar et al. 2017).
DCR
In the FAS population, the DCR was similar between the nirogacestat and placebo arms
(93% and 85%; p = 0.088), respectively, which may have been due to the higher rate of SD in
the placebo arm. The DCR was similar between the Study NIR‐DT‐301 ITT population and the
FAS population.
DoSD
Similar to the NIR‐DT‐301 ITT population, the Kaplan‐Meier estimates of DoSD were not
reached for either treatment arm due to the low number of progressions or deaths in the FAS
population.
The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment with nirogacestat across studies resulted in a clinically meaningful and statistically
significant improvement in ORR compared with placebo. While data are limited, responses
appear durable. Other efficacy analyses including change in tumor volume as assessed by
volumetric MRI and change in T2 hypersensitivity and change in PRO measures demonstrate
improvements with nirogacestat, further supporting the primary efficacy analysis.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA does not agree with Applicant’s pooled analysis for endpoints including ORR, DOR, DCR,
and DoSD. See FDA’s comments above for Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials for the primary
endpoint of PFS for more details regarding issues with pooling data across the three trials.

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Subpopulations
Data
PFS
A statistically significant improvement in the primary endpoint of PFS in the nirogacestat arm
compared with the placebo arm (FAS population) was observed across most demographic;
disease characteristic; prior treatment; and genetic mutation, dose intensity, and AE subgroups.
While the upper boundary of the CIs for a few subgroups were > 1, in all instances this was due
to a small sample size.
ORR
The statistically significant improvement in ORR for nirogacestat over placebo with the FAS
population was maintained across most demographic, disease characteristics, prior treatments,
and genetic mutation, dose intensity, and AE subgroups.
DCR
In the FAS population, the DCR was similar between the nirogacestat and placebo arms across
most subgroups. Statistically significant improvements in DCR in the nirogacestat arm versus
the placebo arm were observed in participants with baseline tumor size ≥ 89.575 to
< 140.35 mm (p = 0.033), no history of FAP (p = 0.019), and participants who did not receive
prior radiation therapy (p = 0.049).
DoSD
Similar to the overall population, no conclusions regarding DoSD evaluated by subgroup can be
drawn due to the low number responses and subsequent events observed.
Exposure
Exposure was evaluated by subgroup; no notable differences between treatment arms were
observed.
The Applicant’s Position:
Results of subgroup analyses were consistent across studies, with statistically significant
improvements in PFS and ORR observed across most subgroups as compared to placebo.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA does not agree with Applicant’s presentation of subgroup analyses based on the pooled
data. See FDA’s comments above for Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials for the primary
endpoint of PFS for more details regarding issues with pooling data across the three trials.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Additional Efficacy Considerations
The FDA’s Assessment:
OS was not evaluated in the three trials submitted to support this NDA. FDA did not object to
this plan because DT is rarely fatal and the existing safety data for nirogacestat indicated that it
is not expected to lead to safety‐related deaths.

Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
Data:
Overall, analyses performed on the FAS population for the integrated summary of efficacy
yielded similar results to analyses of the ITT population performed for the pivotal NIR‐DT‐301
Study.


In the FAS population, participant disposition, demographics, and baseline
characteristics in the nirogacestat arm were similar to those in the placebo group. The
nirogacestat arm had a lower proportion of participants with events meeting the study
endpoint than the placebo arm (14% vs 49%, respectively) and a higher proportion of
participants in the nirogacestat arm than in the placebo arm were ongoing at the time
of the data cut (48% vs 32%, respectively).



In the FAS population, there was a statistically significant improvement in PFS in the
nirogacestat arm versus the placebo arm, with a 78% reduction in the risk of disease
progression or death (HR = 0.22; 95% CI: 0.12, 0.43; p < 0.001); this was consistent with
the ITT population from Study NIR‐DT‐301. The ORR was significantly higher (p < 0.001)
in the nirogacestat arm (34 [40%] participants) versus the placebo arm (6 [8%]
participants), including 5 (6%) participants with CR in the nirogacestat arm versus no
(0%) participants in the placebo arm.

The Applicant’s Position:
In summary, treatment with nirogacestat resulted in statistically significant improvements in
PFS, ORR, and patient‐reported symptoms and impacts, demonstrating a clear and consistent
benefit in participants receiving nirogacestat as compared to placebo. The durability of the PFS
advantage over a time scale of up to 7 years, combined with substantial reduction in disease
progression, increase in response rate, and significant improvements in pain, DT symptom
burden, physical functioning, role functioning, and HRQoL, support a clinically meaningful
benefit conferred by nirogacestat. Ultimately, these data support the efficacy of nirogacestat in
the treatment of DT.

FDA does not agree with the analyses performed using pooled data as described by the

pooling data across the three trials. However, FDA agrees that in the pivotal Study NIR‐DT‐301,
nirogacestat led to an improvement in PFS, and ORR that that was durable and consistent
across subgroups, and pain improvement in those who experienced ≥2 BPI at baseline in
patients with DT.

Review of Safety
Data:
Refer to Section 8.2.4 for summaries of deaths, AEs, SAEs, AEs leading to discontinuations, and
other safety data.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat had a manageable and acceptable safety profile in participants with DT.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA agrees with the above statement; however, refer to Section 1 above and the
safety review below regarding additional considerations. For example, the FDA review team
recommended revising the indication statement to specify patients have progressing DT who
need systemic treatment as select patients may be appropriate for a watch‐and‐wait approach
or surgery. Given that nirogacestat can cause severe toxicities including diarrhea and given the
potential for ovarian toxicity and hepatotoxicity, patients will need close monitoring as
described in labeling and patients should be adequately consented (standard practice in
oncology) for the safe and effective use of nirogacestat.

Safety Review Approach
The Applicant’s Position:
The data pooling strategy for this SCS was agreed upon by the FDA at the Pre‐NDA meeting.
The studies are grouped into the following study pools depending on the study design,
participants, dosing regimen, and targeted underlying conditions:


All DT Pool (All DT Pool) to inform labeling (Studies NIR‐DT‐301, 14‐C‐0007, and
A8641014 [participants with DT only])



All Cancer Participants Treated with Monotherapy (All CM Pool) (All DT Pool, all
participants in A8641014, and Study A8641020)
204
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)



HVs Treated with Single Dose (HV Pool) (Studies A8641001, A8641008, NIR‐DT‐101,
NIR‐DT‐102 and NIR‐DT‐103)

The studies in the All DT Pool and All CM Pool were pooled to characterize the overall safety of
nirogacestat monotherapy in participants with DT and solid tumors including DT, respectively.
Five single dose HV studies (HV Pool), were summarized by active drug and by placebo or other
comparator. In addition, given that the OLE phase of Study NIR‐DT‐301 is still ongoing and the
data are considered interim, descriptive statistics from this phase of the study were provided;
however, this phase of the study was not pooled with other study data and is presented
separately in select outputs (OLE Group).
Data from 8 participants with T‐ALL/LBL in the A8641014 study and 3 completed studies
(A8641002, A8641016, and A8641019) with nirogacestat were excluded from the data pooling
for this summary of safety. As agreed upon at the Pre‐NDA meeting, participants with T‐ALL/LBL
were excluded from the integrated safety analyses as these hematologic malignancies were
anticipated to be sufficiently different from DT to preclude any comparisons regarding safety
data. In addition, given that Study A8641002 was the only study evaluating multi‐dose
administration of nirogacestat in a HV participant population, these data were summarized
separately from the single dose HV studies.
Note that there is overlap of the populations of the nirogacestat pools analyzed. The focus of
this Assessment Aid will be on the All DT Pool, with comparisons to the NIR‐DT‐301 data since
this is the only study that includes a placebo comparator arm.
The All DT Pool included 141 participants from the DB phase of Study NIR‐DT‐301
(69 participants exposed to nirogacestat and 72 participants exposed to placebo),
17 participants from Study 14‐C‐0007, and 9 participants from Study A8641014.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the safety population included in the
primary safety analyses. FDA clarifies that the FDA primary safety analysis was performed on
patients who were enrolled to NIR‐DT‐301 and received at least one dose of study treatment
(N= 141) including 69 patients who received nirogacestat and 72 patients who received placebo
in the double‐blind portion of the trial. Verbatim descriptions of AEs were mapped to the
Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) version 24.0 for AEs. The severity of AEs
and laboratory abnormalities were graded according to CTCAE v5.0. The safety monitoring
period was from the time informed consent was signed until 30 days after the last treatment
dose. The data cutoff for the primary safety analysis was April 7, 2022. A safety update was
provided on March 27, 2023, which consisted of data from the final database lock (June 30,
2022) for NIR‐DT‐301 double‐blind portion and data for the ongoing NIR‐DT‐301 open‐label
extension (OLE) portion (October 24, 2022, interim data cut) and ongoing Study 14‐C‐0007

Overall Exposure
Data:
Key aspects of exposure and compliance for participants receiving nirogacestat 150 mg BID are

and the All DT Pool, it was shorter in the All CM Pool. The relative dose intensity for

both the mean and median values.

Relevant Characteristics of the Safety Population:
Data:
The demographics and other characteristics of the groups receiving nirogacestat 150 mg BID in

Anneberg et al. 2022).

groups, considering the small number of participants in the nirogacestat treatment groups
other than 150 mg BID. The dose groups other than 150 mg BID included no participants
≥ 46 years of age, or from parts of the world other than North America.

Baseline ECOG

a Baseline was defined as the most recent measurement prior to the first administration of study treatment.
b Baseline Body Mass Index (BMI) was calculated as: Baseline weight in kg/(Baseline Height in m)2.

The Applicant’s Position:
In general, the participants in Study NIR‐DT‐301 and the All DT Pool were representative of the
DT population, with a female predominance of 2 to 3‐fold. Participants in the nirogacestat
150 mg BID group of All CM Pool were older than the participants in NIR‐DT‐301 and the All DT
Pool, which was expected given the different indications under study and the fact that DT
typically occurs in a younger population as compared to the other solid tumors evaluated.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment regarding the primary safety analysis. FDA clarifies
that the FDA primary safety analysis was performed on patients who were enrolled to NIR‐DT‐
301 and received at least one dose of study treatment (N= 141) including 69 receiving
nirogacestat and 72 receiving placebo in the double‐blind portion of the trial.
With respect to applicability of the Study NIR‐DT‐301 population to the US population, the
median age (33.5 years in nirogacestat‐treated patients), increased prevalence in women,
appears consistent with DT in the US. There were insufficient numbers of patients to assess
treatment effects among non‐White patients and among older patients with DT.

Adequacy of the Safety Database:
Data
Key aspects of exposure and compliance for participants receiving nirogacestat 150 mg BID are
shown in Table 49.
The Applicant’s Position:
The evaluation of safety is based on data from Study NIR‐DT‐301 and the nirogacestat 150 mg
BID dose level in the All DT Pool. These populations allow for an informed assessment of the
safety profile of nirogacestat 150 mg BID and the overall risk‐benefit in patients with DT.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of exposure and compliance for patients receiving
nirogacestat 150 mg BID. FDA clarifies that the FDA primary safety analysis was performed on
patients who were enrolled to NIR‐DT‐301 which provides an informed assessment of the
safety profile of nirogacestat 150 mg BID in patients with DT. The size and exposure of the
overall safety data set is reasonable in this rare patient population to provide an estimate of
common adverse reactions over time.

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
The Applicant’s Position:
No meaningful concerns are anticipated in the quality and integrity of the submitted datasets
and individual case narratives; these were sufficiently complete to allow for a thorough review
of safety. Furthermore, no data integrity concerns were reported following completion of site
inspections; data in the CRFs and AE databases were consistent.
The FDA’s Assessment:
During review of the Application, the OSI audit noted that the Applicant provided corrected and
updated datasets that affected up to 16 tables and six figures for laboratory summary and shift
tables, and one table for concomitant medications. These updated tables and figures were
received on May 5, 2023. In addition, incomplete information to assess the PRO data which led
to Information Requests for compliance and completion rates, and updated datasets were
received from the Applicant on April 21, 2023, May 5, 2023, May 30, 2023, and on June 7, 2023.
Refer to Section 4 of this review (Office of Scientific Investigation) for further details.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Categorization of Adverse Event
The Applicant’s Position:
Safety analyses included drug exposure and compliance, AEs, clinical laboratory and
electrocardiogram (ECG) parameters, ECOG Performance Status, and vital signs.
All AEs were coded using the MedDRA coding system (MedDRA Version 24) and displayed in
tables and data listings using SOC and PT. Analyses of AEs were performed for those events that
were considered treatment‐emergent adverse events (TEAEs), where treatment‐emergent is
defined per protocol as any AE with initial onset or increasing in severity after the first dose of
study treatment through 30 days after the last dose of study treatment. To facilitate a better
understanding of specific topics of interest that may have comprised multiple PTs, select TEAEs
were evaluated by pre‐defined categories.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s categorization of adverse events. The Applicant
provided appropriate definitions and reporting requirements for AEs and serious AEs (SAEs). AE
evaluation was performed at the time of informed consent up to 30 days after the last dose of
study treatment. In the ADAE dataset, there were 2912 individual listed AEs of which 128 were
not treatment emergent (88 from the primary treatment phase and 40 from the open‐label
extension phase). Of the 88 AEs in the primary treatment phase, all but 29 occurred prior to the
start date. Of these 29, 9 events [7 nirogacestat and 2 placebo] did not have a start date and
the rest occurred 37 or more days after the start date. None of the 9 events without a start
date were serious and no AE term among these 9 occurred more than once. Overall the TEAE
definition used by the sponsor appeared acceptable.
During the course of Study NIR‐DT‐301, Patients reporting AEs/AESIs/SAEs associated with
ovarian dysfunction (amenorrhea, ovarian failure, or premature menopause) had hormone
levels assessed every 3 months until event resolution (or for at least 90 days after discontinuing
study treatment) since Amendment 3 dated January 27, 2020. FDA analyses were conducted
irrespective of attribution and group terms were used that may be different than what is in the
final USPI for nirogacestat.
Routine Clinical Tests
The Applicant’s Position:
Laboratory, ECGs, and vital signs assessments were performed according to the Schedule of
Activities in each protocol. Available data were summarized using the Safety population from all
the study pools as well as the OLE Phase of Study NIR‐DT‐301. Laboratory results were not
available for Study 14‐C‐0007.

Clinical laboratory values were expressed in conventional units. Normal ranges were
determined based on the ranges supplied by the laboratory and were not normalized.
Missing normal ranges were replaced by ranges from the central laboratory from the same
study or standard laboratory ranges.
If ECG results were collected in triplicates, the average of the triplicate ECG records was
calculated and used as analysis values for each visit. Additionally, for the All CM Pool and All DT
Pool, the most extreme value, and its change from baseline per Cycle (1, 2, 3, 4 ‐ 6, 7‐12 and 13
and onward) was summarized. The most extreme ECGs were defined as the highest
post‐baseline value for QT, QTcF, QRS and PR intervals and the largest absolute change for
heart rate.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s statement. FDA further notes blood chemistries, hematology
assessments were obtained at baseline, Cycle 1 Days 1, 8, 15, and 22, Cycle 2 day 28, Cycle 4
Day 1 and every third cycle thereafter on Day 1. Pregnancy tests were conducted in WOCBP at
baseline, prior to first dose of study treatment, on Cycle 1 Day 22, Cycle 2 Day 28, then
monthly. With institution of amendment #3 (January 27, 2020) menstrual history and female
and male hormone levels were collected. Female hormones (TSH, Prolactin, AMH, LH, FSH,
estradiol, and progesterone) were collected at baseline, Cycle 1 Day 22, Cycle 2 Day 28, Cycle 4
Day 1 and every 3 cycles thereafter. Male hormones (total testosterone, free testosterone,
progesterone FSH, and LH) were collected prior to first treatment of study drug, Cycle 4 Day 1
and every 3 cycles thereafter.

Safety Results
Deaths
Data:
A total of 3 deaths were reported in the All DT Pool and all were assessed by the investigator as
unrelated to nirogacestat. The incidence of TEAEs that led to death was the same in both the
nirogacestat and placebo treatment groups. In Study NIR‐DT‐301, one participant in the placebo
arm died due to sepsis. One participant in the nirogacestat 150 mg BID arm of Study NIR‐DT‐
301 died due to multi‐organ failure more than 30 days after discontinuing study treatment in
the setting of aggressive tumor progression and a new diagnosis of spindle cell sarcoma. One
participant from Study 14‐C‐0007 died from a cerebrovascular accident.
All other TEAEs with fatal outcome were reported from participants being treated for advanced
cancer in the All CM Pool. In this pool, there were 7 deaths (4%) across all dosing groups,
including 3 deaths (2%) in the nirogacestat 150 mg arm. Five of the deaths in this pool were
212
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

attributed to disease progression; the other 2 reported deaths were due to other causes in
Studies 14‐C‐0007 and A8541020. The increase in number of deaths in the All CM Pool
compared to the All DT Pool was driven by the older and sicker population of participants in
these early studies of participants with advanced cancer.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat was not considered as a cause of death for any reported event. TEAEs leading to
death were consistent with the underlying disease or disease progression.
The FDA’s Assessment:
At the time of the NDA submission, one patient who received nirogacestat died in Study NIR‐
DT‐301; one death was also reported in the placebo arm. The patient who died in the
nirogacestat arm in Study NIR‐DT‐301 had an identified APC mutation and was diagnosed with
spindle cell sarcoma which had previously been reported as a pulmonary nodule. The patient
discontinued nirogacestat on Day 65 and subsequently died on Day 108 of multiorgan failure.
FDA agrees with the investigator (and Applicant) that this patient’s death is unlikely related to
nirogacestat.
One additional patient with desmoid tumor died in NCI trial 14‐C‐0007. This patient was 60‐
years‐old person who died of a cerebrovascular accident with a CT‐confirmed pontine
hemorrhage. The patient had atrial fibrillation in the hospital and the investigator deemed the
event as unlikely related. This attribution appears reasonable noting that the patient was on his
sixth year of treatment and the patient also had severe hypertension as a potential contributing
factor for the CVA.
Other deaths reported in nirogacestat development occurred in patients with advanced
malignancies. All but two were attributed in the application to underlying disease or disease
progression. One of the two patients died of thrombosis prior to randomization and one patient
with advanced TNBC and ischemic heart disease experienced dyspnea and died. The
investigator deemed the event as unrelated; however, additional details were lacking for this
patient; therefore there is uncertainty regarding this patient’s cause of death.
No new deaths were reported at the time of the 120‐day safety update.
Serious Adverse Events
Data:
In the All DT Pool, SAEs were reported in 23 participants (26%) in the nirogacestat 150 mg BID
arm. In the nirogacestat 150 mg BID arm, premature menopause (3 participants) and hematuria
(2 participants) were the only PTs reported in more than 1 participant; in the placebo arm;
sepsis (3 participants) and COVID‐19 (2 participants) were reported in more than 1 participant.
213
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

All 3 reports of premature menopause were reported in Study NIR‐DT‐301 as an unexpected
event early in the study (prior to identification of OD as an adverse event of special interest
[AESI]) and were considered serious due to medical importance; more information is provided
in Section 8.2.5. Overall, the findings in the All DT Pool align with those described in Study NIR‐
DT‐301.
The Applicant’s Position:
The events and rates of SAEs were generally consistent across studies and are representative of
the overall safety profile. The only SAE reported by > 2 participants receiving nirogacestat was
premature menopause. The relatively low incidence of SAEs supports a favorable benefit:risk
profile with nirogacestat in the treatment of patients with DT.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In Study NIR‐DT‐301 (in the double‐blind phase), SAEs were reported in 20% of patients in the
nirogacestat arm versus 11% among patients in the placebo arm. Overall SAEs of infections
were similar between arms (6% for nirogacestat versus 7% for placebo) and were therefore, not
included in product labeling. The only organ system with a difference between arms was for
ovarian toxicity (4% versus 0% among placebo) (note that the group term of ovarian toxicity
differs from the more general term of reproductive system disorders described in the original
application). Among the 4% of patients with ovarian toxicity SAEs, there were three patients
with an SAE of premature menopause and one patient with reported ovarian failure.
In Study NCI‐14‐C‐0007, following an update to the safety database, there were two SAEs
considered by the investigators as possibly related to nirogacestat: squamous cell carcinoma
and vulvovaginal inflammation. These were Grade 2 in severity. No SAE occurred in more than
one patient. The labeling for the nirogacestat includes a Warning for non‐melanoma skin
cancers (in NIR‐DT‐301, cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma and basal cell carcinoma occurred
in 2.9% and 1.4% of patients, respectively).
When considering the combined DT pool of patients, the only terms that occurred in more than
one patient were premature menopause (n=3) and hematuria (n=2).
The safety update described additional SAEs in the placebo to nirogacestat group in NIR‐DT‐
301. One patient experienced an SAE of proctalgia and one of pyelonephritis. Both events were
considered by the investigator/sponsor as unrelated to nirogacestat. One additional patient in
the safety update was diagnosed with Grade 3 intestinal fistula and intestinal abscess. This
patient with FAP had a prior history of pelvic abscesses and fistulas and these events were
considered unrelated to nirogacestat.
In summary, although there is a slight increase in SAEs among patients who received
nirogacestat versus placebo in Study NIR‐DT‐301 (double‐blind phase), the major explanation

for this difference was ovarian toxicity (also, exposure was longer in the nirogacestat arm). In
general, it is difficult to ascribe most of the specific SAEs to nirogacestat due to low frequency
of individual events (however, non melanoma skin cancers, an event observed in the NCI study,
is described in product labeling).
Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
Data:
Events leading to treatment discontinuation that occurred in > 1 participant in the All DT Pool
were consistent with what was observed in Study NIR‐DT‐301. In the All DT Pool,
17 participants (19%) treated with nirogacestat 150 mg BID discontinued study treatment
compared to 2 participants (3%) treated with placebo (dysphagia and drug‐induced liver injury).
The most frequently reported PTs (occurring in > 1 participant in the nirogacestat 150 mg arm)
were: diarrhea (4 participants), ALT increased (3 participants), premature menopause
(3 participants), and AST increased (2 participants).
The Applicant’s Position:
The profile of TEAEs leading to treatment discontinuation was consistent with the known safety
profile of nirogacestat and GSIs.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In the DeFi study, per the AE dataset, 16 of 69 patients (23%) who received nirogacestat in the
DB phase discontinued due to an AE vs 2 (3%) in patients who discontinued placebo. Per the
CSR, two of these 16 patients in the nirogacestat arm discontinued due to disease progression
with a concomitant AE occurring at the time of treatment discontinuation (such that 14 patients
or 20% discontinued due to a nirogacestat AE, as described in product labeling). The AE for one
of the two patients was tumor hemorrhage. The AE for the other patient was hematuria
occurring in a patient with a tumor located in the pelvis with vital structure involvement,
including bladder, kidney, and intestinal tract. This patient’s narrative is discussed in the review
above (clinical progression event)
The most common PTs occurring in more than one patient in the nirogacestat arm were those
listed by the Applicant: diarrhea (4 participants), ALT increased (3 participants), premature
menopause (3 participants), and AST increased (2 participants). One of the 4 patients who
discontinued nirogacestat with diarrhea also had hot flushes at the time of discontinuation.
One additional patient discounted nirogacestat due to ovarian failure.

Significant Adverse Events
Data:
For the purposes of this Assessment Aid, Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs are considered significant.
TEAEs of Grade ≥ 3 in the All DT Pool occurred in 60% of participants treated with nirogacestat
150 mg and comprised of 5% of total TEAE events (130/2543 events). Fifty participants (57%)
reported Grade 3 events, 2 participants reported Grade 4 events (spindle cell sarcoma and
appendicitis), and 1 participant reported a death (cerebrovascular accident). The most
frequently reported Grade 3 TEAEs across all groups were diarrhea (15 participants; 16%) and
hypophosphatemia (15 participants; 16%). For the nirogacestat 150 mg BID group in the All DT
Pool, the most frequently reported Grade 3 TEAEs were diarrhea (14 participants [16%]) and
hypophosphatemia (11 participants [13%]). Grade 3 hypophosphatemia was notably higher in
the All DT Pool 150 mg BID group compared to Study NIR‐DT‐301 (11 participants [13%] vs
2 participants [3%]) (See Table below).
The incidences of TEAEs of Grade 3 occurring in the groups receiving nirogacestat 150 mg BID in
the All DT, All CM Pool, and OLE Pools were generally aligned with the nirogacestat 150 mg BID
arm in Study NIR‐DT‐301.

The Applicant’s Position:
Ninety‐five percent of all reported TEAEs were Grade 1 or 2. Overall, the incidence of Grade 3
TEAEs was relatively low and only 2 Grade 4 events reported among participants with DT at any
dose level; neither of these events were considered related to nirogacestat. The most
frequently reported Grade 3 TEAE was diarrhea; Grade 3 hypophosphatemia was also
frequently reported in the All DT Pool. These observations were effectively managed with

217
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

appropriate interventions and are consistent with the safety profile of GSIs supporting a
favorable benefit:risk profile.
The FDA’s Assessment: FDA would generally not agree that the incidence of Grade 3 TEAEs was
low noting that 55% of patients treated with nirogacestat in DeFi were described as having a
Grade 3 or greater TEAEs vs 17% for placebo. Nevertheless, most of the severe toxicities are
those that can be managed or mitigated by supportive care or dose modifications. For example,
the most common clinical Grade 3 AE was diarrhea. Skin toxicities such as folliculitis and rash
also occurred in 6% of patients each.
DeFi used CTCAE version 5 to assess severity of AEs which potentially resulted in a lower
incidence of Grade 3 hypophosphatemia because CTCAE v5 requires clinical sequalae for Grade
3. Nevertheless, 20% of patients in DeFi experienced phosphate levels less than 2 mg/dL
(similar to criteria in CTCAE version 4).
With respect to Grade 4 events occurring in DeFi, there were three listed patients in the ADAE
dataset. One patient was described above in this review (diagnosis of spindle cell sarcoma) and
one patient was diagnosed with appendicitis. One additional patient was listed as having Grade
4 obesity with a start date one day after switching from placebo to OLE (as such would not
consider this as related to treatment with nirogacestat).
Refer to sections below (TEAEs and Application‐Specific Safety Issues) for further exploration of
TEAEs of all grades (including Grade 3 or above).
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
Data:
The incidences of frequently (≥ 20% of participants) occurring TEAEs in the groups receiving
nirogacestat 150 mg BID in the All DT, All CM Pool, and OLE Group were generally aligned with
the population in Study NIR‐DT‐301 (Table 52). Overall, the most frequently (> 30%) reported
TEAEs in the NIR‐DT‐301 study and All DT Pool were diarrhea, nausea, fatigue,
hypophosphatemia, and rash maculopapular, which are expected with GS inhibition
(Krop et al. 2012; Tolcher et al. 2012; Richter et al. 2014; Messersmith et al. 2015;
Pant et al. 2016).

The Applicant’s Position:
The most frequently reported AEs (> 20% in Study NIR‐DT‐301) were diarrhea, nausea, fatigue,
hypophosphatemia, rash maculopapular, stomatitis headache, dermatitis acneiform, and
vomiting (with vomiting being reported with a similar incidence in the placebo arm).
OD occurred in 75% of WOCBP. Additional frequently reported AEs in the All DT Pool include
AST increased, hot flush, ALT increased, abdominal pain, and cough. These were generally
consistent with the effects of GS inhibition. The majority of participants (97%) reported their
first TEAE in Cycle 1, and 95% of all reported TEAEs were Grade < 3.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA was able to reproduce the incidence rate of individual adverse events in Study
NIR‐DT‐301 described in Table 52 above using standard FDA‐run analyses/scripts (of SDTM
datasets) with the exception of nausea (FDA script 55% for nirogacestat, headache 30%
nirogacestat, and ALT increased 17% for nirogacestat). With alternative (but similar)
methodology, grouping TEAEs in the dataset by patient ID, organ class, preferred term (by max
toxicity grade), and arm, FDA was able to replicate the applicant’s results for these three AE
terms.

Laboratory Findings
Data from physical examinations, ECOG scores, vital signs, and laboratory safety evaluations
were not centrally databased for Study 14‐C‐0007 and were therefore not included in the
integrated analyses of these data.
Data:
Hematology
In the All DT Pool and All CM Pools, an increase from baseline in basophils and eosinophils was
observed by the first cycle and persisted into the third cycle. Concurrently, the percentage of
basophils and eosinophils out of total leukocytes also increased from baseline.
In Study NIR‐DT‐301, post‐treatment eosinophil values consistently trended higher among
participants receiving nirogacestat as compared to placebo; this finding did not appear to
correlate with an increased rate of AEs of eosinophilia or AEs associated with the development
of allergic reactions. A persistent increase in the basophil/leukocyte and eosinophil/leukocyte
ratios, along with a transient small decrease in absolute lymphocyte count, is suggestive of a
mild inflammatory state associated with on‐target activity of nirogacestat.
Laboratory data from clinical studies do not suggest that nirogacestat 150 mg BID is associated
with changes in lymphocyte or neutrophil count values that are different from those observed
in the placebo arm of Study NIR‐DT‐301. Participants from studies other than NIR‐DT‐301 in the
All DT Pool did not contribute meaningful data to inform this aspect of the safety profile of
224
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

nirogacestat. Observations from the data in the All CM Pool and OLE Group were aligned with
those from Study NIR‐DT‐301.
Clinical Chemistry
There was a trend toward increased ALT and AST values and decreased phosphate and
potassium in the All DT Pool and All CM Pool compared to placebo. Alkaline phosphatase,
glucose, total bilirubin, albumin, total protein, creatinine, and chloride remained relatively
stable in both treatment arms.
The Applicant’s Position:
A persistent increase in the basophil/leukocyte and eosinophil/leukocyte ratio along with a
transient small decrease in absolute lymphocyte count is suggestive of a mild inflammatory
state associated with on‐target activity of nirogacestat. The data described above concerning
the effect of nirogacestat on lymphocytes and neutrophils do not suggest that GS inhibition due
to nirogacestat is having adverse effect on their numbers, and the data are not consistent with
an immunosuppressed state.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the most important (clinically) laboratory abnormalities appeared to be increases
liver enzymes and depletion of electrolytes. Because laboratory data generally describe the
finding more accurately than AE data, FDA recommended inclusion of a separate laboratory
finding table in product labeling. Overall, the incidence of hyperbilirubinemia was slightly higher
in the placebo arm (11% versus 6%) with one Grade 3 or greater event in both arms. No case of
Hy’s law was observed in nirogacestat‐treated patients in Study NIR‐DT‐301.
FDA recommend inclusion of phosphate levels < 2 mg/dL in Section 5 of labeling in patients
treated with nirogacestat (20%) because below 2 mg/dL represents hypophosphatemia that
should be acted upon to prevent development of symptomatic disease.

Data
There was an increased incidence of Grade 1 proteinuria and Grade 1 glucosuria in the
nirogacestat arm of Study NIR‐DT‐301 compared to the placebo arm.
The Applicant’s Position:
The observation of an increased incidence of Grade 1 proteinuria and glucosuria in participants
receiving nirogacestat compared to placebo, along with a plausible effect of GS inhibition on
the functioning of renal tubule epithelium, supports recognizing proteinuria and glycosuria as
identified risks for nirogacestat.

proteinuria at Baseline showed Grade 1 at the end of cycle 2 (27% versus 12%); however, there

Vital Signs
Data:
No clinically significant trends were identified in vital sign parameters when compared to
placebo or across dose subgroups.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat did not appear to have clinically meaningful adverse effects on participant vital
signs during any study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that there did not appear to be sustained clinically significant changes in blood
pressure or heart rate upon review of box‐whisker plots in the application.
Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
Data:
No clinically significant trends were identified in ECG parameters. In the All DT Pool,
9 participants (10%) reported QTcF increases of 30‐60 msec. This was increased compared to
placebo (1 participant [1%]) and was consistent across subgroups of age, gender, BMI, race,
dose intensity, and prior therapy. No increases in QTcF > 60 msec were identified in the All DT
Pool nirogacestat 150 mg arm.
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat did not appear to have clinically meaningful adverse effects on ECG values during
any study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
See below for QT assessment. Per the QT‐IRT review, there were no patients who experienced
PR values > 220 msec with 25% increase over baseline or QRS value > 120 msec with 25%
increase over baseline in Study NIR‐DT‐103 or in Study A8641014.
QT
A concentration‐QT (C‐QT) analysis was conducted using time‐matched PK and triplicate
12‐lead ECG data from five Phase 1 studies in healthy participant studies (A8641001, A8641002,
NIR‐DT‐101, NIR‐DT‐102, and NIR‐DT‐103) and 2 studies in participants with DT (NIR‐DT‐301)
and advanced solid tumors or T‐ALL/LBL (A8641014). The final C‐QT model was used to predict
the nirogacestat concentration‐related change in QTcF in participants with DT and in
participants with other advanced solid tumors or T‐ALL/LBL. For participants with DT, the mean
227

predicted ΔQTcF and 90% CI were below 10 msec for each evaluated concentration of interest,
including 2 × Cmax at steady‐state in the presence of strong CYP3A4 inhibition. For participants
with other advanced malignancies, as evaluated in Study A8641014, the mean predicted ΔQTcF
and 90% CI were below 10 msec for each evaluated concentration of interest, including 2 × Cmax
at steady‐state in the presence of moderate CYP3A4 inhibition.
The Applicant’s Position:
The results of a C‐QT analysis do not support the existence of a clinically significant
prolongation of QTcF intervals associated with therapeutic dosing of nirogacestat alone or with
moderate CYP3A4 inhibition.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Although the maximum change in QT from baseline was slightly higher in the nirogacestat arm
IN Study NIR‐DT‐301 (12% vs 1% with a 30 msec increase or greater, per the applicant’s CSR), a
detailed review by via FDA QT‐IRT determined that nirogacestat does not cause large mean
increases in mean QTc interval (i.e., 20 msec or more) at the approved dose. There were no
subjects in Study NIR‐DT‐103 who experienced a QTcF of greater than 450 msec.
Immunogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
Nirogacestat is a small molecule and not expected to elicit an immune response.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees.

Analysis of Submission‐Specific Safety Issues
In Study NIR‐DT‐301, designation of an event as an AESI was an investigator decision based on
guidance provided in the protocol. According to the protocol, most AESI PTs (except drug
reactions) had to be Grade ≥ 2 to qualify for designation as an AESI. AESIs were not specified in
other studies included in this summary.
To better characterize AESIs and to account for legacy studies in which no AESIs were
prospectively identified per the protocol, AESIs for the integrated safety analyses were
identified by reported PT and included the following topics: OD; skin events of rash, hidradenitis
suppurativa, and folliculitis; electrolyte insufficiency events of hypophosphatemia and
hypokalemia; elevated liver enzymes; and hypersensitivity drug reactions. Of note, events of
Grade ≥ 3 hypomagnesemia were listed as AESIs in the NIR‐DT‐301 protocol; however, no
Grade ≥ 3 events of hypomagnesemia were reported in the NIR‐DT‐301 study or in the All DT,
All CM, or OLE Pools at any dose level.
228
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Data:
Ovarian Dysfunction
OD emerged as an AESI for nirogacestat based on observations made early in the conduct of
Study NIR‐DT‐301. Evaluation of the data concerning this event, and review of the literature
concerning the role of GS in ovarian function, support recognizing OD as an important identified
risk for nirogacestat.
Note that OD was not available as a PT at the time of the amendment or in MedDRA
v24.1, which is the MedDRA version used for this summary; therefore, for the purposes of
identifying participants with this condition, OD was defined narrowly by the PTs of ovarian
failure, premature menopause, amenorrhea, and menopause.
The majority of WOCBP receiving nirogacestat 150 mg BID in Study NIR‐DT‐301 reported OD
(27 of 36 WOCBP [75%]) as compared to 0 of 37 WOCBP receiving placebo. OD events resolved
in all participants (n = 11; 100%) following discontinuation of nirogacestat with available
follow‐up data and also in the majority of participants (n = 9; 64%) remaining on study
treatment with nirogacestat. OD events were accompanied by suppression of AMH and
estradiol production, and increases in FSH, LH, and progesterone; however, nirogacestat
treatment did not appear to result in a prolonged suppression of estradiol. Leading possibilities
for the mechanism behind the observed ovarian events involve disruption of the growth and
development of the ovarian follicle from the primary stage to the corpus luteum rather than
damage or destruction of ovarian tissue or primordial follicles. The increased incidence
observed in participants receiving nirogacestat 150 mg BID compared to placebo, nature of the
event, and its potential impact on the WOCBP experiencing it support OD being designated an
important risk.
Skin Events
Skin events were an AESI in Study NIR‐DT‐301 based on observations with other GSIs
(Takebe et al. 2014) and in early clinical studies of nirogacestat (Messersmith et al. 2015).
Evaluation of the data concerning these events, and review of the literature concerning the role
of GS in skin homeostasis, support recognizing skin rashes (rash maculopapular, dermatitis
acneiform, rash, rash erythematous, rash pruritic, and rash papular), hidradenitis, folliculitis,
and alopecia as identified risks for nirogacestat. In addition, based on the reports of Grade 3
events of maculopapular rash, hidradenitis, and folliculitis, these events have been designated
as important risks.
Skin events were reported at a higher incidence in the nirogacestat 150 mg BID groups
compared to the placebo group. Only 2 skin event reports were serious: 1 report of rash
maculopapular from a participant in the nirogacestat 150 mg arm in Study NIR‐DT‐301 and
229
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

1 report of rash in a participant transitioning from placebo to nirogacestat 150 mg BID in the
OLE Pool.
Due to the increased incidence of skin events observed in participants receiving nirogacestat
150 mg BID compared to placebo and the severity of the events in affected participants,
hidradenitis suppurativa, folliculitis, and rash maculopapular are considered important
identified risks.
Electrolyte Insufficiency Events
Hypophosphatemia and hypokalemia emerged as AESIs based on observations with other GSIs
(Takebe et al. 2014) and in early clinical studies of nirogacestat (Messersmith et al. 2015). The
increased incidence of hypophosphatemia and hypokalemia in the nirogacestat arm of Study
NIR‐DT‐301 compared to the placebo arm, along with a plausible role for GS inhibition, the
occurrence of Grade 3 events, and the frequent need for management with phosphate or
potassium supplementation supports their recognition as important identified risks for
nirogacestat.
The increased incidence of hypophosphatemia in the nirogacestat arm of Study NIR‐DT‐301
compared to the placebo arm, along with a plausible role for GS inhibition, supports its
recognition as an identified risk of nirogacestat. The occurrence of Grade 3 events, and the
frequent need for management with phosphate supplementation, support designating
hypophosphatemia as an important event.
The possible mechanisms described for hypophosphatemia may also affect potassium
homeostasis. The increased incidence of hypokalemia in the nirogacestat arm of Study NIR‐
DT‐301 compared to the placebo arm and severity of the reported events, along with a
plausible role for GS inhibition, supports recognizing hypokalemia as an important identified
risk of nirogacestat.
Elevated Liver Transaminases
Effects on hepatic function were an AESI for nirogacestat based on observations of mild hepatic
vacuolation in nonclinical studies in rats, and transient transaminase elevations in early clinical
studies with nirogacestat.
The clinically relevant increased incidence of elevated transaminases in the nirogacestat 150 mg
BID arm compared to the placebo arm in Study NIR‐DT‐301, and the time to first onset of the
events during the first 3 cycles for most participants reporting an event, supports the
identification of elevated transaminases as an identified risk associated with the use of
nirogacestat. The occurrence of Grade 3 increases in transaminase values, along with a need to
inform prescribers that some of these events may be managed with drug interruption followed

by dose reduction, rather than permanent drug discontinuation, supports designation of this
risk as important.
Hypersensitivity Drug Reactions
A total of 5 participants receiving nirogacestat reported clinically relevant drug hypersensitivity
/ anaphylaxis events; however, only 2 events were suggestive of a hypersensitivity reaction to
nirogacestat. The other 3 events were either associated with another concomitant medication
(trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole or morphine) or did not necessitate the immediate
discontinuation of nirogacestat.
Overall, the infrequent occurrence of relevant events and the details of the individual events
are not supportive of recognizing drug reactions (hypersensitivity and anaphylactic reaction) as
risks for nirogacestat.
Applicant’s Position
The data support recognizing OD, skin events, electrolyte insufficiency events, and elevated
transaminases as important identified risks of nirogacestat. The recommended dose
modifications for these events are provided in Table 58; diarrhea is not an AESI but it is an
important identified risk described in Section 8.2.6. For other severe adverse reactions, or in
the event of life‐threatening adverse reactions, nirogacestat should be withheld until resolved
to Grade 1 or baseline. Only restart at a dose of 100 mg BID after carefully considering the
potential benefit and likelihood of recurrence of the adverse reaction. Nirogacestat should be
permanently discontinued for recurrence of severe or life‐threatening nirogacestat‐related
toxicity upon rechallenge at the reduced dose.

The FDA’s Assessment:
Ovarian toxicity: Labeling contains an FDA‐recommended Waring with respect to ovarian
toxicity informing prescribers that female reproductive function and fertility may be impaired
following treatment with nirogacestat. Labeling recommends monitoring patients for changes
in menstrual cycle regularity or the development of symptoms of estrogen deficiency, including
hot flashes, night sweats, and vaginal dryness. Overall 75% of women with reproductive
potential (n=36) were reported to have an adverse event related to ovarian toxicity versus no
patient in the control arm. Reported terms included ovarian failure, premature menopause,
amenorrhea, and menopause. All events were Grade 2 in severity per the CTCAE definition. Per
the ISS, 20 out of 27 patients reported resolution, including 11 that resolved off nirogacestat;
however, per FDA analysis, the criteria to assess resolution were not adequate and therefore,
additional study is needed.
Ultimately, because of potential overlap between terms such as ovarian failure, menopause,
premature menopause, etc., a combined term was used in labeling. Additional sequelae of
ovarian toxicity occurred during DeFi, such as hot flushes and weight gain (weight gain was
similar to placebo in men but greater than placebo in women). These are not specifically
described in labeling because they appeared to be downstream symptoms of ovarian toxicity
(and there may have been variability with respect to reporting these symptoms versus a more
global term such as premature menopause). With respect to hot flush events, 12 of 13 occurred
in the setting of ovarian toxicity.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Due to limitations in the selected patient population, timing of reproductive hormone
measurements, missing laboratory values, definitions of adverse events, and criteria to
designate recovery, FDA was not able to clearly describe the incidence and severity of ovarian
toxicity. Given these limitations, DUOG consultants (and DO3 agrees) recommended a Post
Marketing Requirement to conduct a new study to characterize ovarian toxicity more
adequately.
Consistent with reports of ovarian toxicity, changes in hormone levels were reported following
treatment with nirogacestat including FSH, LH, AMH, and estrogen, and progesterone.
Skin toxicity/events: Based on the results observed in DeFi, cutaneous squamous cell
carcinoma and basal cell carcinoma were diagnosed in 2.9% and 1.4% of patients, respectively.
With respect to squamous cell carcinoma of the skin, one patient was diagnosed with a lesion
on the left cheek and underwent Mohs surgery. The other patient’s lesion occurred on the
anterior thigh. FDA recommended inclusion of non‐melanoma skin cancers in product labeling.
As described above, GS may have a role in skin homeostasis. Rash, as a grouped (composite)
term was identified in 68% of patients who received nirogacestat versus 14% in placebo with
6% of patients in the nirogacestat arm with Grade 3 rash. Dermatological events (terms)
included rash maculo‐papular, rash papular, rash, eczema, rash erythematous, dermatitis
acneiform, palmar‐plantar erythrodysaesthesia syndrome, dermatitis, and rash pruritic. The
most common of these terms were rash maculopapular (32%), dermatitis acneiform (22%), and
rash (19%). Alopecia and hidradenitis suppurativa were also reported. Dry skin and pruritis
were reported; however, these were generally co‐reported with other dermatological adverse
events (consistent with a manifestation of the other rash‐events). Specifically 10 of the 11
nirogacestat‐treated patients with dry skin reported another skin event, compared to 1 of the 5
patients in the placebo arm.
Per the ISS, the median onset to first skin rash was 22 days with most patients having onset in
the first cycle. Rash led to dose reduction in seven patients, dose interruption in 10 patients,
and nirogacestat discontinuation in one patient. During DeFi, agents used to manage rash
included topical steroids, antibiotics, and acne treatments.
Of note, FDA sent an information request to SpringWorks regarding bacterial infections given
the potential signal noted during the assessment of all PTs and HLTs. The majority of bacterial
infections in patients treated with nirogacestat in DeFi were folliculitis (9 of 14 events) which is
described in labeling. Most other bacterial infections were also related to skin (e.g., paronychia,
cellulitis, and staph abscess).
Electrolyte insufficiency: Electrolyte depletion, particularly for potassium and phosphorus can
occur following treatment with nirogacestat. Labeling describes these risks in Section 5. In DeFi,
these included decreased phosphate (65%) and decreased potassium (22%). Phosphate <2

mg/dL occurred in 20% of patients who received OGSIVEO. Grade 3 decreased potassium
occurred in 1.4% of patients. Final Labeling for nirogacestat recommends withholding
nirogacestat (with future dose reduction) for Grade 3 or 4 hypophosphatemia persisting for 3 or

There were no adverse event reports of magnesium; however, this electrolyte was not
routinely collected during the conduct of the study.
With regard to hypophosphatemia, these events were frequently recurrent and six patients
underwent dose modification (interruption, dose reduction, or discontinuation) for
hypophosphatemia. Hypophosphatemia frequently occurred early with most patients being
diagnosed with low phosphate during the first cycle.
With respect to potassium, per the CSR, the median onset to first hypokalemia event was 15
days in the nirogacestat arm. No patient was reported to have an SAE due to hypokalemia and
there were no dose modifications due to hypokalemia.
Elevated liver transaminases: The FDA review team recommended inclusion of hepatotoxicity
in the Warnings section of product labeling as ALT or AST elevations occurred in 30% and 33%
of patients who received nirogacestat in DeFi, respectively. ALT or AST greater than 5 times the
upper limit of normal occurred in 6% and 2.9% of patients, respectively. No report of Hy’s law
was observed during DeFi. Median onset to first increase AST or ALT was reported to be 22 days
in the nirogacestat arm and the median duration of increased AST or ALT was 29 days.
In DeFi, nirogacestat let to dose reduction in one patient and drug discontinuation in three
patients. In addition to the Warning, labeling also includes recommendations regarding
increased AST or ALT including withholding nirogacestat for Grade 2 transaminitis (with a dose
reduction).
Hypersensitivity drug reactions: In the application, SpringWorks identified 5 potential cases of
clinically relevant drug hypersensitivity or anaphylaxis. At least two of the cases, upon review of
narrative summaries in the SCS, appeared to be more likely related either trimethoprim /
sulfamethoxazole or morphine. In the other three patients, the events appeared likely related
to nirogacestat; however, based on the narratives, it was difficult to ascertain whether the
events were hypersensitivity events or manifestations of other known nirogacestat‐related
adverse events, particularly rash and stomatitis.

Other Noteworthy Adverse Events
The Applicant’s Position:
Some of these events had also been identified as being of interest based on the available
nirogacestat nonclinical data and information concerning other GSIs.
Clinically relevant increases in incidences in the nirogacestat 150 mg BID groups compared to
the placebo arm in Study NIR‐DT‐301 were observed in the PTs of diarrhea, nausea, stomatitis,
fatigue, epistaxis, upper respiratory tract infection, cough, dyspnea, and headache, along with
the laboratory findings of Grade 1 proteinuria and glucosuria. Most of these events are not
identified as important risks due to the absence or very low incidence of SAEs or Grade ≥ 3
events for these risks; however, diarrhea is an important risk.
In Study NIR‐301‐DT, diarrhea occurred in 84% of participants who received nirogacestat
compared to 35% in participants given placebo; Grade 3 diarrhea occurred in 16% of
participants. Participants should be monitored and managed according to Table 58.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Diarrhea commonly occurred following treatment with nirogacestat with 84% of patients
reporting diarrhea in DeFi versus 35% in the placebo arm (Grade 3: 16% versus 1.4%). Per the
ISS, non‐clinical data have suggested that GS inhibition “may interfere with the integrity of the
lower gastrointestinal tract epithelium and control of intestinal motility.” In total, 129 diarrhea
events were reported among the 58 nirogacestat‐treated patients with diarrhea. Per the ISS,
the median time to first reporting diarrhea was 9 days. Supportive medicines for diarrhea (e.g.,
loperamide) were used in 62% of patients with diarrhea in the nirogacestat arm.
Stomatitis in labeling consists of a composite term of different but related PTs. There was one
event of an SAE due to stomatitis occurring on Day 9 where the patient developed Grade 3
stomatitis requiring treatment for infection and tramadol. The patient was later able to re‐start
treatment with a dose reduction.
Dyspnea occurred in 16% of patients in the nirogacestat arm versus 6% among patients who
received placebo. Although the CSR assessed potential risk factors, e.g., based on location of
disease, FAP status, or prior chemotherapy there were too few patients to provide any
conclusions. Dyspnea could be long‐lasting however, with the median duration of dyspnea
reported as 197 days in the nirogacestat‐treated arm.

Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing
Safety/Tolerability

PROs are discussed in Section 8.1.3 and Section 8.1.9.

See FDA’s assessments in Section 8.1.3 and Section 8.1.9.

Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
Data:
Intrinsic factors
The safety of nirogacestat was analyzed by the following subgroups: age, sex, race, ethnicity,
BMI, other illness, and organ dysfunction. There were no overall differences in safety among
participants based on age and the small numbers of non‐White and Hispanic or Latino
participants limits the ability to draw conclusions based on race or ethnicity.
As previously discussed, events associated with OD were reported at a higher incidence in
female participants in the nirogacestat arm of Study NIR‐DT‐301 compared to the placebo arm
(Section 8.2.5). Other TEAEs that occurred more frequently in females and in the nirogacestat
arm include nausea, stomatitis, alopecia, influenza‐like illness, hypokalemia, sinusitis, and
dizziness. Hypophosphatemia was the only TEAE that occurred more frequently in males and
had a higher incidence in the nirogacestat arm. These trends were also observed in the All DT
and All CM Pools.
Apparent trends observed in the TEAEs by BMI subgroup analyses included: pruritis and
dyspnea appeared to increase in incidence as BMI rose, at a level that was greater than that in
the placebo group. Stomatitis and alanine aminotransferase increase occurred with greater
incidence in the lower BMI subgroups, at a level that was greater than in the placebo group.
However, no definitive conclusions could be made regarding the rates of TEAEs in select
subgroups given the small number of participants within each subgroup.
Extrinsic factors
The safety of nirogacestat was analyzed by geographic location; no overall differences in safety
among participants in these subgroups were observed.
The Applicant’s Position:
There were no overall differences in safety among participants in most subgroups.

(consistent with overall demographics of DT) in Study NIR‐DT‐301.
In the safety population, about two thirds of patients in both arms were women (44 of 69 for
nirogacestat and 47 of 72 for placebo) with 25 men in both arms. Although the applicant
conduct analyses of AEs by sex, such analyses should be considered exploratory and non‐
conclusive given the small number of patients. In general the AEs listed above in Data were
those that occurred at a higher incidence rate in men or women. Per the CSR there were a
limited additional terms (e.g., back pain in men or non‐cardiac chest pain in women); however,
the overall number of patients/events was low.
Most importantly, ovarian toxicity (see above) was limited to women enrolled in the trial.
Alopecia occurred at a relative higher rate among women as compared to men (27% versus
4%).

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Data:
Studies with nirogacestat have not been conducted in women who are pregnant or lactating.
Reproductive toxicology studies in rats have been completed with nirogacestat and confirm the
reproductive hazard of nirogacestat as a result of gamma‐secretase inhibition in the developing
fetus. In the fertility and early embryo toxicity studies (Studies 01214013 and 01214026),
nirogacestat administration of doses of 20, 40, and 80 mg/kg/day prior to and during early
pregnancy induced lower mating, fertility, and pregnancy indices compared to untreated rats
and those dosed at 5 mg/kg/day. In pregnant rats treated with nirogacestat doses of 0, 5, 20,
50, or 150 mg/kg/day on gestation days 6 through 17, during the period of organogenesis,
nirogacestat induced fetal loss and resorptions (Study 01214011). These fetal effects occurred
at systemic exposures that are below those observed in participants at the proposed 150 mg
dose level.
The Applicant’s Position:
(b) (4)

the patient should
be apprised of the potential hazard to the fetus.
Since drugs are commonly excreted in human milk and because of the potential for serious
adverse reactions in nursing infants, women should not breastfeed during treatment with
nirogacestat and for at least 1 week after the last dose of nirogacestat.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Nirogacestat may cause ovarian toxicity in women of child bearing potential (see review above).
A post marketing requirement to further assess reproductive function / ovarian toxicity is being
requested to characterize the effects of nirogacestat more fully. Additionally, labeling will
contained a warning regarding the risk of ovarian toxicity.
The non‐clinical section of this review above describes findings from animal studies. In
summary, oral administration of nirogacestat to pregnant rats during the period of
organogenesis resulted in embryo‐fetal toxicity and death at maternal exposures below the
human exposure at the recommended dose of 150 mg twice daily.
Based on these findings, labeling will state that nirogacestat can cause fetal harm when
administered to pregnant women. Labeling will advise women and men of reproductive
potential to use effective contraception during treatment with OGSIVEO and for 1 week after
the last dose. Labeling will also recommend against breast feeding during treatment with
nirogacestat and for one week after the last dose.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
Data:
No pediatric studies are included in this NDA submission; however, the open‐label COG Study
ARST1921 of nirogacestat in pediatric participants with DT is currently ongoing. A signal
assessment was conducted following 2 AE reports (1 of epiphysiolysis and 1 of hip fracture)
occurring in pediatric participants in Study ARST1921 and SpringWorks subsequently identified
2 additional relevant reports in 2 pediatric participants receiving nirogacestat via the
compassionate use program for the treatment of DT (1 event of epiphyseal disorder and
1 event of osteonecrosis). At the time of the signal assessment analysis through the
13 December 2021 data cutoff, 26 pediatric participants have been exposed to nirogacestat in
the compassionate use program (N = 8) and in the COG Study ARST1921 (N = 18). The total of
4 events occurring in 4 participants out of 26 participants is higher than would be expected in
the healthy pediatric population (Peck et al. 2017; Palocaren 2018; Perry et al. 2018; Duffy et al.
2020).
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety and effectiveness of nirogacestat have not been established in pediatric patients.
Epiphyseal disorder, manifesting as a widening of the epiphyseal growth plate, has been
reported in pediatric participants with open growth plates treated with nirogacestat. Increased
physeal cartilage thickening and retention of the hypertrophic zone of the growth plate have
(b) (4)
been observed in rodents in animal studies with nirogacestat.

The available data support recognizing epiphyseal disorder as a potential risk for nirogacestat in
pediatric participants with open growth plates, and the possible impact of this event on bone
growth supports designating this potential risk as an important risk for pediatric participants.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The pediatric section of product labeling will contain a statement that the safety and
effectiveness of nirogacestat have not been established in pediatric patients. Epiphyseal
disorder, manifesting as a widening of the epiphyseal growth plate, has been reported in
pediatric patients with open growth plates treated with nirogacestat.
The Children’s Oncology Group (COG) is conducting study ARTST1921 to characterize the effects
of nirogacestat in children with DT (> 1 year of age). SpringWorks will submit the results of this
study as a PMR with a projected study completion date milestone of September 2025. The
results of this study may further inform labeling with respect to risk and benefit in younger
patients with DT.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
Data:
The NIR‐DT‐301 protocol defined overdose as any dose greater than 450 mg daily within a
24‐hour period. The largest treatment dose of nirogacestat administered to oncology
participants is 330 mg BID in Study A8641014 during the dose‐finding stage of development,
which 3 participants with solid tumors received. No Grade 4 or 5 TEAEs were reported by
participants with solid tumors who received this dose. Dose‐limiting toxicities were evaluable in
2 participants which included Grade 3 rash in 1 participant and Grade 1 palpitations and
Grade 1 oropharyngeal pain in the other participant. TEAEs for all 3 participants with solid
tumors who received this dose included the following PTs in more than one participant:
diarrhea (2, Grade 1 and 2), rash (2, Grade 1 and 3), vomiting (2, both Grade 2).
Single doses of nirogacestat larger than these amounts should be managed by close
observation and supportive measures as needed. Due to very high protein binding, dialysis is
not anticipated to be effective for removing nirogacestat from the blood in participants with
normal serum protein levels.
The potential for drug abuse, withdrawal, and rebound were assessed by reviewing reported
TEAEs in the nervous system and psychiatric disorders SOCs for the presence of events that
would suggest that nirogacestat has psychoactive properties, and for events that are included
in the drug abuse and dependence SMQ. No TEAEs suggested nirogacestat could cause a
psychoactive effect that could lead to drug abuse, physical dependence, or symptoms of
withdrawal.
The Applicant’s Position:
No potential for drug abuse, withdrawal, or rebound has been identified for nirogacestat.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees.

Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees as this is an NME.

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
The Applicant’s Position:
Toxicities appear to have been adequately represented in the studies with nirogacestat.
Potential safety concerns beyond the risks described in the proposed labeling are not expected.
Enhanced pharmacovigilance will be conducted to monitor for OD events and routine
pharmacovigilance will be conducted to monitor for unexpected adverse events.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general FDA agrees; however, uncertainty exists with respect to long‐term ovarian toxicity.

Integrated Assessment of Safety
Data
The most frequently (> 30%) reported TEAEs in Study NIR‐DT‐301 and the All DT Pool were
diarrhea, nausea, fatigue, hypophosphatemia, and rash maculopapular, which are expected
with GS inhibition (Krop et al. 2012; Tolcher et al. 2012; Richter et al. 2014;
Messersmith et al. 2015; Pant et al. 2016) and were generally aligned with the most commonly
reported events in the All CM Pool and OLE Group.
Consistent with the NIR‐DT‐301 study, most participants in the All DT Pool reported their first
TEAE during Cycle 1 (98% in the nirogacestat 150 mg BID group).
The incidences of TEAEs of Grade 3 occurring in the groups receiving nirogacestat 150 mg BID in
the All DT, All CM Pool, and OLE Group were generally aligned with the population in Study
NIR‐DT‐301, with diarrhea and hypophosphatemia being the most frequently reported Grade 3
TEAEs across all groups.
Nirogacestat was not considered as a cause of death for any reported event. The SAE data did
not reveal any additional events with causality attributable to nirogacestat that were not
already identified during the TEAE review. No new safety signals were observed in reviewing
treatment‐related SAEs.
Events leading to treatment discontinuation and occurring in > 1 participant in the All DT Pool
and All CM Pool were consistent with what was observed in Study NIR‐DT‐301. The most
frequently (occurring in > 1 participant in the nirogacestat 150 mg arm) reported PTs resulting
in treatment discontinuation were diarrhea (4 participants), ALT increased (3 participants),
premature menopause (3 participants), and AST increased (2 participants).
The following events are recognized as important identified risks for nirogacestat because of
their increased incidence relative to placebo and also the potential impact to patients: OD,
hidradenitis suppurative, folliculitis, rash maculopapular, hypophosphatemia, hypokalemia,
241
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

elevated transaminases, and diarrhea. With the exception of OD, these events have been
observed in prior studies with GSIs and are expected events associated with gamma secretase
inhibition (Krop et al. 2012; Tolcher et al. 2012; Richter et al. 2014; Messersmith et al. 2015;
Pant et al. 2016). OD emerged as a new safety signal during the NIR‐DT‐301 study given the
younger participation population included in studies of DT as compared to prior studies of other
GSIs that were predominantly conducted in an older participant population for the treatment of
Alzheimer’s disease (Coric et al. 2012; Doody et al. 2013; Henley et al. 2014).
The following PTs were reported at an increased incidence relative to placebo and are
recognized as identified risks, but not designated as important risks as these events were
generally Grade 1 or 2 and did not result in discontinuation of study treatment: stomatitis, skin
rashes (excluding rash maculopapular), alopecia, nausea, fatigue, epistaxis, URTI, cough,
dyspnea, headache, proteinuria, and glucosuria.
The clinical data from the studies discussed in this NDA support epiphyseal disorder being
recognized as a potential risk for nirogacestat in pediatric patients with open growth plates.
Nonclinical data support embryofetal toxicity being recognized as an important potential risk
(b) (4)
for nirogacestat
. Impaired fertility in
both male and female rats were also observed; however, effects on male and female fertility in
humans are unknown.
The following relevant observations were made, but they do not represent adverse clinical
findings: increased eosinophil values, increased basophil/leukocyte ratio, and increased
eosinophil/leukocyte ratio.
No clinically significant changes in chemistry (other than hypophosphatemia, hypokalemia, and
elevated transaminases), vital signs, ECG, or ECOG score data were observed.
The most clinically impactful identified risks associated with the use of nirogacestat 150 mg BID
in the treatment of DT are OD, hidradenitis suppurative, folliculitis, rash maculopapular,
hypophosphatemia, hypokalemia, elevated transaminases, and diarrhea. Except for OD and
elevated liver transaminases, concomitant medications were frequently used to manage these
events. Severe instances of these events were also managed with dose interruptions, followed
by dose reductions in some instances. OD was observed to resolve both with continued dosing
of nirogacestat and in all participants with drug discontinuation and did not appear to be
associated with prolonged estrogen suppression. None of these events, nor any of the other
risks associated with the use of nirogacestat, in isolation or in aggregate, would preclude the
use of nirogacestat 150 mg BID for the treatment of patients with DT.
