The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, the safety population included in Study NIR‐DT‐301 and the All DT Pool is
representative of the intended real‐world population of patients with DT (Constantinidou 2012;
Penel 2017; DTWG 2020; Anneburg 2022; Federman 2022). Therefore, the observed safety
profile described in this SCS can be reasonably expected to reflect the safety profile when
nirogacestat is used to treat adult patients with DT. No aspects of the observed safety profile
would preclude the use of nirogacestat for treating adult patients with DT and the benefit:risk
profile is favorable.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Treatment with nirogacestat resulted in frequent gastrointestinal toxicity, ovarian toxicity (in
premenopausal women), dermatological toxicity, electrolyte wasting, and LFT abnormalities. In
general, these adverse events were manageable with supportive care or dose modifications.
Adverse events resulted in permanent discontinuation in 20% of patients, so a subset of
patients may not be able to tolerate nirogacestat. Although there is uncertainty with respect to
reversibility of ovarian toxicity (and a PMR will be requested), most adverse events appeared to
be reversable following discontinuation. Furthermore, most adverse events were Grade 1 or 2
in severity; however, 16% of patients experienced Grade 3 diarrhea.
Although nirogacestat can cause moderate toxicity in some patients, DT is a rare disease
without available systemic therapies. Approval of nirogacestat will allow for an additional
treatment option in addition to local therapies, and prescribers (generally oncologists with
expertise in sarcomas) can consider the optimal treatment for each patient (preferably in a
multi‐disciplinary setting). If not tolerated, most patients can discontinue without sequalae.
Although nirogacestat can cause toxicity, patients also may benefit from treatment (e.g., due to
pain reduction).

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
Statistical Issues
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA evaluation of efficacy of nirogacestat was primarily based on the pivotal Study NIR‐DT‐
301. Study NIR‐DT‐301 showed statistically significant improvements in PFS and ORR for
nirogacestat compared to placebo. It should be noted that the primary PFS endpoint included
both clinical and radiographic progression as assessed by blinded independent review. A
sensitivity analysis of PFS based only on radiographic progression was consistent with the
primary analysis of PFS. Although the PRO endpoints showed statistical significance, the
estimates may not be reliable due to the large amount of missing data at cycle 10 and
243
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

differential missingness between arms. However, additional analyses conducted at earlier
timepoints using patient‐reported pain were supportive of the primary PFS results. Overall, FDA
concluded that a clinically meaningful benefit for nirogacestat was demonstrated.

Conclusions and Recommendations
The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on the totality of evidence, the multidisciplinary review teams recommend granting
regular approval of nirogacestat 150 mg BID for the treatment of adult patients with
progressing desmoid tumors who require systemic treatment. FDA recommends approval of
nirogacestat for patients for whom systemic therapy is recommended because consensus
guidelines recommend initial active surveillance for patients who do not need immediate
treatment (e.g., vital organ involvement, and intolerable symptoms) (DTWG 2020, NCCN
2.2023). For patients with DT who do need to initiate treatment, management is heterogenous,
but systemic therapy is sometimes preferred over local therapy, such as surgery or
radiotherapy (DTWG 2020).
The Applicant submitted data from 3 trials, among which Study NIR‐DT‐301 was the pivotal,
randomized, placebo‐controlled trial, and the remaining two trials (Study A8641014, and Study
14‐C‐0007) provided supporting data.
Study NIR‐DT‐301 demonstrated that nirogacestat 150 mg BID provided clinically meaningful
and statistically significant improvement in PFS, where the median was not reached (NR, 95%
CI: NE, NE), compared to placebo 15.1 months (95% CI: 8.4, NE) in patients with progressive DT
based on RECIST 1.1. The ORR was also statistically different between the two arms with 41%
(95% CI: 30, 54) and 8% (95% CI: 3, 17) in the nirogacestat, and placebo arms, respectively. The
responses were durable with a median duration that was NR in both arms, NR (95% CI: NE, NE)
with nirogacestat, and NR (95% CI: 8.3, NE) with placebo. Patient‐reported pain improvement in
the nirogacestat arm in patients experiencing baseline pain ≥2 supports the PFS and ORR
results. Because DT is rarely fatal and the safety data for nirogacestat indicated that it would
not lead to safety‐related deaths, OS was not formally tested.

10 PEDIATRICS
The Applicant’s Position:
Reference is made to the Agreed iPSP letter (25 April 2022). The iPSP outlines a request for a
partial product specific waiver for the subset of the pediatric population from birth to less than
24 months of age. In addition, the iPSP includes a deferral request for the results of Study
ARST1921 to be submitted post‐approval.
Study ARST1921 is an ongoing Phase 2 study to evaluate the safety, PK, and efficacy of
nirogacestat in children and adolescents with progressive, surgically unresectable DT.
The ARST1921 study was activated in September 2020 and is expected to take approximately
3 years to accrue. Participants will be followed for 2 years to evaluate the 2‐year PFS rate
allowing for the primary analysis including PFS in approximately 2026.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA granted the partial waiver request of PREA requirements for pediatric patients with DT
from birth to less than 24 months of age. FDA will grant a deferral of the PREA requirements for
patients aged 2 to 17 years where SpringWorks will submit the results of ARST1921 in the post‐
approval setting as a PMR (see Section 13 below).
An initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) was agreed upon between FDA and SpringWorks on April
25, 2022, for a partial waiver of PREA requirements for patients 0‐<2 years of age and a deferral
245
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

in older pediatric patients awaiting the results of Children’s Oncology Group (COG) Study
ARST1921, which will be submitted post‐approval. ARST1921 is an ongoing Phase 2 study
evaluating the safety, pharmacokinetics, and efficacy of nirogacestat in pediatric patients >1 to
<18 years‐old with progressive, surgically unresectable DT. Study ARST1921 was activated in
September 2020 and was expected to take approximately 3 years to accrue; per
clinicaltrials.gov, updated on 11/22/2023, this study is active but no longer accruing patients.
Patients are being followed for 2 years to evaluate outcomes including safety.
Although Study ARST1921 allowed for the enrollment of patients above one year of age (rather
than 2 years as described in the Waiver of the PREA requirement for patients ages 0‐<2), FDA
agreed with the waiver for children under age 2 years given the following:


Recent research and guidelines espousing a conservative strategy for desmoid‐type
fibromatosis in children (Orbach D et al. 2017, DTWG Guidelines 2020);



COG study requirements for non‐resectable tumors, progressing tumors, and previous
systemic treatment;



Observed risk of widening of growth plates, and theoretical risk on organ maturation
(however, this risk would be solely based on non‐clinical embryonic studies of the
NOTCH pathway and not on data observed in patients), and



Age distribution of 59 patients (over 63 months) in prior COG trial ARST0321 (Skapek SX,
et al, 2013) with 78% of 59 patients being 10 years of age or older (additionally
ARST0321 did not require previous systemic treatment).

Given the above factors, enrollment of a sufficient number of patients younger than 24 months
of age would be highly impracticable for this rare disease.

13 POSTMARKETING REQUREMENTS AND COMMITMENT
The FDA’s Assessment:
As a Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) (21 U.S.C. 355c) PMR as described above in this
review, SpringWorks will submit the results of the following study that will assess safety and
anti‐DT effects in the pediatric population:
4550‐1 ‐ Complete clinical Study ARST1921, “A Safety, Pharmacokinetic and Efficacy
Study of a gamma Secretase Inhibitor, Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014), in Children and
Adolescents with Progressive, Surgically Unresectable Desmoid Tumors” in pediatric
patients to further characterize the safety, pharmacokinetics, and anti‐tumor activity of
nirogacestat in pediatric patients 2 to 17 years with desmoid tumors. The study will
include long‐term monitoring to characterize epiphyseal disorders and ovarian toxicity
in pediatric patients.

As discussed above and in the consult from DUOG, FDA is requiring SpringWorks, under Section
505(o)(3) of the FDCA to conduct a clinical trial to further assess the known serious risk of
ovarian toxicity. As stated in the consult from DUOG, there were limitations of the DeFi design
in the selected patient population, timing of reproductive hormone measurements, missing
laboratory values, definitions of adverse events, and criteria to designate recovery. Given these
limitations, a clinical trial was needed to ensure proper selection of patients, documentation of
adverse events, assessment of labs (including timing), and recovery. SpringWorks will conduct
the following trial:
4550‐2 ‐ Conduct a prospective, open‐label, single‐arm clinical trial in adult
premenopausal females with desmoid tumor to further characterize the incidence and
severity of ovarian toxicity after a minimum of 12 months of nirogacestat treatment.
Ensure that ovarian safety assessments after 12 months of continuous treatment are
available from a minimum of 40 subjects. Post‐treatment follow‐up assessments
including reproductive hormone measurements should be obtained at regularly
scheduled intervals and after treatment cessation to assess time to ovarian function
recovery.

Financial Disclosure
The Applicant’s Position:
See Sections 8.1.1 through Section 8.1.7 for financial disclosure information.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant submitted a Financial Interests and Arrangements of Clinical Investigators
Certification for Study NIR‐DT‐301, which stated that the Applicant did not enter into any
financial arrangements with the clinical investigators. However, FDA inspection revealed that
four investigators had financial arrangements, none of which were disclosed by the Applicant.
The FDA clinical reviewer requested the Applicant to provide details regarding these financial
arrangements. After reviewing the Applicant’s response, FDA concluded that the financial
disclosure forms submitted by the Applicant were accurate, and that no investigators had
financial interests or arrangements that were disclosable as defined in 21CFR54.2. See Section
4.1 for more details.

Bioanalytical Review
OSIS Executive Summary
There are thirteen clinical studies submitted to support this NDA, in which eleven clinical
studies include pharmacokinetic (PK) data/information. OSIS reviewed the following method
validation and bioanalytical reports which support eleven of the clinical studies. This review
focuses on the data quality and reliability of (a) four method validations for the quantification
of nirogacestat in human serum and human urine, (b) one method validation for the
262
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

quantification of [14C]‐nirogacestat in human serum, and (c) fifteen bioanalytical reports
associated with eleven clinical studies.
OSIS finds (1) the results of 32 samples from bioanalytical study A2007079.01 are unreliable
due to analysis outside of the established long‐term stability window and (2) limited details
were provided in the method validation 193/006V to support the quantification of 14C‐
nirogacestat in human serum. These concerns related to bioanalytical report A2007079.01 (for
clinical study NIR‐DT‐301) and method validation 193/006V (for clinical study NIR‐DT‐102) have
been communicated to the Office of Clinical Pharmacology (OCP) review team.

OSIS Review
The Applicant submitted method validation and bioanalytical reports for measuring
nirogacestat (originally PF‐03084014) and other analytes of interest (Table 59; data from the
Summary of Biopharmaceutic Studies and Associated Analytical Methods in Table 3 of Module
2.7.1). Thirteen method validations were provided: six were for measuring nirogacestat in
different biological matrix (three in serum, two in cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), and one in urine),
one was for measuring [14C]‐nirogacestat in serum, and six were for measuring the drugs
midazolam, docetaxel, gemcitabine, dabigatran, nab‐paclitaxel, and itraconazole in plasma.

tolerability, pharmacokinetics (PK) and pharmacodynamics (PD).

OSIS Reviewer’s Assessment
Method validation (DM2006‐03084014‐025) appears appropriate for quantifying nirogacestat
in human serum for bioanalytical studies DM2007‐03084014‐028 and DM2007‐03084014‐
029, which appear to be acceptable to support clinical studies A8641001 and A8641008.
(2) Method Validation Report AR0308A

is presented in Table 62 below. This method validation was used to support bioanalytical
reports 25933, 28585, 30854, 31325, 34408, and 34559‐1. These bioanalytical studies are used

clinical study A8641016, Phase 1/2 clinical study A8641019, Phase 1 clinical study NIR‐DT‐101,
and Phase 1 clinical study NIR‐DT‐102.

OSIS Reviewer’s Assessment
The method showed the acceptable performance for nirogacestat in the presence of 32,400
ng/mL rifampicin, 2,360 ng/mL digoxin, 300 ng/mL dabigatran etexilate (V2007078.02
Addendum 1), or 4000 ng/mL itraconazole (V2007078.02 Addendum 2). Hemolysis and
lipemia had no notable effect on assay performance. Seven cycles of freeze‐thaw stability
were established and acceptable. For assessments not evaluated in method validation
V2007078.02 (i.e., stock solution stability, long‐term stability), references were made to the
(b) (4)
nirogacestat method validation report A8649007 conducted by
,
which is considered acceptable.
It was noted that in clinical study NIR‐DT‐301, there were 604 human serum samples initially
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
analyzed by

The explanation for reanalysis and
(b) (4)
the dataset of the 604 samples initially analyzed by
were requested in the
Information Request (conveyed on 04/05/2023). The Applicant provided response and
dataset on 04/12/2023. This Reviewer calculated the mean difference of the drug
(b) (4)
and the reanalyzed from
concentration values between the initially analyzed from
(b) (4)
, resulting in 3.5% ± 10.9% SD by using the equation for incurred sample
reanalysis (ISR): (repeat – original)/average * 100. The Information Request response appears
reasonable per the OSIS perspective, but OSIS finds the results of the 32 samples analyzed
outside of the 520‐day long‐term stability unreliable (please see Information Request and
Response section below for details).
Overall, partial method validation V2007078.02 and related amendments appear to be

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Population PK Analysis
Executive Summary
A series of clinical studies (including DDI and ADME studies) have been completed to
characterize the clinical pharmacology of nirogacestat. Nirogacestat was rapidly absorbed and
Tmax was reached between 0.5‐2 hours across all evaluated doses. Nirogacestat
concentration‐time profiles showed multi‐phasic disposition. Following repeat doses of
nirogacestat, steady‐state appears to have been achieved by approximately Day 7. The absolute
bioavailability (F) of nirogacestat following oral administration of a 150 mg dose was 19.2%.
Nirogacestat is a sensitive substrate of CYP3A4 and subject to DDI based on CYP3A4
metabolism. Following administration of 100 mg nirogacestat with itraconazole, a strong
CYP3A4 inhibitor and clinical inhibitor of P‐gp, nirogacestat Cmax and AUCinf were increased by
2.5‐ and 8.2‐fold, respectively, compared to when given alone. Renal excretion of nirogacestat
is negligible (0.020 to 0.042% recovered unchanged in urine).
A PopPK model was developed to support the dose rationale for nirogacestat and is included in
this NDA. This activity included the following objectives:


Develop a PopPK model to characterize the PK of nirogacestat in healthy subjects and
participants;



Evaluate the effect of covariates on the variability of the PK of nirogacestat;



Evaluate the magnitude and clinical relevance, when possible, of the statistically
significant findings from the analysis;



Predict exposures following doses of nirogacestat under scenarios of clinical
importance, if applicable

Methods: Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was used to develop the PopPK model using data
from healthy volunteers and oncology participants following single or multiple dose oral
administration of nirogacestat. Model building started with a simple one compartment,
first‐order absorption model and increased step‐wise in complexity until further improvement
in fit was not supported by the data. The impact of structural covariates (e.g. dose and DDI),
study‐specific parameters and individual subject characteristics (e.g. body weight and
participant population) were assessed using manual forward addition/backward elimination.
The final model was qualified by numerical and graphical goodness of fit (GOF) checks, including
visual predictive checks (VPCs).

285
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

Results: PK data from 335 healthy adult volunteers and oncology participants, collected in six
healthy volunteer (A8641001, A8641002, A8641008, NIR‐DT‐101, NIR‐DT‐102, and NIR‐DT‐103)
and two oncology participant studies (A8641014 and NIR‐DT‐301), were available for the PopPK
analysis. The disposition and elimination of nirogacestat in serum was best described by a
three‐compartment model with lag‐time, first‐order absorption kinetics after oral
administration.
Clearance (CL) was 9.09 L/h in healthy volunteers and participants with DT. In participants with
solid tumors or T‐ALL/LBL, CL was lower and estimated to be 4.4 L/h. Estimates for central
volume of distribution (Vc), peripheral volume of distribution ‐ shallow compartment (Vp1) and
peripheral volume of distribution – deep compartment (Vp2) were 7.74 L, 11.5 L, and 115 L,
respectively.
Following oral administration, nirogacestat was rapidly absorbed through a lag‐time, first‐order
absorption process. Absorption in Study NIR‐DT‐301 was slower (absorption rate constant
(ka) = 0.38 h‐1) compared to the Phase 1 studies (ka = 0.876 h‐1).
In addition to the participant‐specific elimination, body weight, CYP3A4 inhibition, and dose
were each found to have a statistically significant influence on the nirogacestat PK. Vp2 was
found to increase proportionally with weight. Nirogacestat was subject to CYP3A4‐based DDI
with approximately 4.7‐fold lower CL (CL = 1.94 L/h) and F increased 2‐fold (absolute F = 38%)
following co‐administration with the strong CYP3A4 inhibitor itraconazole. Other
co‐medications did not appear to affect nirogacestat PK, but inference may be inconclusive
given the overall low number of subjects.
A trend between F and dose was seen at lower doses. F was found to be dose‐dependent and
asymptotically increased to a value of 19.2% as dose levels increased. The dose‐dependent
effect was not meaningful at doses equal or greater than 10 mg.
Conclusions:


Nirogacestat PK was adequately characterized by a three‐compartment, lag‐time, first‐
order absorption model.



Study‐phase specific differences in ka and residual variability were observed, with
slower nirogacestat absorption, shorter Tlag, and a distinct error model for Study NIR‐
DT‐301 – residual variability was also dependent on the bioanalytical lab used for serum
quantification.



Administration of nirogacestat with a powder‐in‐bottle formulation in early Phase 1
studies resulted in a shorter Tlag and lower F as compared to tablet formulations.



CL was lower in participants with solid tumors or T‐ALL/LBL (4.4 L/h) compared to
healthy volunteers and participants with DT (9.09 L/h).
286
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)



Vp2 was found to increase proportionally with weight.



Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors affected nirogacestat PK, with approximately 4.7‐fold lower CL
and 2‐fold higher F following itraconazole co‐administration.



F was dose‐dependent and asymptotically increased with increasing doses. For doses
equal or greater than 20 mg, F converged to a value of 19.2%.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees.

PPK Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
The disposition and elimination of nirogacestat in serum is best described by a
three‐compartment model with lag‐time and first‐order absorption kinetics after oral
administration. Key clinically relevant covariate relationships described include an effect for
CYP3A4 inhibition on CL and F, weight on the peripheral volume compartments, and participant
population on CL. Notably, the PK of nirogacestat in participants with DT was more similar to
that observed in healthy volunteers than to nirogacestat PK described in participants with
advanced solid tumors (other than DT) and T‐ALL/LBL (collectively referred to as advanced
malignancies for the PopPK analyses). This difference was explained by a 52% reduction in the
fixed effects estimate for CL in participants with advanced malignancies relative to healthy
volunteers and participants with DT.

extensively metabolized by CYP3A4, with

Specific Populations
Effects of Age, Gender and Race
A PopPK analysis of nirogacestat was
performed, which found no clinically
meaningful effect of age, gender, or race
on the PK of nirogacestat.
Effects of Hepatic Impairment

Based on PPK assessment, CYP3A
strong inhibitors (i.e.,
itraconazole) is expected to have
clinically meaningful impact on
exposure (9.3 folds higher) and is
recommended against
concomitant use.

The PK of nirogacestat were evaluated in
participants with moderate to severe
hepatic impairment based on Child‐Pugh
classification and moderate hepatic
impairment based on NCI‐ODWG
classification. Participants classified as
moderate by Child‐Pugh classification
with total bilirubin levels 1.5 × ULN or
lower (i.e., those that did not meet the
criteria for moderate impairment by NCI‐
ODWG) did not exhibit significant
differences in nirogacestat PK. Some
participants that met the criteria for
moderate impairment by NCI‐ODWG
(total bilirubin > 1.5 × ULN) exhibited
lower Cmax, higher volume of distribution,
longer half‐lives, and greater overall
exposure compared to healthy controls.
Therefore, nirogacestat is
contraindicated in patients diagnosed
with moderate or severe hepatic
impairment per NCI‐ODWG criteria (total
bilirubin > 1.5 × ULN).
Effects of Renal Impairment
The effects of renal impairment on
nirogacestat PK have not been evaluated
in a clinical trial; however, in a PopPK
model no meaningful relationship was
observed between renal function tests
and nirogacestat PK. Clinical studies
determined that nirogacestat is
extensively metabolized, and no major
metabolites were found in urine. Only
small amounts of unchanged
nirogacestat were detected in the urine.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)
Drug Interaction Studies
Effect of other drugs on nirogacestat
Nirogacestat is a sensitive CYP3A4
substrate. In vitro studies with human
liver microsomes indicated that
nirogacestat is primarily metabolized by
the drug‐metabolizing enzyme CYP3A4
(85%). The effect of CYP3A4 inhibitors on
nirogacestat was evaluated with
itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor,
in healthy participants. Using clinical and
in vitro data, a mechanistic PBPK model
was developed to simulate interaction
scenarios with various CYP3A4 inhibitors
and inducers.
Co‐administration of nirogacestat with
strong and moderate inhibitors of
CYP3A4 may increase serum nirogacestat
concentrations exceeding those observed
at the maximum tolerated dose.
Co‐administration of nirogacestat with
strong and moderate inducers of CYP3A4
may decrease serum nirogacestat
concentration to poorly effective levels.
Co‐administration of nirogacestat with
strong CYP3A4 inducers may result in
negligible nirogacestat exposure. Co‐
administration of nirogacestat with
moderate CYP3A inducers may result in
significant decrease of nirogacestat
exposure, but not to a negligible level.
In vitro permeability studies and
knockout mouse studies indicate that
nirogacestat may be a substrate of the P‐
gp transporter. Since itraconazole may
also inhibit P‐gp, the results of the clinical
evaluation with itraconazole may also be
in part due to P‐gp inhibition. CYP3A4
inhibition, however, is the dominant
interaction.
The solubility of nirogacestat is
substantially reduced at pH greater than
6.0. No clinical studies have been
conducted to evaluate the effects of
drugs that increase gastric pH, such as
proton pump inhibitors or H2‐receptor
antagonists, on nirogacestat; however,
co‐administration of these drugs with

nirogacestat may reduce the
bioavailability of nirogacestat.
Effect of nirogacestat on other drugs
The effects of nirogacestat on
midazolam, a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate,
were evaluated in clinical studies of
healthy participants. Using clinical and in
vitro data, a mechanistic PBPK model was
developed to simulate interaction
scenarios with substrates of CYP3A4,
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19.
Nirogacestat is a weak inhibitor of
CYP3A4 and administration of
nirogacestat may increase the exposure
of CYP3A substrates. No meaningful
effects were predicted for nirogacestat
on substrates of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and
CYP2C9.
PBPK simulations predicted minimal‐to‐
weak induction of CYP2C19, however a
sensitivity analysis under worst‐case
conditions predicted potential for
moderate induction. Effects of
nirogacestat on CYP2C19 substrates have
not been evaluated in a clinical study;
however, weak‐to‐moderate induction
may be possible.

Exposure‐Response Analysis
ER (efficacy) Executive Summary
PK/PDx models were developed to evaluate the PK/PDx and exposure‐response relationship
(ERR) between nirogacestat and efficacy endpoints in participants with DT, which included the
following objectives:


Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and changes in tumor
size;



Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and PFS;



Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and BOR and DOR;

Methods: The previously developed nirogacestat PopPK model was used to derive individual
exposure parameters. Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was employed to develop PKPDx
models to characterize safety and efficacy of nirogacestat in participants with DT, participants
with solid tumors other than DT, and participants with T‐ALL/LBL. The binary outcome for PFS
was analyzed using a time to event model, whereas tumor size was analyzed as a continuous
variable. Model development was carried out sequentially, first describing disease progression
and placebo response, followed by assessment of the nirogacestat ERR. Covariate‐parameter
relationships of interest were explored graphically and formally tested if substantial trends
were evident. Final PKPDx models were qualified by VPCs, in addition to numerical checks of
parameter precision and model robustness.
Bayesian posthoc PK parameter estimates were used to estimate nirogacestat exposures
(nominal steady state or exposure at day of onset) for the subsequent exposure‐response
analysis. For each safety endpoint, nominal steady state exposure was used for participants
with no event. To assess the impact of changes in exposure for DOR and BOR, exposure‐
301
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

response relationships were graphically evaluated in all participants and in nirogacestat treated
participants only. Significant relationships were further examined by comparing treatment
effect models (i.e., to assess response for nirogacestat treated participants compared to
placebo participants) to exposure‐response models (i.e., to assess changes in response as
nirogacestat concentration changes). DOR was evaluated using exploratory Kaplan‐Meier plots
and BOR, was evaluated by logistic regression.
Results: A total of 212 participants were available for PFS evaluation. Of those,
150 (70.8%) were participants with DT. The hazard for DT, as a measure of PFS, was 19‐fold
lower than for other tumors. PFS probability was associated with nirogacestat treatment,
independent of nirogacestat exposures, at the evaluated dose range. The PFS HR estimate for
treatment relative to no treatment was 0.26. Age and sex were not associated with PFS.
Changes in tumor size over time were characterized by RECIST (DT and other tumors) and
tumor volume (DT only) models comprising a first‐order tumor growth rate, which was flexible
to describe both disease progression and regression in the tumor volume model, and a
first‐order tumor shrinkage rate. Nirogacestat‐mediated tumor regression was described by an
on‐off treatment effect. An ERR between nirogacestat AUC and tumor sizes could not be
established at the evaluated nirogacestat exposure ranges. Tumor size was predicted to change
by ‐22% (RECIST sum of longest diameter) and ‐55% (tumor volume) following treatment with
nirogacestat at doses ≥ 100 mg BID for 24 months. Tumor type, APC tumor suppressor
genotype and β‐catenin genotype (CTNNB1) were not associated with change in tumor size.
Thirty‐six nirogacestat‐treated participants (17.3%) and 5 placebo participants (2.4%) were
found to have a CR or PR. A statistically significant negative trend was found for nominal Cmax,
Cmin, AUC at steady state only when placebo participants were excluded from evaluation,
indicating that responses occur at lower exposures compared to non‐responders across in the
nirogacestat treated groups. However, the negative trend was driven by the lack of efficacy and
higher exposures observed in patients with non‐DT tumor types. The BOR relationship with
nominal steady state nirogacestat exposure was not found to be significant in participants with
DT alone. Higher exposure did appear to correlate with a longer response, though exposure was
not a statistically significant predictor of duration of response.
Conclusion: This analysis represents a comprehensive examination of PKPDx data collected
from a Phase 1 trial of nirogacestat in participants with advanced solid tumors and participants
with relapsed or refractory T‐ALL/LBL, and a Phase 3 trial of nirogacestat in participants with
DT. Principal findings of the efficacy analyses are described below.
Efficacy:


For PFS, the HR estimate for solid tumors and T‐ALL/LBL relative to DT tumors was 19.
The PFS probability for all tumors was not associated with nirogacestat exposures at the

ER (efficacy) Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
Exposure‐response and PKPDx analyses were conducted using a nirogacestat PopPK model and
response data obtained from Studies A8641014 and NIR‐DT‐301. PKPDx analyses characterized
nirogacestat‐mediated treatment effects for PFS and tumor size that were independent of
pharmacological exposure at the evaluated dose ranges. For analyses of nirogacestat exposure
and tumor size, an exposure‐response plateau was achieved within the exposure ranges
predicted for the ≥ 100 mg BID to 150 mg BID dose range. A significant relationship between
nirogacestat exposure and OR was not identified at the evaluated dose ranges. There was an
apparent trend towards longer responses in the higher exposure group; however, it was not
found to be a statistically significant predictor of DOR.

from the final PopPK model added to the PKPDx

The treatment effect and exposure‐response models
were parameterized as follows (shown here including
covariate effects):
Efficacy analysis:

was characterized
by a first‐order
tumor growth
(evaluated with
placebo data) and
first order
elimination by
nirogacestat
treatment.

Treatment effect: h(t) = h0(t) * exp(Effect * Inirogacestat +
b1 * (Cov‐median) + b2i * ICovi)
Exposure‐response: h(t) = h0(t) * exp(α * (Exposure‐
Median Exposure) + b1 * (Cov‐median) + b2i * ICovi)
where h0(t) is the hazard in the participant with
nominal covariate effects. For the treatment effect
model, h0(t) is the hazard for placebo‐treated
participants. For the exposure‐response model, h0(t)
is the hazard for participants with median exposure.
Relative risk (h(t)=h0(t)) is provided by the righthand‐
side of the equations for specified treatment or
exposure and covariate values. Exposure is the
estimated nominal steady state AUC for each
nirogacestat‐treated participant and 0 for placebo‐
treated participants, and α is the parameter estimate
of the slope of the exposure‐response relationship.
Inirogacestat is a binary variable with value 0 for placebo
participants and value 1 for nirogacestat treated
participants. Effect is the parameter estimate of the
treatment effect of nirogacestat over placebo. For
continuous covariates, a slope (b1) was estimated for
the median‐centered covariate (Cov).
For categorical covariates, a separate effect (b2i) was
estimated for each categorical value of the
covariate (ICovi).

ER (safety) Executive Summary
PK/PDx models were developed to evaluate the PK/PDx and exposure‐response relationship
(ERR) between nirogacestat and efficacy endpoints in participants with DT, which included the
following objectives:


Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and changes in FSH
over time;



Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and OD;

312
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)



Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and the incidence of the Grade ≥ 3
diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade ≥ 3 rash, Grade ≥ 3 treatment‐related
AEs, any treatment‐related AE, any treatment‐related SAE, any treatment‐related AE
leading to dose modification, Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs, any TEAE, any TESAE, and any TEAE
leading to dose modification ;



Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and the duration of Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia, Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3 rash, and OD.

Methods: The previously developed nirogacestat PopPK model was used to derive individual
exposure parameters. Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was employed to develop PKPDx
models to characterize safety and efficacy of nirogacestat in participants with DT, participants
with solid tumors other than DT, and participants with T‐ALL/LBL. The binary outcome OD was
analyzed using a time to event model, whereas FSH and phosphate maximum reduction from
baseline were analyzed as continuous variables. Model development was carried out
sequentially, first describing disease progression and placebo response, followed by assessment
of the nirogacestat ERR. Covariate‐parameter relationships of interest were explored
graphically and formally tested if substantial trends were evident. Final PKPDx models were
qualified by VPCs, in addition to numerical checks of parameter precision and model
robustness.
Bayesian posthoc PK parameter estimates were used to estimate nirogacestat exposures
(nominal steady state or exposure at day of onset) for the subsequent exposure‐response
analysis. For each safety endpoint, nominal steady state exposure was used for participants
with no event. To assess the impact of changes in exposure for binary safety endpoints,
exposure‐response relationships were graphically evaluated in all participants and in
nirogacestat treated participants only. Significant relationships were further examined by
comparing treatment effect models (i.e., to assess response for nirogacestat treated
participants compared to placebo participants) to exposure‐response models (i.e., to assess
changes in response as nirogacestat concentration changes). Safety endpoints were evaluated
by logistic regression.
Results: A total of 73 WOCBP were available for FSH evaluation, all of which were participants
with DT from Study NIR‐DT‐301. FSH was found to linearly increase with nirogacestat serum
concentrations. The delay between serum concentrations and FSH change was characterized by
an indirect response model. A time‐dependent drug effect was used to characterize the
reduction of FSH observed in some participants. Variability in FSH, drug effect, and time‐
dependent drug effect decay were relatively high and could not be further explained by
covariates.
A total of 82 WOCBP were available for OD evaluations, including 76 participants with DT,
3 with solid tumors other than DT, 2 with T‐ALL/LBL, and 1 participant with a missing
313
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

description for tumor type. Time to OD occurrence was related to nirogacestat treatment,
independent of nirogacestat exposures, at the evaluated dose range. The HR for nominal
treatment relative to placebo was estimated to be 3.3. No relationships between OD and
population or age were observed.
A total of 214 participants were available for evaluation of AE endpoints. Eleven endpoints
were available for safety analysis: Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade ≥ 3
rash, Grade ≥ 3 treatment‐related AEs, any treatment‐related AE, any treatment‐related SAE,
any treatment‐related AE leading to dose modification, Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs, any TEAE, any TESAE,
and any TEAE leading to dose modification. Nirogacestat exposure was found to be a significant
predictor of any Grade ≥ 3 TEAE, though further exposure response models showed that this
was a treatment effect rather than an exposure effect. Of these binary endpoints, only
Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia was found to have a relationship with nirogacestat exposure.
Compared to treatment, Cmax was found to be the best predictor of Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia occurrence. At a 100 mg dose, the probability of a Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be 5.89%. For a 150 mg dose, the probability
of a Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be 6.78%.
Dose modifications, such as dose interruptions (or holds) and dose reductions, during Study
NIR‐DT‐301 DB phase or OLE phase were also compared against nominal nirogacestat exposure
at steady state. Both Cmin and AUC were found to be significant predictors of dose modifications
for NIR‐DT‐301, with higher exposure predictive of a subject decreasing their dose, though Cmax
was not.
Phosphate maximum reduction from baseline was modeled by linear and nonlinear regression.
Treatment effect, linear, exponential, Emax, and sigmoid Emax models were tested with and
without an intercept. Models with an intercept were found to be more significant than those
without due to the non‐zero phosphate change from baseline seen in the placebo group. For
the nirogacestat treated group, at treatment effect model was found to best describe the
relationship of phosphate change from baseline and nirogacestat.
The duration of four endpoints (Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade ≥ 3
rash, and OD) were explored through Kaplan‐Meier plots. No significant ERR relationships were
detected within the range of nirogacestat exposure.
Conclusion: This analysis represents a comprehensive examination of PKPDx data collected
from a Phase 1 study of nirogacestat in participants with advanced solid tumors and
participants with relapsed or refractory T‐ALL/LBL, and a Phase 3 trial of nirogacestat in
participants with DT. Principal findings of the safety analyses are described below:


FSH linearly increased with nirogacestat serum concentrations in WOCBP. The temporal
difference between nirogacestat serum concentrations and FSH changes was described
314
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

by an indirect response model. A reduction of nirogacestat effect on FSH over time was
detected. Although the typical half‐life attenuation was 49 weeks, the effect was
associated with high variability.


A nirogacestat treatment effect on OD occurrence was observed, which was exposure
independent at the evaluated dose range of 80 mg BID to 330 mg BID. The HR for
nominal treatment relative to placebo was estimated to be 3.3.



A statistically significant and plausible ERR (p < 0.05) was observed for Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia. Compared to treatment, Cmax was found to be the best predictor of
Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence. At a 100 mg dose, the probability of a
Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be 5.9%. For a 150 mg dose,
the probability of a Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be
6.78%. No ERRs were observed for the other binary safety events.



A Treatment effect model with an intercept was found to best describe the relationship
of phosphate maximum reduction from baseline and nirogacestat.



Sixty participants were found to have dose modifications during Study NIR‐DT‐301 or
OLE. Nominal nirogacestat exposure at steady state (Cmin and AUC) was found to be a
significant predictor (p < 0.05) of dose modifications.



No ERRs were detected within the range of nirogacestat exposure for the duration of
Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade ≥ 3 rash, or OD.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees. Positive E‐R relationships for Grade 3 hypophosphatemia was well characterized
over the dose range from 20 mg BID to 330 mg BID. We added the following statement in
section 12.2 in the USPI label.
Exposure-Response Relationships
There is an exposure response relationship between OGSIVEO exposure and Grade 3
hypophosphatemia with a higher risk of Grade 3 hypophosphatemia at higher exposure.
Additionally, increased rate of Grade 3 hypophosphatemia occurred more frequently with higher
nirogacestat doses (0.13 to 2.2 times the recommended dose).

ER (safety) Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
Exposure‐response and PKPD analyses were conducted using a nirogacestat PopPK model and
safety data obtained from Studies A8641014 and NIR‐DT‐301. A linear relationship between
FSH and nirogacestat serum concentration in WOCBP was observed. However, high variability in

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

the drug effect, presumably due to menstrual cycle oscillation and sparse sampling, made
precise estimation of drug effects difficult. A nirogacestat treatment effect was described for
OD occurrence, which was also independent of exposure at the evaluated dose ranges.
Significant relationships between nirogacestat exposure and Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia
were described, with a 0.91% absolute increase in risk estimated at the 150 mg dose compared
to the 100 mg dose. Finally, a statistically significant relationship between nirogacestat
exposure (trough concentration and AUCtau) and dose modification was identified; however, the
relationship with Cmax was not statistically significant. This suggests that the need for dose
modifications may be associated with inter‐individual differences in CL. Thus, individuals with
higher nirogacestat CL are less likely to require dose reductions or interruptions than individuals
with lower nirogacestat CL.
General Information
Goal of ER analysis

PKPDx models were developed to evaluate the PKPDx and ERR for
nirogacestat in participants with DT, which included the following
objectives:


Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure
and changes in FSH over time;



Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure
and OD;



Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and the
incidence of the Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia,
Grade ≥ 3 rash, Grade ≥ 3 treatment‐related AEs, any treatment‐
related AE, any treatment‐related SAE, any treatment‐related AE
leading to dose modification, Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs, any TEAE, any
TESAE, and any TEAE leading to dose modification;



Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and
phosphate maximum reduction from baseline;



Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and the
duration of Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea,
Grade ≥ 3 rash, and OD.

oncology participants, NIR‐DT‐301 only included participants with DT.

Model Structure

The relationships between nirogacestat exposure
and safety endpoints were evaluated as PKPDx or
ERR models. The PKPDx model development for
OD and FSH used individual parameter estimates
from the final PopPK model added to the PKPDx
datasets. For the ERR analyses of safety endpoints
evaluated by logistic regression, the final PopPK
model was used to calculate individual estimates
of exposure on the day of onset, as available. For
each safety endpoint, nominal steady state
exposure was used for participants with no event.

Yes.
PK/PD models for AEs
of interest (FSH and
OD) were considered
exploratory for
reasons detailed in
19.4.3.5 ER review
issues.

Logistic regression models tested covariates on the
model intercept only. Covariates were tested in a
stepwise forward search at a p < 0.05 level of
significance. Only covariates that were significant
in a univariate search were included in
multivariate testing. The treatment effect and
exposure‐response models were parameterized as
follows (shown here including covariate effects):
Treatment effect: Pr(Response) = invlogit(Intercept
+ Effect * Inirogacestat + b1 * (Cov‐median) + b2i * ICovi)
Exposure‐response: Pr(Response) =
invlogit(Intercept + Slope * Exposure + b1 * (Cov‐
median) + b2i * ICovi)
where Inirogacestat is a binary variable with value 0 for
placebo participants and value 1 for nirogacestat
treated participants. Effect is the parameter
estimate for the treatment effect of nirogacestat
over placebo. Exposure is the estimated nominal
steady state AUC for each participant treated with
nirogacestat and 0 for placebo participants. Slope
is the parameter estimate of the slope of
exposure‐response relationship. For continuous
covariates, a slope (b1) was estimated for the
median‐centered covariate (Cov). For categorical
covariates, a separate effect (b2i) was estimated
for each categorical value of the covariate (ICovi).
FSH was described with a linear relationship with
nirogacestat serum concentration using an indirect
response model. The probability of OD was
described by a parametric time‐to‐event model
(Gompertz hazard) comprising an effect of
nirogacestat treatment on the hazard. Probability
of Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia and dose
modifications were described by logistic
regression. Phosphate maximum reduction from
baseline was described with a treatment effect
model with an intercept. No ERR could be

Overall Clinical Relevance for
ER



FSH linearly increased with nirogacestat
serum concentrations in WOCBP. The
temporal difference between nirogacestat
serum concentrations and FSH changes was
described by an indirect response model. A
reduction of nirogacestat effect on FSH over
time was detected. Although the typical half‐
life attenuation was 49 weeks, the effect was
associated with high variability.

The relationships
between exposures
and FSH elevation or
ovarian dysfunction
are inconclusive for
reasons detailed in

issues.

Labeling Language



A nirogacestat treatment effect on OD
occurrence was observed, which was
exposure independent at the evaluated dose
range of 80 mg BID to 330 mg BID. The HR for
nominal treatment relative to placebo was
estimated to be 3.3.



A statistically significant and plausible ERR (p
< 0.05) was observed for Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia. Compared to treatment,
Cmax was found to be the best predictor of
Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence. At
a 100 mg dose, the probability of a Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia occurrence was
calculated to be 5.9%. For a 150 mg dose, the
probability of a Grade ≥ 3 hypophosphatemia
occurrence was calculated to be 6.78%. No
ERRs were observed for the other binary
safety events.



A treatment effect model with an intercept
was found to best describe the relationship
of phosphate maximum reduction from
baseline and nirogacestat.



Sixty participants were found to have dose
modifications during Study NIR‐DT‐301 or
OLE. Nominal nirogacestat exposure at
steady state (Cmin and AUC) was found to be
a significant predictor (p < 0.05) of dose
modifications.



No ERRs were detected within the range of
nirogacestat exposure for the duration of
Grade ≥ 3 diarrhea, Grade ≥ 3
hypophosphatemia, Grade ≥ 3 rash, or OD.

R relationship was
also observed for
Grade 3 TEAE.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA verified the Applicant’s analysis and in general agreed with the Applicant’s position. The
PK/PD model for tumor size adequately characterized longitudinal patient data that included
two intra‐patient doses. The observed longitudinal tumor size data supports that efficacy
should not be affected by dose reduction. The PK/PD model was considered reliable to provide
further support for the dose reduction. PK/PD models for FSH level and ovarian dysfunction are
limited to inform dose modification from safety perspective due to sparse/incongruent data.
However, positive E‐R relationships for Grade 3 hypophosphatemia was well characterized over
the dose range from 20 mg BID to 330 mg BID.
Taken together, the longitudinal tumor PK/PD model, positive ER relationship for dose
modification and hypophosphatemia, as well as positive trend in E‐R for FSH and Grade 3+
TEAE, suggest benefits in improving safety and tolerability with a lower exposure.

ER Review Issues
E‐R for ORR with steady‐state exposure
A relatively flat E‐R relationship excluding placebo between steady‐state exposure and
response was observed which could be due to small exposure range for a single dose. Given
dose reduction occurred in about half of the patients with DT, the Applicant was asked to
perform additional E‐R analysis with average exposure till response (for responders) or the end
of treatment (for non‐responders). Similar to Figure 13, a flat E‐R relationship was observed,

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

suggesting that response was retained despite a lower average exposure due to dose reduction
(Figure 24).
Figure 24:

Logistic Regression Relationships for Objective Response for Patients with DT
and Patients with DT treated with Nirogacestat

Source: Response to FDA IR 07/19/2023 Figure 2.
PK/PD for AEs of interest are considered exploratory or inconclusive
PK/PD model for FSH elevation was described by an indirect response model where the drug
effect with a time‐dependent decay was parameterized on the generation of FSH. This model is
limited due to the sparse sampling and lab collected with no regards to monstrous cycle. As a
result, the intra‐ and inter‐ individual variabilities of the model are large, and the model does
not describe the data well. The exploratory analysis for ovarian dysfunction characterized by
FSH level and clinical symptoms is subject to the same issues. The details of these models were
thus not provided in this review. Despite the poor model fitness, there was a clear treatment
effect on inducing FSH level or ovarian dysfunction. Whether the effect was concentration
dependent is inconclusive due to the stated issues.

Reviewer’s Independent Analysis
Efficacy does not appear to be affected by dose reduction
Dose modifications in patients with DT with 150 mg daily nirogacestat were primarily due to
acute Grade 3+ TEAEs such as diarrhea, rash, and hypophosphatemia. Chronic TEAEs including
Grade 2+ premature menopause or primary ovarian insufficiency also led to dose modifications.
As shown in Figure 25, a significant proportion of patients had dose reduction as early as 1st
cycle and this proportion continued to increase over time.

328
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

To assess if efficacy is affected, an exploratory analysis PFS was visualized in patients with dose
reduction which was not found to differ from those whose dose was unchanged (Figure 26). In
an additional exploratory analysis, tumor shrinkage appeared to correlate with PFS (Figure 27).
Tumor size summarized by real‐time dose in Study NIR‐DT‐301 showed comparable slope of
tumor size decrease between patients with and without dose reduction.
These results however do not provide information regarding whether the timing of dose
reduction affects tumor shrinkage. To visualize temporal relationship between dose reduction
and tumor dynamics, patients were further grouped by dose reduction occurring in the
following time frames: <12 weeks, 12‐<24 weeks, 24‐<48 weeks, >=48 weeks. Considering that
tumor size was captured in a time frame for each planned visit and there were nonuniform
missing records amongst patients, reviewer used PK/PD model to simulate tumor size every 4
weeks based on patient’s individual parameter estimates. The predicted tumor dynamic profiles
overlaid with observations for all patients (Figure 28).
For different dose reduction categories (i.e., <12 weeks, 12‐<24 weeks, 24‐<48 weeks, >=48
weeks), the median tumor size reductions over time were summarized. As shown in Figure 29,
all groups that were dose reduced to 100 mg had steeper slopes of tumor size reduction
compared to the group that stayed on 150 mg. This trend was observed with no regards to the
time frame in which dose reduction occurred, suggesting that efficacy was retained with dose
reduction including those occurred early. This observation aligns with the modeling results that
a treatment effect independent of concentrations was identified as a predictor for tumor
dynamics.
Longitudinal PK/PD model for tumor dynamics included two intra‐patient doses and thus data
were considered robust for evaluating concentration dependent effect on tumor shrinkage in
patients with dose reduction. However, one cannot conclude the absence of concentration
dependent response in all patients because the analysis of exposure response is determined by
the data from patients who underwent dose reduction. These patients could have an increased
sensitivity to drug for PK (e.g., high free drug concentrations not reflected by total drug
concentrations) and/or PD (e.g., elevated biochemical characteristics) reasons compared to
other patients whose dose was unchanged.
Given the very high percentage of protein binding for nirogacestat (99.4%), variability in this
percentage may lead to a large difference in free drug concentrations. The possibility of
increased free drug in patients with dose reduction could not be verified since it was not
measured. To evaluate a potential PD difference, known patient factors were modeled as
covariates and none were found statistically significant. For unknown confounding factors,
randomized study evaluating two doses is warranted to interrogate the exposure‐response
relationship. Therefore, the flat exposure response should only be interpreted in the scope of
data used to develop the PK/PD model. Although it does not provide conclusive evidence that a
lower starting dose could be as effective as the 150 mg BID, the flat ER relationship observed in

the PK/PD model for tumor size, along with observed data supports maintenance of efficacy in
patients who need dose reduction in response to AEs. The onset of AEs may have reflected the
possibly increased sensitivity to the drug whether it is attributed to PK and/or PD differences in
those patients.

Overall Benefit‐Risk Evaluation based on E‐R Analyses
The Applicant’s Position:
Exposure‐response relationships were explored for several efficacy and safety endpoints using
data from Studies A8641014 and NIR‐DT‐301, and the final model from the PopPK Analysis. An
332
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

exposure‐response plateau was achieved within the predicted exposure ranges (AUCtau and
Cmin) for the ≥ 100 mg to 150 mg dose range. Reduction in tumor size was independent of
nirogacestat exposure within the evaluated dose range, whether it was determined by
volumetric MRI (NIR‐DT‐301 only) or using RECIST (by CT or MRI). Similarly, there was a
treatment effect relationship with PFS that was independent of exposure within the evaluated
dose ranges. Additional analyses were conducted to explore if a relationship between
nirogacestat exposure and objective response could be described; however, exposure was not
found to be a significant predictor of duration of response due to low number of participants
with loss of response.
A statistically significant relationship was found between exposure and dose modifications.
Participants with higher predicted exposures (AUCtau and Cmin) at steady‐state were more likely
to have had dose modifications than those with lower steady‐state exposures. Given that the
majority of dose modifications were due to TEAEs, this relationship may suggest that
participants with lower nirogacestat clearance were more likely to experience TEAEs that
required a dose modification.
The exposure‐response data support a recommended starting dose of 150 mg for all patients
with allowance for dose modification based on tolerability to ensure optimal exposure for each
individual.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees.

Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic Modeling Analysis
Executive Summary
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s following PBPK
report to support the intended uses.
“Development of a PBPK model for nirogacestat with the Simcyp population‐based simulator
and subsequent evaluation of DDI liability as a victim and perpetrator of CYP3A4.”
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report and supporting modeling files
and concluded the followings.
o The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the nirogacestat PK profiles following a single
intravenous dose (0.0435 mg), a single oral dose administration (1, 2.5, 5, 10, 25, 40, 50, 65
120 and 150 mg), or multiple oral dose administration (7 and 40 mg BID) in healthy subjects
333
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

and multiple oral dose administration (20, 40, 80, 100, 130, 150 and 220 mg BID) in cancer
patients.
o The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of itraconazole and ketoconazole
(strong CYP3A4 inhibitors) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration
of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). Model predicted nirogacestat geometric mean AUC
ratio was around 5‐7‐fold in the presence of itraconazole or ketoconazole in healthy
subjects.
o The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of fluconazole, clarithromycin and
erythromycin (moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple
dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). Model predicted nirogacestat
geometric mean AUC ratio was around 3‐fold in the presence of fluconazole, clarithromycin
or erythromycin in healthy subjects.
o The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of cimetidine (a weak CYP3A4
inhibitor) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat
(100 mg or 150 mg BID). Model predicted nirogacestat geometric mean AUC ratio was
about 1.4‐fold in the presence of cimetidine in healthy subjects.
o The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of rifampicin (a strong CYP3A4
inducer) and efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) on the PK of nirogacestat following
multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). The model predicted
nirogacestat exposure decreased about 85% and 67% when coadministered with rifampicin
and efavirenz, respectively, in healthy subjects.
o The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of
midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate) following multiple dose administration of
nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). The model predicted midazolam exposure increased
about 100% when coadministered with nirogacestat in healthy subjects.
o The nirogacestat PBPK model is adequate to predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of
rosiglitazone (a CYP2C8 substrate) and warfarin (a CYP2C9 substrate) following multiple
dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg, BID). Model predicted changes in
the exposure of rosiglitazone and warfarin were not considered clinically significant when
coadministered with multiple doses of nirogacestat in healthy subjects.
o In vitro study and PBPK modeling efforts suggest that the DDI potential of nirogacestat (100
mg or 150 mg BID) with mephenytoin and omeprazole (CYP2C19 substrates) cannot be
excluded.

1
Co‐administration of Itraconazole and nirogacestat resulted in increases in plasma systemic exposure of
nirogacestat by approximately 8.2‐fold and 2.5‐fold for AUCinf and Cmax, respectively; 2Co‐administration of
nirogacestat 95 mg QD and midazolam 2 mg resulted in increases in plasma systemic exposure of midazolam by
approximately 58.9% and 30% for AUCinf and Cmax, respectively.

PBPK software
Simcyp V19 (Simcyp Ltd, UK) was used by the Applicant to develop the PBPK models and assess
the DDI liability. The reviewer used the Simcyp V21 for analyses. The simulation results for the
PK profiles of nirogacestat and DDI assessment using Simcyp V21 were not significant difference
when compared to those generated using Simcyp V19. As a result, the Applicant’s simulation
results were included in the review.
Model development
PBPK model parameters are listed in Appendix.
The nirogacestat model consisted of a first order absorption model and a whole‐body PBPK
model. The absorption rate constant (ka) was estimated to be 2.899 1/h based on the
permeability data in MDCK cells. The lag time (Tlag) of 0.40 hr was assumed based on gastric
emptying time under fasted state.
The fu,gut (unbound fraction in enterocytes) was set to be 1.0. The Peff,man (permeability in man)
was predicted to be 6.64X10‐4 cm/s based on the permeability data in MDCK cells.
The whole‐body full PBPK model was used to simulate nirogacestat distribution. The volume of
distribution (Vss, 3.55 L/kg) was predicted using Method 2 in Simcyp and subsequently
optimized based on the observed nirogacestat PK in healthy subjects using a global Kp scalar of
0.29. The plasma protein binding (fu) is about 0.4%.
Nirogacestat clearance was estimated to be 13.4 L/h after intravenous administration of
nirogacestat in Study NIR‐DT‐102. CYP3A4 is the primary enzyme responsible for the
metabolism of nirogacestat. The fmCYP3A4 value of 0.857 was estimated based on HLM
chemical inhibition data. The rest of the metabolism was assigned as the additional human liver

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

microsome clearance (14.3%). Renal elimination was not considered in the model because the
majority of nirogacestat clearance was attributed to the metabolic elimination, with minimal
drug excreted unchanged in urine.
The induction parameter (Indmax and IndC50) values of CYP3A4, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and
(b) (4)
CYP2C19
. The KI
(8.46 μM) value for CYP3A4 time‐dependent inhibition was optimized based on data in clinical
study following multiple dosing of 95 mg QD nirogacestat (study A8641002), and Kinact (4.57
1/h) value for CYP3A4 time‐dependent inhibition was obtained from in vitro study (Study
103822). The CYP3A4 competition inhibition parameter value (Ki=0.04 mM) applied in the
model was refined based on the DDI study results with midazolam (Study A8641002) by the
reviewer.
Victim and perpetrator drug models
The default PBPK models of itraconazole, ketoconazole, fluconazole, clarithromycin,
erythromycin, cimetidine, rifampicin efavirenz, midazolam, bupropion, rosiglitazone, warfarin,
mephenytoin and omeprazole in SimCYP V19 were used without any modification for DDI
prediction. Default healthy volunteer population and cancer population in SimCYP V19 were
used in simulations.
PBPK model application
The developed PBPK model was used to simulate the DDI for nirogacestat in the following
scenarios.
o To predict the effects of itraconazole and ketoconazole (strong CYP3A inhibitors) and
fluconazole and clarithromycin (moderate CYP3A inhibitors) and cimetidine (a weak
CYP3A4 inhibition) on the PK of nirogacestat.
o To predict the effect of rifampicin (a strong CYP3A inducer) and efavirenz (a moderate
CYP3A inducer) on the PK of nirogacestat.
o To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A4
substrate).
o To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of bupropion (a CYP2B6 substrate).
o To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of rosiglitazone (a CYP2C8 substrate) and
warfarin (a substrate of CYP2C9.
o To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of mephenytoin and omeprazole (CYP2C19
substrates).
Results
1. Can nirogacestat PBPK model describe nirogacestat PK in healthy subjects?
Yes. Table 79 showed the Applicant’s model verification results. The PK profiles after multiple
oral doses of 95 mg QD nirogacestat in healthy subjects was used to verify the net auto DDI
336
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

effect (TDI and induction on CYP3A4) on the PK of nirogacestat. The in vitro obtained parameter
values for mechanism‐based inhibition and induction on CYP3A4 were included in the model.
The in vitro KI value (reduced by 5‐fold) was optimized to capture the nirogacestat PK profile on
day 1 and day 14 after multiple oral doses of 95 mg QD nirogacestat in healthy subjects. In
general, the model was able to capture the nirogacestat PK profiles following a single or
multiple dose administration (Figure 30). The simulated geometric mean AUC and Cmax values
for nirogacestat were within 0.7‐1.3‐fold of the observed values after a single or multiple dose
administration in healthy subjects or cancer patients, except for the dose groups where the
number of subjects per dose level is less than 8 (Table 79). It was noted that the PK parameters
for nirogacestat tended to have substantial inter‐subject variability since most geometric CV
values are greater than 30% and even approach 100% in some studies. Therefore, considering
the limited number of subjects in some dose groups and the significant variability, the
Applicant’s model is deemed to be acceptable for the purpose of DDI potential evaluation as a
victim. The uncertainty associated with the DDI potential evaluation as a perpetrator will be
further assessed using sensitivity analyses.

Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

2. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict its effect on midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate)
PK following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg BID)?
Yes. The nirogacestat mediated combined induction and inhibition effects on CYP3A4 (net
inhibition effect) was verified based on the clinical DDI study with midazolam following multiple
dose administration of nirogacestat (95 mg QD) (Table 80). The verified model was then used to
predict the effect of nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) on the PK of midazolam. The
model predicted about 84% and 107% increase in the exposure of midazolam after
coadministration of 100 mg BID and 150 mg BID nirogacestat, respectively, in healthy subjects
(Table 80).
3. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of itraconazole and ketoconazole (strong
CYP3A inhibitors) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of
nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
Yes. The contribution of CYP3A4 to the overall clearance of nirogacestat was estimated based
on invitro data and verified based on the clinical DDI study data of single dose nirogacestat with
itraconazole (Table 80). The verified model was then used to estimate the effect of strong
CYPP3A4 inhibitors on the PK of multiple dose nirogacestat. The model predicted nirogacestat
(100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) exposure increased 7.02, and 6.33‐fold and 5.56 and 5.19‐fold
when coadministered with itraconazole and ketoconazole, respectively, in healthy subjects
(Table 80).
4. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of fluconazole, clarithromycin and
erythromycin (moderate CYP3A inhibitors) and cimetidine (a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor) on the
PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg
BID) in healthy subjects?
Yes. The contribution of CYP3A4 to the overall clearance of nirogacestat was estimated based
on invitro data and verified based on the clinical DDI study data of single dose nirogacestat with
itraconazole (Table 80). The verified model was then used to estimate the effect of moderate or
weak CYPP3A4 inhibitors on the PK of nirogacestat. The model predicted nirogacestat (100 mg
BID and 150 mg BID) exposure increased about 3.26 and 3.18‐fold, 3.83 and 3.46‐fold, 3.00 and
2.73‐fold, and 1.40 and 1.40‐fold when coadministered with fluconazole, clarithromycin,
erythromycin and cimetidine, respectively, in healthy subject.
5. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of rifampicin (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) and
efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose
administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
Yes. The contribution of CYP3A4 to the overall clearance of nirogacestat was estimated based
on invitro data and verified based on the clinical DDI study data of single dose nirogacestat with
itraconazole (Table 80). The verified model was then used to estimate the effect of CYPP3A4
340
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5283130

NDA/IND Multi‐disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
Nirogacestat (PF‐03084014)

inducers on the PK of nirogacestat. The model predicted nirogacestat exposure decreased
about 85% and 67% when coadministered with rifampicin and efavirenz, respectively, in
healthy subjects (Table 80).
6. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of bupropion (a
CYP2B6 substrate) following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg BID and
150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
No. The bupropion substrate models have not been adequately validated; therefore, it cannot
be used to evaluate the potential effect of nirogacestat on the PK of CYP2B6 substrate.
7. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of rosiglitazone (a
CYP2C8 substrate) and warfarin (a substrate of CYP2C9) following multiple dose
administration of nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
Yes. In vitro studies indicated that nirogacestat is a CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inducer with an in vitro
determined Indmax and IndC50 values of 11.14 and 2.38 μM for CYP2C8 induction and 12.33
and 4.96 μm for CYP2C9 induction, respectively. The model predicted rosiglitazone and
warfarin exposures were not significantly affected by concomitant use of nirogacestat based on
in vitro determined enzyme induction parameter values (AUCR~1.00 for both rosiglitazone and
warfarin. The Applicant further conducted a sensitivity analysis of nirogacestat Indmax values to
assess the effect on the predicted rosiglitazone and warfarin exposure. Due to the uncertainties
regarding the in vitro to in vivo extrapolation of the induction parameter, reviewer also
conducted additional sensitivity analysis of nirogacestat IndC50 values. The simulated
rosiglitazone and warfarin AUC ratios were around 1.0 with 10‐fold lower Ind50 values or 10‐
fold higher Indmax values than the in vitro determined when coadministered with 100 mg BID
or 150 mg BID nirogacestat. These results indicated that the induction potential of nirogacestat
is low towards CYP2C8 and CYP2C9.
8. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of mephenytoin and
omeprazole (CYP2C19 substrates) following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100
mg BID and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
(b) (4)

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

nirogacestat as a perpetrator with CYP2C19 substrates cannot be excluded.
